Preface
A mathematical object can appear in several forms: as local data, as a sequence of finite observations, as
a family of coordinates, or as the output of an approximate calculation. These descriptions may agree,
fail to agree, or agree only after a correction. This monograph studies the conditions under which they
describe one coherent realization.
The starting point is a declared system of views and maps between them. Its coherent families form
a limit. Prescribed data then define an extension problem, whose obstruction records whether a com-
pletion exists and whose ambiguity records the freedom in choosing it. Resolution towers make these
questions sensitive to scale. Groupoids retain the symmetries of a realization, while descent asks whether
compatible local realizations assemble. In normed and Hilbert models, the same questions acquire
quantitative forms: how much correction is needed, which information can be preserved, and whether
small residuals certify proximity to an exact answer.
These constructions give a common language for realization, obstruction, and repair. Their connections
are made through explicit maps and estimates. Existence, uniqueness, and stability remain separate
assertions. A change of representation must identify both the transformed object and the data it preserves.
The underlying limit constructions, gauge groupoids, stack definitions, orthogonal projections, and
bounded inverse principles are part of established mathematics. The contribution of this presentation
is their integration with specified view systems, protected correction spaces, resolution differentials,
graph transports, and worked examples. The comparison maps and hypotheses carry the substance of
that integration. Formal verification establishes the recorded declarations, without making a claim of
historical novelty for each ingredient.
The word space does not assert one universal topological object containing all of mathematics. The
strict construction is a set of coherent families. Symmetry is retained in a groupoid, and effective
descent is proved for a specified site. Analytic estimates require a declared normed or Hilbert
model. Each layer has its own hypotheses and its own notion of realization.
The book presents proofs together with examples that show why their hypotheses matter. Its companion
visual guide emphasizes geometry and explicit calculations. Its Lean guide explains the formal defi-
nitions, interfaces, and evidence. Mathematical figures are reproducible vector diagrams or plots of
explicit formulas.

Reading guide
Part I constructs coherent sections and follows their obstructions as more data are prescribed. Part II
develops resolution towers, finite observations, split repair coordinates, and multiscale reconstruction.
Part III retains symmetries, proves effective descent, and distinguishes coherent phases from removable
ones. Part IV develops analytic stability and a geometry of transformation costs. Part V follows
approximate answers to their limits, separates a coherent point from its intended Hilbert realization,
and derives budget, regularization, and stopping criteria. Part VI works through finite loop models and
returns to mathematical semantics.
A short technical route is Sections 1, 3, 13, 18, and 21. For packets and residuals, include Sections 8
and 10. The graph models in Section 17 show how a categorical realization problem acquires a nu-
merical condition number. Sections 7 and 9 explain how the constructions behave under changes of
representation.
For concrete motivations, begin with Sections 33, 34, and 26. The last connects finite realization, closure,
and failure of protected infinite existence in one calculation. Sections 27 through 30 develop its positive
converse and regularized solutions. Sections 31 and 32 explain how to certify an inexact calculation and
select its tolerance.
Appendix A contains fifteen solved problems. Appendix B collects the assumptions behind the principal
results. Appendix C supplies notation and a map into the formal source. Appendix D describes formal
coverage, and Appendix E proves the analytic background used in the text.
Proofs and formal scope
The accompanying Lean development covers the foundational constructions and all 16 principal theorem
groups on bounded realization, budgets, regularization, and certificates. This includes the actual infinite
diagonal family and its tail bounds, the sharp asymptotic over all real budgets, the rounded noisy and
inexact rates, and the concrete nonclosed-range example. Appendix D identifies the formal counterparts
and their scope.
A formal counterpart can establish a theorem by a different route from its printed proof. In particular, the
bounded-realization result is formalized through a compactness argument without assigning a separate
formal counterpart to the printed weak-subsequence lemma. Explanatory prose, background proofs,
and numerical illustrations are not all individually machine checked.
The complete source has passed a fresh integrated compilation, declaration audit, and diagnostic-control
replay. The earlier records remain available as historical evidence. Lean’s compiler and kernel, its
stated standard axioms, and the pinned external dependency cache remain part of the trust boundary.
The cache is recorded but was not rebuilt by this audit. Appendix D gives the exact counts and their
interpretation.

Part I
Realizations and their obstructions
A global object is not added to local data by terminology. It is a family satisfying every
prescribed transition equation. The first task is to construct the space of such families
and to keep its possible emptiness visible.
1 Views and coherent realizations
1.1 The data of a view system
Fix a small category V, or work in fixed universes so that the products below exist in the chosen ambient
universe. A view is an object of V. It can encode a resolution level, a coordinate presentation, or a context
in which part of an object is visible. These interpretations are mathematical choices, not additional
axioms.
Definition 1.1 (View system). A view system consists of sets Fv and maps
f ∗: Fw −→Fv
( f : v −→w)
satisfying (1v)∗= id and (g ◦f )∗= f ∗g∗. Equivalently, it is a functor F : Vop →Set.
The direction matters. An arrow v →w induces a restriction from the fiber at w to the fiber at v. A
restriction need not be invertible. A category with several arrows between two views can retain different
routes between their descriptions. Replacing it by a preorder can lose those routes and the constraints
they impose.
Definition 1.2 (Strict Komorebi realization). The ambient realization set is
K(F) = Γ(F) =
(
x ∈∏
v∈Ob V
Fv : f ∗(xw) = xv for every f : v →w
)
.
(1.1)
A point of K(F) is a whole coherent family. Its value in view v is the evaluation evv(x) = xv.
The raw product contains all families, including inconsistent ones. Equation (1.1) cuts out the exact
families. No nonemptiness hypothesis is built into the definition.
Theorem 1.3 (Joint determination and the universal property). The evaluations jointly determine a realization.
If a set T carries maps cv : T →Fv satisfying f ∗cw = cv, there is a unique map c : T →Γ(F) satisfying evv c = cv
for every view.
Proof. We first verify that the evaluation maps themselves form a compatible family. If x ∈Γ(F) and
f : v →w, the defining equation for x gives
f ∗(evw x) = f ∗(xw) = xv = evv x.
If x, y ∈Γ(F) have equal evaluations, then xv = yv for every v. Equality of functions into a dependent
product gives equality of their underlying families. Membership in Γ(F) is a property of a family, not a
further choice of coordinate. Thus x = y.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
1

Now fix the compatible maps cv : T →Fv. For each t ∈T define a family by x(t)v = cv(t). For every
arrow,
f ∗x(t)w = f ∗cw(t) = cv(t) = x(t)v.
This calculation verifies membership in Γ(F), so the rule c(t) = x(t) defines an actual function c : T →
Γ(F). Evaluating it gives evv c(t) = cv(t), as required.
To prove uniqueness, let b : T →Γ(F) be another such function. At every t and v,
(b(t))v = evv b(t) = cv(t) = evv c(t).
Joint determination first yields b(t) = c(t), and function extensionality then yields b = c. Nothing in this
argument assumes that T or Γ(F) is inhabited. For example, when T is empty there is the unique empty
function. When T is inhabited, existence of the compatible family (cv) is already substantive data.
Thus the strict construction is precisely the ordinary limit of the view diagram, with the usual limit
interpretation in the sequential case [1]. This identifies its mathematical content and its universal property.
It does not supply a universal view diagram for every possible mathematical question.

the transition equations.
1.2 Maps of realizations
Let α : F →G consist of maps αv : Fv →Gv with
αv f ∗
F = f ∗
Gαw.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
2


Komorebi Space
Monograph
The induced map on realizations is Γ(α)(x)v = αv(xv). The naturality square is exactly what is needed
to show that the image family is coherent.
Proposition 1.4 (Functorial transport). These induced maps preserve identities and composition:
Γ(idF) = idΓ(F),
Γ(βα) = Γ(β)Γ(α).
Proof. Evaluate each side at an arbitrary view and apply the corresponding identity for the coordinate
maps. Theorem 1.3 then identifies the maps on the whole realization set.
An arbitrary collection of fiber maps need not define a map on realizations. Its compatibility equations
are part of the theorem, not a convention that can be omitted after a notation has been chosen.
1.3 A global view and a useful warning
Proposition 1.5 (Terminal evaluation). If t is terminal in V, evaluation induces an equivalence Γ(F) ≃Ft.
Proof. For a ∈Ft, take its restriction along the unique arrow v →t at each view. Uniqueness of the
arrow to t, together with the functor laws, proves coherence. Evaluation at t recovers a. Conversely, any
coherent family is the restriction of its coordinate at t.
This proposition is both useful and diagnostic. If the supposedly reconstructed global object has already
been placed in a terminal fiber, the limit recovers that fiber. The proposition does not prove that arbitrary
partial data have a preimage there.
The category of elements R
V F and Γ(F) also have different roles. The former organizes individual
appearances and their transitions. The latter selects coherent families across all views. The category of
elements serves here as explanatory notation, while the Lean formalization directly represents families
and their coherence.
2 Partial data, gluing, and correspondence fibers
2.1 Prescribed views
For U ⊆Ob V, use the full subcategory on U. A partial section s ∈ΓU(F) satisfies all transition equations
whose endpoints lie in U. Define
ExtU(s) = {x ∈Γ(F) : x|U = s}.
(2.1)
This fiber can be empty, a singleton, or much larger. A partial section is already coherent on its prescribed
domain. A raw assignment that fails an internal transition equation is not a partial section of this kind.
Let (Ui)i∈I be full patches. A matching family consists of si ∈ΓUi(F) with equal values on every overlap.
The relevant covering condition is
for every f : v →w,
some Ui contains both v and w.
(2.2)
Identities imply that this condition also covers every object.
Theorem 2.1 (Strict gluing). Under (2.2), restriction induces an equivalence
Γ(F) ≃Match(F, (Ui)).
Every fixed matching family has a unique global glue.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
3


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Proof. Step 1: every coordinate can be selected. Apply the cover condition to the identity arrow of v. It gives
an index containing v. Choose one such index i(v) and put
xv = (si(v))v.
The choice is an existence construction. It is not an algorithm selecting patches in a preferred order. If
v ∈Uj, matching on the overlap Ui(v) ∩Uj gives
xv = (sj)v.
(*)
In particular the value is independent of the selected index.
Step 2: the selected coordinates satisfy every arrow equation. For f : v →w, choose a single index j with
v, w ∈Uj. Formula (*) applies to both endpoints. Since the patch is full, it contains this actual arrow,
even when other arrows have the same endpoints. Coherence of sj gives
f ∗xw = f ∗(sj)w = (sj)v = xv.
Thus x ∈Γ(F). Object coverage alone would not supply the common patch used in this step.
Step 3: assembly and restriction are inverse. Formula (*) proves x|Uj = sj for every j. Conversely, start with
y ∈Γ(F) and assemble its restrictions. The resulting coordinate at v is yv, regardless of which containing
patch is chosen. Joint determination gives the original y. These two identities are exactly the inverse
identities for the displayed equivalence.
For uniqueness with a fixed matching family, any other glue has coordinate (si(v))v = xv at every v,
hence equals x. If the view category is empty, its product has one empty family and the same conclusion
follows without making any choices.
The proof explains why object coverage alone is insufficient. Coherence is an equation attached to an
arrow, so existence of a glue must see that equation somewhere in the local data.
Example 2.2 (An object cover with no glue). Take two views 0 →1, Boolean fibers, and the identity
restriction. The singleton patches cover all objects. A local value false at 0 and true at 1 agrees on the
empty overlap, yet no global section realizes both values. A global family would have to assign the same
Boolean to both views.
Object coverage does prove uniqueness of a possible glue. It does not prove its existence. This distinction
persists when sets are replaced by objects with symmetries.
2.2 Correspondences must also be coherent
Suppose natural maps form a span F
p←−M
q−→G, and fix a ∈Γ(F), b ∈Γ(G). At each view define the
ordinary fiber
Ba,b(v) = {m ∈Mv : pv(m) = av, qv(m) = bv}.
Naturality and coherence provide its restrictions. A global bridge is an element of
B(a, b) = {z ∈Γ(M) : Γ(p)z = a, Γ(q)z = b}.
Theorem 2.3 (Fiber and section compatibility). There is a coordinate preserving equivalence
Γ(Ba,b) ≃B(a, b).
Under a transition cover, matching local bridge sections glue to a unique global bridge with both prescribed
projections unchanged.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
4


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Proof. We must first check that the displayed fibers really form a view system. Let m ∈Ba,b(w) and
f : v →w. Naturality of p and coherence of a give
pv( f ∗
Mm) = f ∗
F pw(m) = f ∗
Faw = av.
The same calculation with q, b gives qv( f ∗
Mm) = bv. Therefore f ∗
Mm belongs to Ba,b(v). Identity and
composition of these restricted maps are inherited from M, because equality in a subset is equality of its
underlying elements.
Given u ∈Γ(Ba,b), forget each fiber-membership condition and call the underlying family zv ∈Mv.
Coherence of u is precisely coherence of z, so z ∈Γ(M). The equations pvzv = av hold at every coordinate.
Joint determination in Γ(F) yields Γ(p)z = a, and the equations for q similarly yield Γ(q)z = b. Thus z is
a global bridge.
In the opposite direction, let z ∈B(a, b). Evaluate its two global projection equations at v. They show
that zv belongs to Ba,b(v), and its original coherence shows that these elements form a section of the
fiber system. Forgetting and reattaching membership conditions preserves every coordinate, so the two
constructions are inverse.
Finally apply strict gluing to this specific fiber system. The glued section retains its membership in every
Ba,b(v), which is why both prescribed projections survive gluing. Uniqueness is for the fixed matching
bridge family. Different matching families for the same (a, b) can still yield different global bridges.
These are ordinary fibers. They do not retain comparison isomorphisms or automorphisms. Section 14
constructs the appropriate groupoid version when this information matters.
2.3 Locally inhabited does not mean globally inhabited
Example 2.4 (The loop with no coherent choice). Let the view category have one object and automorphism
group C2. Let its nonidentity arrow act on a set with two elements by swapping the elements. The fiber
is nonempty and every transport is bijective. Nevertheless a global section would be a fixed point of the
swap, and there is none.
The problem is not missing local elements or noninvertible transition maps. It is the absence of an
element satisfying the loop equation. Inserting a point in each fiber does not certify a coherent selection
of those points.
The strict theory already distinguishes three levels: possible local appearances, compatible partial
descriptions, and an actual global realization. A correspondence must pass through the same levels.
Local nonemptiness of its fibers is not a globalization theorem.

3 The additive obstruction and the space of extensions
The strict definition gives a system of equations. In an additive setting, that system has an actual
differential, and the failure to extend prescribed data has a quotient valued obstruction.
3.1 The compatibility differential
Assume each fiber Av is an abelian group and each restriction is additive. Set
E = ∏
v
Av,
R = ∏
f :v→w
Av,
(δx) f = f ∗(xw) −xv.
The group R retains an entry for every arrow. It is not silently identified with a cohomology group.
Proposition 3.1 (Differential identities). The map δ : E →R is additive, Γ(A) = ker δ, and
(δx)1v = 0,
(δx)g◦f = f ∗((δx)g) + (δx) f .
Proof. Additivity follows coordinate by coordinate. Vanishing is exactly the defining coherence equation.
For composition, add and subtract f ∗xw:
f ∗g∗xz −xv = f ∗(g∗xz −xw) + ( f ∗xw −xv).
The identity equation uses (1v)∗= id.
This exact formula is more informative than a scalar error score. It specifies how defects transform and
combine. Norms can be added later, but they do not replace the typed residual.
3.2 The admissible corrections
Fix U ⊆Ob V. Write pU : E →∏v∈U Av for raw restriction. A correction preserving the prescribed
values belongs to ker pU. Its possible defects form the subgroup
BU = δ(ker pU) ≤R.
For a partial section s, choose any raw extension es, for example zero off U, and define
QU = R/BU,
oU(s) = [δes] ∈QU.
(3.1)
If another raw extension is chosen, their difference vanishes on U, so its differential belongs to BU. Thus
the class is independent of the lift.
Theorem 3.2 (Extension criterion). For every coherent partial section,
oU(s) = 0
⇐⇒
ExtU(s) ̸= ∅.
Proof. Let πU : R →R/BU be the quotient homomorphism. By the definition of a quotient by a
subgroup,
πU(r) = 0
⇐⇒
r ∈BU.
Here BU is the image of ker pU under δ. Consequently membership of δes means the existence of a specific
h ∈E satisfying both pUh = 0 and δh = δes. The subgroup need not be closed in any topology. No
topology has been introduced.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
6


Komorebi Space
Monograph
If such h exists, set x = es −h. Additivity gives the two equations separately:
δx = δes −δh = 0,
pUx = pUes −pUh = s.
The first equation puts x in Γ(A), and the second puts that coherent family in ExtU(s).
Conversely, take x ∈ExtU(s) and set h = es −x. Since both raw families restrict to s, one has pUh = 0.
Since δx = 0, one has δh = δes. This witnesses δes ∈BU, hence vanishing of its quotient class.
The proof is independent of the selected raw lift. Indeed, if es′ is another lift, k = es′ −es lies in ker pU, and
δes′ −δes = δk ∈BU.
Thus the two quotient classes agree before either feasibility direction is invoked. A raw lift always exists
in this additive model by using zero outside U. Constructing a raw lift does not solve the coherence
equations.
The quotient is constructed before the solvability test. The theorem therefore does not define obstruction
circularly by saying that it vanishes when a solution exists. It also does not give a decision procedure for
membership in an arbitrary subgroup of an infinite product.

3.3 Existence is not uniqueness
Define the ambiguity group
HU = ker δ ∩ker pU.
It consists of coherent changes that are invisible on the prescribed domain.
Theorem 3.3 (Classification of a nonempty extension fiber). If x0 ∈ExtU(s), translation gives an equivalence
HU ≃ExtU(s),
h 7−→x0 + h.
Consequently the extension is unique exactly when oU(s) = 0 and HU = 0.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
7


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Proof. Define Θ : HU →ExtU(s) by Θ(h) = x0 + h. It lands in the extension set because
δ(x0 + h) = δx0 + δh = 0,
pU(x0 + h) = s + 0 = s.
Define Ψ : ExtU(s) →HU by Ψ(x) = x −x0. Its differential is zero, and its restriction is s −s = 0, so the
claimed target is correct. The formulas give
ΨΘ(h) = x0 + h −x0 = h,
ΘΨ(x) = x0 + (x −x0) = x.
This proves the equivalence rather than only a comparison of cardinalities.
If HU = {0}, the parameter of every extension is zero, so every extension equals x0. Conversely, if x0 is
the only extension, then for any h ∈HU the equality x0 + h = x0 forces h = 0 by cancellation. Combining
this observation with the existence criterion proves the stated necessary and sufficient condition for a
unique extension.
The choice of x0 only supplies an origin for the parameters. If x1 = x0 + h1 is used instead, the parameter
of x changes from x −x0 to x −x1 = (x −x0) −h1. The affine set of answers itself has not changed.
An extension fiber is therefore an affine copy, or torsor, of the invisible coherent group. Selecting one
solution gives coordinates on the fiber. Without a selected solution, the affine structure does not designate
an origin.
Example 3.4 (Parity and an independent ambiguity). For two views with f ∗(m) = 2m on Z, a prescribed
value k at the coarse view extends exactly when k is even. The extension is (k, k/2) and is unique. In
particular 1 is obstructed and 2 extends nontrivially. The parity statement is an instance of Theorem 3.2.
If a separate, disconnected integer view is added and left unprescribed, every extendable seed has
infinitely many completions. This last variant is a direct application of Theorem 3.3, not a claim that the
parity obstruction has changed.
3.4 The exact sequence behind the criterion
The additive structures make restriction a homomorphism rU : Γ(A) →ΓU(A). Using the zero extension,
or lift independence, oU is also a homomorphism.
Proposition 3.5 (Exactness at partial sections). The sequence
Γ(A)
rU
−→ΓU(A)
oU
−→QU
is exact at ΓU(A). Thus ker oU = im rU, and restriction is onto precisely when oU is the zero map.
Proof. First verify that oU is a homomorphism, since exactness is being asserted in an additive setting.
Given s, t ∈ΓU(A), choose raw lifts es,et. Their sum lifts s + t. Lift independence and additivity imply
oU(s + t) = [δ(es + et)] = [δes] + [δet] = oU(s) + oU(t).
The zero lift gives oU(0) = 0. Restriction rU is a homomorphism because addition of global sections is
coordinatewise.
If s = rUx for a global section x, use x itself as a raw lift. Its differential vanishes, so oU(s) = 0. This
proves im rU ⊆ker oU. If s ∈ker oU, the extension criterion constructs a global x restricting to s, proving
the reverse inclusion.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
8


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Finally, rU is surjective exactly when its image is all of ΓU(A). By the equality just proved, this is
equivalent to every partial section having zero obstruction, which says that oU is the zero homomorphism.
Nothing here claims that its image is all of QU.
No surjectivity onto the entire residual quotient is asserted. The residual group contains many entries
that need not arise as the differential of a partial lift.
3.5 Extension in stages
For U ⊆W ⊆Ob V, an extension first to W and then globally must retain the intermediate choice. There
is an explicit equivalence
ExtU(s) ≃
⨿
t∈ΓW(A)
t|U=s
ExtW(t).
(3.2)
It sends a global extension to its restriction to W, together with itself as an extension of that restriction.
The inverse forgets the intermediate coordinate. These operations are inverse because the required
intermediate coordinate of a global section is its actual restriction. A coherent intermediate choice can
still be obstructed, even when a different intermediate choice completes. Equation (3.2) is why extension
by stages is a dependent sum rather than a statement that every branch succeeds.
4 Protected correction as a general principle
The additive extension theorem is a particular instance of a useful general construction. Let E, R, S be
abelian groups, with additive maps d : E →R and p : E →S. The residual d measures compatibility,
and p specifies the information that repair must preserve.
Theorem 4.1 (Semantics preserving correction). For x ∈E, the following conditions are equivalent:
[dx] = 0 in R/d(ker p),
∃h ∈E : p(h) = 0,
d(h) = d(x),
∃y ∈E : d(y) = 0,
p(y) = p(x).
The class of dx is unchanged when an admissible correction is subtracted from x.
Proof. The image d(ker p) is a subgroup of R. The defining property of the quotient says
[dx] = 0
⇐⇒
dx ∈d(ker p)
⇐⇒
∃h ∈E : ph = 0, dh = dx.
This is the first equivalence with its witness made explicit.
For the second, a permitted h gives y = x −h. Then
dy = dx −dh = 0,
py = px −ph = px.
Conversely an exact y with py = px gives h = x −y, which satisfies ph = 0 and dh = dx. The two
witness transformations are inverse: subtracting h from x and then subtracting that answer from x
recovers h, and conversely.
To verify invariance, fix any h with ph = 0. Then d(x −h) = dx −dh, and dh lies in the subgroup divided
out. Therefore [d(x −h)] = [dx]. This does not assert that the residual itself is unchanged. It asserts that
permitted changes can alter the residual only within its already correctable coset.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
9


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Here semantics means the declared mathematical observable p. It is not an assertion that one homomor-
phism captures every meaning of every object. In a tower, p might preserve a seed operator and every
new output component. In a graph, it might preserve designated vertex values. Different choices give
different problems.
Proposition 4.2 (A certificate of impossibility). Let L : R →D be additive. If L vanishes on d(ker p) but
L(dx) ̸= 0, no exact y can preserve p(x).
Proof. Suppose, for contradiction, that dy = 0 and py = px. Then h = x −y satisfies ph = 0 and dh = dx.
By the annihilation hypothesis,
L(dx) = L(dh) = 0,
contrary to the assumed nonzero value. This already proves impossibility directly.
Equivalently, if r −r′ ∈d(ker p), then L(r −r′) = 0, so L(r) = L(r′). Hence L([r]) = L(r) is a
well-defined homomorphism on R/d(ker p). Its value on [dx] is nonzero. This supplies the quotient
interpretation of the same contradiction, without assuming that every nonzero quotient class can be
detected by a map into one fixed preferred coefficient group.
This is often the most economical negative proof. One need not classify every possible correction. It is
enough to find an invariant annihilating all admissible corrections but not the observed defect.
4.1 Why unrestricted cancellation is empty information
If every correction is permitted, taking h = x leaves the exact candidate zero. Thus ∃h : d(x −h) = 0 is
automatic. It says nothing about recovering an object of interest. A repair theorem becomes informative
only when it preserves something, is quantitatively controlled, or satisfies a comparison property that
excludes arbitrary replacement.
Example 4.3 (A rigid obstruction). Take E = R = S = Z, d = p = id, and x = 1. The only admissible
correction is zero, while the residual is nonzero. Hence there is no exact answer preserving the observed
value. This is not a failure of the formalism. It is the correct certificate for an incompatible demand.
4.2 What an obstruction does not yet provide
An algebraic quotient does not automatically carry a useful norm. A zero infimum of distances to its
boundary subgroup does not imply membership unless closure is controlled. Nor does vanishing supply
a preferred correction when ker d ∩ker p is nontrivial. These two issues lead to different parts of the
book: ambiguity is treated algebraically and through groupoids, while closed range and condition
numbers enter the analytic theory.
A repair problem is not determined by its defect alone. It is determined by the defect and the
admissible changes. Keeping the pair (d, p) explicit prevents a successful cancellation from being
mistaken for a successful reconstruction.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
10


Komorebi Space
Monograph
5 Changing what is prescribed
The observable is part of a realization problem, not an annotation added after it has been solved.
Prescribing more views can remove ambiguity, but it can also exclude every completion. This section
follows both effects through the actual obstruction groups. It then works through a three-view model in
which an initially harmless choice creates a genuine dead end.
5.1 Three maps that must not be confused
Keep the additive view system of Section 3. For a prescribed domain U, there are three distinct maps:
pU : E −→∏
u∈U
Au,
rU : Γ(A) −→ΓU(A),
oU : ΓU(A) −→QU.
The first restricts a raw family, whether or not it is coherent. The second restricts a global coherent family.
The third detects whether an already coherent partial family has a global completion. Their domains are
different. In particular, surjectivity of the raw coordinate projection does not imply surjectivity of rU.
The obstruction group is QU = R/BU, where BU = δ(ker pU). The ambiguity group is HU = ker δ ∩
ker pU. Both depend on what has been fixed. Enlarging the prescription reduces the permitted correction
space and the invisible exact space at the same time.
5.2 Forgetting a prescription gives a quotient map
Let U ⊆W. Then
ker pW ⊆ker pU,
BW ⊆BU,
HW ⊆HU.
A correction invisible on all of W is certainly invisible on U. The direction of the quotient map is
consequently
πW,U : QW −→QU,
[r]BW 7−→[r]BU.
The larger prescribed domain appears in the source. More protection means a smaller subgroup is
divided out, so more obstruction information can remain.
Theorem 5.1 (Functorial forgetting of protected views). The homomorphism πW,U is well defined and
surjective. For U ⊆W ⊆Z, it satisfies
πU,U = I,
πW,UπZ,W = πZ,U.
For every s ∈ΓW(A),
πW,UoW(s) = oU(s|U).
(5.1)
In particular an obstructed restriction remains obstructed when more compatible prescribed data are retained. A
successful extension on the larger prescribed domain gives a successful extension of its restriction.
Proof. If r −r′ ∈BW, then r −r′ ∈BU, proving well-definedness. Every class modulo BU has a
representative r, and the class of that same representative modulo BW maps to it. This proves surjectivity.
Identity and composition follow by applying the formulas to a representative.
Choose a raw extension es of the partial section on W. It is also a raw extension of s|U. Lift independence
of the two extension obstructions therefore identifies both sides of (5.1) with the class of δes modulo BU.
A homomorphism sends zero to zero. Thus nonvanishing on the smaller domain rules out vanishing on
the larger one, while vanishing on the larger domain implies vanishing after restriction.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
11


Komorebi Space
Monograph
The converse implication does not hold. The quotient map can annihilate the obstruction created by a
newly fixed value. Surjectivity of πW,U is also not a theorem that arbitrary larger partial sections extend.
It is a statement about representatives in two obstruction groups, not about the image of restriction from
global objects.
5.3 An initially free parameter can become an obstruction
Consider the chain of views 0 →1 →2, with fiber Z2 at each view. Write the maps in the direction in
which data are restricted:
ρ0,1(a, b) = (a, 0),
ρ1,2(a, b) = (a, 2b),
ρ0,2(a, b) = (a, 0).
The composite identity ρ0,1ρ1,2 = ρ0,2 is literal. A global family is exactly
x0 = (k, 0),
x1 = (k, 2n),
x2 = (k, n),
k, n ∈Z.
(5.2)
Indeed the first coordinate must agree at every view. The second coordinate at view zero is forced to
vanish, and the second coordinate at view one must be twice the second coordinate at view two. These
conditions also suffice.
First prescribe only U = {0}, with seed (k, 0). Every integer n in (5.2) gives a completion. Now prescribe
W = {0, 1}, with values (k, 0) and (k, m). This is a coherent partial section for every integer m, because
ρ0,1 forgets its second coordinate. Global extendability is a stronger requirement: m must be even.
Proposition 5.2 (A coherent branch can be a dead end). Every initial seed (k, 0) in this model has a completion
set in bijection with Z. A fixed intermediate value (k, m) extends precisely when m ∈2Z, and then has a unique
completion. In particular the nonzero initial seed (7, 0) extends, while the coherent intermediate choice (7, 1) does
not.
Proof. Equation (5.2) supplies a completion for each n, and the second coordinate of x2 recovers the
parameter. Fixing the intermediate value imposes 2n = m. This equation has no integer solution for odd
m, and exactly one for even m. Taking k = 7 and m = 1 proves the final assertion.
The invisible coherent changes for the initial prescription are
((0, 0), (0, 2n), (0, n)),
n ∈Z.
Thus HU ≃Z. Requiring invisibility also at view one forces 2n = 0, hence n = 0, so HW = 0. More
observation removes all ambiguity in the successful branches. It does not turn the unsuccessful branches
into solutions.
5.4 Why the extension space is a dependent sum
Let s be the initial partial section. The stage theorem gives
ExtU(s) ≃
⨿
t∈ΓW(A)
t|U=s
ExtW(t).
In the present example the index t is the integer m. The summand is empty for odd m and a singleton for
even m. Taking their disjoint union gives one integer’s worth of completions again. No contradiction
appears between many initial completions and unique completion after a successful intermediate choice.

This formula also explains a logical error in greedy reconstruction. The fact that some branch extends
does not justify replacing the existential choice by an arbitrary coherent branch. A method that fixes
an intermediate value must either retain the remaining extension obligation or supply a rule proved to
preserve extendability. Coherence on the current patch alone provides no such guarantee.
For an increasing sequence of prescribed domains, the same warning applies repeatedly. The finite stage
formula keeps each intermediate choice explicit. It does not by itself manufacture one compatible infinite
sequence of choices. Proposition 35.2 supplies an existence criterion for towers of nonempty finite sets.
5.5 Changing the coefficients changes both questions
The equations behind the example can be read over a general abelian coefficient group T. With the initial
first coordinate fixed, the possible completions are parametrized by n ∈T. Prescribing the middle value
m gives the equation 2n = m. Direct substitution therefore gives
feasible middle values = 2T,
ambiguity after prescribing the middle = ker(2 : T →T).

The integer instance is the actual formal example cited above. The other rows are worked coefficient
calculations from the same two equations. In characteristic two, stronger prescription does not eliminate
the hidden parameter because doubling annihilates it. Over the rationals it creates no parity obstruction
because doubling is invertible.

Monograph
A solver that changes the coefficient domain while leaving its user-facing description untouched changes
the mathematical problem. The rational answer 1/2 is not an integral completion of the odd middle
value one. Conversely, a failure over Z is not evidence of a failure over Q. The coefficient structure
belongs beside the views, restrictions, and preserved values in the problem specification.
More information can reduce the set of answers without making the problem easier. The obstruction
quotient records which new demands become impossible, while the ambiguity kernel records which
freedom survives the demands that can be met.

Part II
Resolution, repair, and retained detail
Resolution gives a concrete family of views. Its compatibility differential connects
exact maps, protected information, and residual coordinates. Recovering every finite
coordinate is then separated from convergence in a chosen norm.
6 Resolution towers and exact transport
6.1 The sequential model
A tower in a category C is a sequence
· · ·

For sets, a point is a sequence xn ∈Xn with ρn(xn+1) = xn. Composite restrictions turn the tower into a
view system on N, with an arrow i →j when i ≤j. Its section space is the same coherent point type,
with the same coordinates.
A candidate transport from X to Y consists of maps fn : Xn →Yn. It is exact when
ρY

(6.1)
Proposition 6.1 (The exact tower category). Identity maps and composites of exact transports are exact. An
exact transport induces a map of coherent point spaces by x 7→( fnxn)n, preserving identities and composition.

Identity, associativity, and composition on points are checked coordinatewise.
This is a category on a fixed schedule. A full procategory allows additional reindexing and equivalence
relations on maps. Those constructions must be supplied rather than identified with the fixed schedule
by terminology.

∂(g f )n = (∂g)n fn+1 + gn(∂f )n.
In particular exactness is equivalent to vanishing of the differential.

This is the differential of the composite. The exactness statement is (6.1).
There is no additional mixed defect term in this identity. Such a term would require a different definition
of the defect or a different expansion. Typed cancellation is a useful safeguard against importing an
attractive but incorrect error formula.
6.3 Cofinal views preserve points, not every measurement
Let ϕ : N →N be monotone and unbounded. The sampled tower uses levels Xϕ(n) and their composite
restrictions.
Theorem 6.3 (Cofinal reconstruction of points). Sampling induces an equivalence between the coherent points
of X and of its sampled tower.
Proof. Write ρi,j : Xj →Xi for the composite restriction when i ≤j, with ρi,i = I. These maps satisfy
ρi,jρj,k = ρi,k. A coherent sampled point obeys
ρϕ(n),ϕ(m)ym = yn
(n ≤m),
by iterating its adjacent coherence equations.
For each k, unboundedness gives an index n with k ≤ϕ(n). Set xk = ρk,ϕ(n)yn. If m is another permissible
index, choose ℓ≥n, m. Monotonicity permits both composite restrictions, and sampled coherence gives
ρk,ϕ(n)yn = ρk,ϕ(n)ρϕ(n),ϕ(ℓ)yℓ= ρk,ϕ(ℓ)yℓ= ρk,ϕ(m)ym.
Thus xk is independent of the choice, including when the schedule repeats a level.
For the adjacent equation at k, choose one n with k + 1 ≤ϕ(n). Then
ρk,k+1xk+1 = ρk,k+1ρk+1,ϕ(n)yn = ρk,ϕ(n)yn = xk.
The recovered family is therefore a point of the original tower.
Sampling a full point and recovering its kth coordinate gives ρk,ϕ(n)xϕ(n) = xk by its original coherence.
Recovering a sampled point and evaluating at k = ϕ(n) allows the index n itself, giving ρϕ(n),ϕ(n)yn = yn.
These are both inverse identities. No surjectivity of the bonding maps or of the point evaluations was
used.
This theorem is about exact coherent points. Raw residual size is a different quantity. On identity scalar
towers, fn = (−1)n has nonzero fine differences, but its even subsequence is constant. The coarse
residual vanishes because adjacent fine defects cancel. A cofinal equivalence of limits does not imply
equality of residual norms or an absence of aliasing in uncorrected data.

7 Finite observations and realized transformations
The point-space construction has a converse question. Given a function between coherent realization
spaces, can it be represented by compatible maps between finite views? The answer is not automatically
yes. This section constructs the relevant category of reindexed maps and identifies its realized functions
under an explicit observability hypothesis. Here a finite view means a view at a finite resolution level.
Its set of possible values need not be finite.
7.1 An output level may require a finer input level
Let X and Y be sequential set-valued resolutions. Write ρX
i,j : Xj →Xi for composite restriction. A
reindexed transport consists of a monotone schedule ϕ : N →N and maps
fn : Xϕ(n) −→Yn
satisfying, for i ≤j,
ρY
i,j fj = fiρX
ϕ(i),ϕ(j).
(7.1)
The schedule records how much input is used for each output level. Monotonicity means that asking for
more output never forces the representation to discard input already available. It does not require strict
growth, a bounded look-ahead, or an efficiency estimate.
The realized map is
| f | : Point(X) →Point(Y),
| f |(x)n = fn(xϕ(n)).
Equation (7.1) proves that this is a coherent output. A second transport gn : Yψ(n) →Zn composes with f
using schedule ϕ ◦ψ and coordinate map gn fψ(n). The order of the schedules is fixed by the types, not by
the order in which their letters are written in prose.

7.2 Equality is witnessed at finite stages
Two different schedules can represent the same transformation. To compare (ϕ, f ) and (ψ, g), require
that for every output level n there be a finite level k ≥ϕ(n), ψ(n) with
fnρX
ϕ(n),k = gnρX
ψ(n),k
as functions on all of Xk.
(7.2)
Call this eventual equality. The witnessing k may depend on n. Equality is required on the whole
finite-level fiber, not only on states that happen to extend globally.
Theorem 7.1 (The eventual transport category). Eventual equality is an equivalence relation and is preserved
by composition. Its quotient therefore gives a category of resolutions and eventual transports. Realization descends
to a functor into sets, preserving identities and composition.
Proof. A finite-stage equality remains true after moving to a later stage. Indeed, if it holds on Xk and
ℓ≥k, precompose both sides with ρX
k,ℓand use the composite-restriction laws. Reflexivity uses k = ϕ(n),
symmetry reverses the equality, and transitivity moves two witnesses to their maximum. The shared
middle presentation then cancels as a literal function on that common fiber. This proves an equivalence
relation without referring to global points.
We check compatibility with composition with all indices displayed. Write f, f ′ with schedules ϕ, ϕ′ and
g, g′ with schedules ψ, ψ′. Fix an output level n. Choose j ≥ψ(n), ψ′(n) with
gnρY

This is an actual quotient category in the formal source. No identification with the full procategory of an
arbitrary base category is needed or claimed. The objects here are sequential set-valued resolutions, and
the equivalence relation is the finite-stage relation just defined.

7.3 When global behavior determines the finite presentation
Say that X has surjective point projections when
∀k
evk : Point(X) →Xk
is onto.
This means that every finite input state really occurs in some coherent global input. It is stronger than
nonemptiness of the global space.
Theorem 7.2 (Faithfulness on an observable source). If X has surjective point projections, two reindexed maps
out of X are eventually equal exactly when they induce the same realized function. Surjective adjacent bonding
maps are a sufficient condition for surjective point projections.
Proof. Realized equality follows from eventual equality by the preceding theorem, so only its converse
remains. Suppose | f | = |g|. Fix the output level n, and let k = max{ϕ(n), ψ(n)}. To prove equality of
two maps defined on Xk, fix an arbitrary element a ∈Xk. The assumed surjectivity of evk supplies a
coherent point x with xk = a. Equality of the two realized maps gives equality of their output coordinates:
fn(xϕ(n)) = gn(xψ(n)).
Coherence of x rewrites this as
fn
 ρX
ϕ(n),k(a)
 = gn
 ρX
ψ(n),k(a)

.
The index k was selected before a, so this is one common finite-stage equality for all elements of Xk. It is
exactly the witness required by eventual equality at output n. Repeating for each n proves faithfulness.
For the sufficient condition on bonding maps, fix k and a ∈Xk. Define coordinates below k by restriction
of a. At each later stage use surjectivity of the adjacent bonding map to choose a preimage of the
coordinate already chosen. Recursion with classical choice constructs the upper tail. Adjacent equations
hold by construction, and induction on the interval length gives every composite equation. This produces
a point evaluating to a. If a fiber is empty, the statement that each of its elements lifts is vacuous, as
required by surjectivity onto that fiber. Without the projection-surjectivity assumption, equality on
realizable inputs alone need not identify finite maps on values that never occur in a global point.
The hypothesis is sufficient, not presented as a characterization of every source on which faithfulness
happens to hold. Its role in the proof is precise: it converts a statement about globally realized inputs
into a statement about all finite inputs.
Without it, global behavior can lose information. For example, use Xn = {m ∈N : m ≥n} with inclusion
bonds and a constant Boolean output tower. The source point space is empty. The two transports that
always output false and always output true induce the same function from the empty set, but disagree at
every finite stage. Every Xk is nonempty, so no eventual equality witness exists. This calculation uses the
finite-prefix boundary of Example 35.1 and does not identify empty global behavior with equality of
finite presentations.
7.4 The exact image of realization
For a function F : Point(X) →Point(Y), define finite observation by the quantifiers
∀n ∃k ∃a : Xk →Yn ∀x :
F(x)n = a(xk).
(7.3)
The input level is uniform over all global inputs for each fixed output level. It may vary with the
output level. This is not the weaker condition that each individual input can be inspected to some
input-dependent finite depth.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
19


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Theorem 7.3 (Characterization of realized transformations). If X has surjective point projections, realization
induces a bijection
EventualHom(X, Y) ≃{F : Point(X) →Point(Y) : F is finitely observed}.
Proof. For a reindexed map (ϕ, f ), the formula | f |(x)n = fn(xϕ(n)) immediately supplies a finite-
observation witness for each n. Eventual equality does not affect the realized function by Theorem 7.1,
so this defines a map from the quotient to the stated set of functions.
For surjectivity, let F be finitely observed. Choose one pair (kn, an) at each output level, satisfying
F(x)n = an(xkn) for every coherent input. Define
ϕ(n) = max
0≤i≤n ki,
fn = anρX
kn,ϕ(n).
The maximum is of a nonempty finite set. It gives a monotone schedule with kn ≤ϕ(n). For every point
x, coherence in X gives
fn(xϕ(n)) = an
 ρX
kn,ϕ(n)xϕ(n)
 = an(xkn) = F(x)n.
(*)
The remaining obligation is naturality on all finite states. Fix i ≤j and u ∈Xϕ(j). By surjectivity of that
point projection, choose x with xϕ(j) = u. Equation (*) and output coherence yield
ρY
i,j fj(u) = ρY
i,jF(x)j = F(x)i,
fiρX
ϕ(i),ϕ(j)(u) = fi(xϕ(i)) = F(x)i.
The state u was arbitrary. Hence the two finite-level functions are equal, which proves scheduled
naturality. The resulting transport realizes F by (*).
Different witness choices can give different schedules and coordinate maps, but the realized function is
still F. By Theorem 7.2 these transports are eventually equal, so their quotient class is independent of all
choices. The same faithfulness result says that two classes with equal realized functions are identical.
We have proved both surjectivity and injectivity, including the well-definedness of the inverse choice
construction. No bound on the growth of ϕ follows from these quantifiers.
The finite-level compatibility is derived in this proof. It is not inserted as an additional field in the
hypothesis that F is finitely observed. This is one of the useful bridges between a global description of a
transformation and an actual presentation in the resolution calculus.

7.5 Binary prefixes: a complete interpretation
Let Xn consist of binary words of length n, with prefix restriction. A coherent point is exactly an infinite
binary sequence: its length-n view is its first n bits. Every prefix extends, for example by appending
zeros. Hence the source satisfies the hypothesis of the characterization theorem.
Bitwise complementation is represented at level n by complementing the first n bits. The left shift
F(x)i = xi+1 uses the schedule ϕ(n) = n + 1. A nearest-neighbor difference over F2,
G(x)i = xi + xi+1,
also uses n + 1 input bits for n output bits. Applying G twice uses n + 2 bits, and the middle terms
cancel:
G(G(x))i = xi + xi+2.
The compatible finite maps make each statement meaningful on the entire coherent sequence, not merely
on one chosen truncation. These are worked instances of the scheduled formulas. The finite regression
suite checks their prefix compatibility independently.
Not every set function on these points is finitely observed. Consider the Boolean output that is one
exactly when every input bit is zero, regarded as a point of the constant Boolean tower. Given any
proposed finite level k, compare the all-zero sequence with a sequence whose first one occurs at position
k. Their first k bits agree, but the outputs differ. Therefore no finite witness a : Xk →{0, 1} can compute
even that one output coordinate. This rules out a reindexed presentation by the forward direction of the
theorem.
The obstruction in this example is one of information, not a runtime benchmark. No amount of com-
putation on the specified finite prefix distinguishes those two inputs. The theorem does not prohibit a
OPERATION KOMOREBI
21


Komorebi Space
Monograph
different mathematical representation that supplies more information as part of each view. Changing the
available view is changing the input model.
7.6 Cofinal changes also preserve the morphism calculus
A monotone unbounded schedule θ gives the sampled resolution Xθ. There is a transport from X to Xθ
that reads the selected level. For the reverse direction choose, for each n, a level j(n) with n ≤θ(j(n)),
and restrict the available value down to Xn. Taking a least such index gives a monotone schedule.
Theorem 7.4 (Cofinal invariance of eventual transports). Sampling and recovery are inverse in the eventual
transport category. Consequently, for monotone unbounded schedules θ and η, there is a bijection
EventualHom(X, Y) ≃EventualHom(Xθ, Yη).
On point spaces it agrees with the previously constructed cofinal realization equivalences.
Proof. Let s : X →Xθ be sampling, with schedule θ and identity level maps. For recovery choose the
least j(n) with n ≤θ(j(n)). Unboundedness makes this set nonempty. Increasing n only strengthens the
inequality, so j is monotone. The level map of r : Xθ →X is ρX
n,θ(j(n)). Composite restriction proves its
naturality.
The composite rs : X →X reads level θ(j(n)) and restricts to n. At that very stage its map agrees with
the identity transport after restriction from θ(j(n)) to n. Thus rs is eventually equal to the identity.
For sr : Xθ →Xθ, the schedule at output n is j(θ(n)), and the level map is ρX
θ(n),θ(j(θ(n))). Compare it with
the identity at a sampled index
k ≥max{n, j(θ(n))}.
Restricting either route from level k of the sampled system gives the same original restriction ρX
θ(n),θ(k).
This proves eventual equality even when θ has repeated values. Hence the two classes are inverse
isomorphisms, without requiring them to be literal inverse schedules.
Write sX, rX and sY, rY for the sampling and recovery isomorphisms of source and target. The asserted
Hom map is
[ f ] 7−→sY[ f ]rX,
with inverse [g] 7→rY[g]sX. Associativity and the two inverse equations cancel the inserted pairs, proving
both round trips. Applying the realization functor transports these equations to point spaces, where
sampling and recovery are the cofinal point equivalences already constructed. No norm invariance or
surjectivity of source point projections is needed for this categorical conjugation argument.
This strengthens cofinal invariance from points to a specified category of transformations. It still does
not make raw residual norms invariant, identify every global set function with a finite presentation, or
preserve the numerical conditioning of a repair. Those are separate questions, addressed by the defect
maps in the next sections.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
22


Komorebi Space
Monograph
For a projection-surjective source, eventual finite presentations correspond exactly to uniformly
finite observations at each output level. This is a precise machine-oriented representation theorem.
It is not a claim that every represented map is computationally efficient, or that every arbitrary
function of an infinite input has such a presentation.

The surjectivity is onto all bounded residual families. It is stronger than the assertion that some exact
output can be assigned to each candidate. The gap and the chosen splittings are genuine inputs.

Finally, P f has coordinates (pL f, 0). Any exact answer with the same observable has those same two
coordinates, hence equals P f. This uniqueness is relative to the complete observable of seed and
innovations. It is not obtained by assuming that the seed alone determines an exact growing-target
family.
This is an actual analytic coordinate equivalence, not generally an isometry. The protected coordinate
depends on the declared splitting. It should not be called an intrinsic semantic coordinate without a
further comparison theorem.

8.5 A growing example and the gap boundary
Use Xn = Yn = Rn+1 with maximum norms. Let πn delete the last coordinate, take ρX
n = 1
2πn, ρY
n = πn,
and let sn append zero. Then S = BY = 1, BX = 1/2, and K = 2.
Example 8.4 (Repair of the identity family). The bounded candidate fn = I is not exact. Its repair is
(P f )n = diag(2−n, 2−(n−1), . . . , 2−1, 1).
The new last output row is retained at every stage while inherited rows are propagated by the factor 1/2.
Proof. At level zero the value is one. Exactness forces every inherited row at the next level to be one half
of its previous value. Preservation of the innovation forces the new row to remain the corresponding
identity row. Induction gives the displayed diagonal.
For constant real scalar identity towers, by contrast, (∂f )n = fn+1 −fn. The constant residual rn = 1 has
no bounded primitive, since any primitive satisfies fn = f0 + n. Here SBX = 1. This counterexample
excludes discarding the gap indiscriminately. It does not prove that the gap is necessary for every
individual tower. Exact repair preserving the initial coordinate still exists in this constant example, by
replacing a candidate with its initial constant family. Surjectivity onto all bounded residuals does not
follow from that repair.
8.6 What is canonical
For fixed splitting and protected observable, the repair above is unique. Changing the splitting can
change both the observable and the repaired answer. In the growing example, replace sn(y) = (y, 0) by

9 Repair under a change of representation
An exact transformation should carry coherent inputs to coherent outputs. It need not carry the selected
repair of an incoherent input to the selected repair of its transformed image. The distinction has an exact
formula. In split coordinates, a compatible change of representation is generally block triangular rather
than block diagonal. Its off-diagonal term records precisely the discrepancy between the two repair
routes.
9.1 A section is a choice of residual coordinates
Let d : E →R be a continuous linear map between normed spaces over a nontrivially normed field.
Suppose it has a continuous linear right inverse A : R →E, so dA = IR. Set
K = ker d,
PA = IE −Ad.
Then dPA = 0, PA fixes K, and P2
A = PA. The right inverse chooses where each residual is represented
inside the candidate space. Different right inverses can choose different representatives of the same
residual.
Theorem 9.1 (The split defect chart). The maps
χA : E ≃L K × R,
χA(x) = (PAx, dx),
χ−1
A (u, r) = u + Ar
are inverse continuous linear equivalences. In these coordinates, PA sends (u, r) to (u, 0). If B is another right
inverse, then A −B takes values in K, and
PBx −PAx = (A −B)dx.
The projections commute if and only if they are equal, and they are equal if and only if A = B.
Proof. The identity dA = I gives dPA = d −dAd = 0, so the proposed first chart coordinate actually
belongs to K. If u ∈K, then du = 0 and PAu = u. Therefore, for (u, r) ∈K × R,
d(u + Ar) = r,
PA(u + Ar) = u + Ar −A(r) = u.
In the other direction, PAx + A(dx) = x −Adx + Adx = x. These are both inverse equations, including
the membership condition for the kernel coordinate. All component maps are bounded linear, so they
define a continuous linear equivalence on the standard finite product. Applied to PAx, the chart gives
(PAx, 0), proving the repair formula.
For a second right inverse B, the equation d(A −B) = I −I = 0 places its difference from A in K. Direct
subtraction gives
PB −PA = (I −Bd) −(I −Ad) = (A −B)d.
Both projections have range K and fix every point of K. Thus applying PA after PB gives PB, while
applying PB after PA gives PA. These two compositions are equal if and only if the projections are equal.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
28


Komorebi Space
Monograph
If PA = PB, evaluate their difference at Ar. Since dAr = r, the difference formula yields (A −B)r = 0 for
every r, hence A = B. Equality of sections conversely makes their projection formulas identical. The
argument uses the right inverse to test every residual. It does not infer equality of sections merely from
agreement on some restricted set of observed residuals.
This is a linear splitting, not necessarily an orthogonal one. The exact coordinate u depends on the
chosen section. The residual coordinate r = dx does not. A specified Hilbert geometry can make one
section distinguished through orthogonality, but the algebraic split alone does not do so.
The tower construction is stronger in a different direction. Its recurrence builds a right inverse from the
target splittings, and its observable identifies what that repair preserves. The abstract chart here isolates
the mechanism shared by those concrete constructions.
9.2 A change of representation has two components
Let D : F →T be a second continuous linear defect map. A compatible change consists of bounded
linear maps
S : E →F,
C : R →T,
DS = Cd.
The equation is the commuting defect square. It says that transforming a candidate and then measuring
its defect agrees with transforming its actual defect. No sections have yet been used.
In particular, S carries ker d into ker D. It may still have a kernel, lose information, or fail to be onto.
Calling it a change of representation does not assert an equivalence unless inverse maps have also been
supplied.
Choose sections A of d and B of D. Define
M = SA −BC : R −→F.
(9.1)
The commuting square and section identities give DM = 0. Thus this mismatch takes residual informa-
tion into an exact direction of the target.
Theorem 9.2 (Triangular transport and repair mismatch). In split defect coordinates, the change of representa-
tion is
χBSχ−1
A (u, r) = (Su + Mr, Cr).
(9.2)
The two repair routes differ by
PBSx −SPAx = M(dx).

For two consecutive compatible changes (S1, C1), (S2, C2), with compatible choices of sections at the three ends,
their mismatches satisfy
M21 = S2M1 + M2C1.
If dxj →0 in norm for a fixed change and fixed sections, the repair discrepancy tends to zero.

Proof. First DS = Cd sends ker d into ker D, and

This proves the triangular formula as an equality in ker D × T.
For a general input, use the same commuting square to obtain
PBSx −SPAx = Sx −BDSx −Sx + SAdx
= (SA −BC)dx = M(dx).
Its norm is at most ∥M∥∥dx∥. The triangle inequality and submultiplicativity give ∥M∥≤∥S∥∥A∥+

That equality conversely makes the discrepancy vanish everywhere.
For composition, denote the three sections by A, B, A2. The composite residual map is C2C1, so its
mismatch is S2S1A −A2C2C1. Insert and subtract S2BC1:
S2S1A −A2C2C1 = S2(S1A −BC1) + (S2B −A2C2)C1

bound.
The off-diagonal term in (9.2) has a specific meaning. A raw residual can become part of the target’s
chosen exact coordinate after a change of presentation. Deleting the residual before changing presentation
removes that contribution. Deleting it afterwards does not. This is not a failure of exactness preservation.
Both routes end in the target exact space, but they can end at different points in it.
9.3 A two-coordinate model in which the routes differ
Use E = F = R2, write candidates as (u, r), and take
d(u, r) = D(u, r) = r,
A(r) = B(r) = (0, r).
The exact space is the horizontal axis and both repairs are P(u, r) = (u, 0). For a real parameter c, set
Sc(u, r) = (u + cr, r),
C = I.
The defect square commutes because the second coordinate is unchanged. The mismatch is Mc(r) =
(cr, 0). Hence
PSc(u, r) = (u + cr, 0),
ScP(u, r) = (u, 0).
With c = 2 and input (0, 1), one route gives (2, 0) and the other gives (0, 0). Both have zero residual. A
report containing only their final residuals would miss the entire difference.
Two shears compose by adding their parameters. In the mismatch law this appears as Mc2+c1 =
Sc2Mc1 + Mc2, and Sc2 fixes the exact horizontal axis. The identity is therefore a simple instance of the
same composition rule, not an unrelated coordinate trick.

9.4 Actual tower resampling gives a defect map
Return to Banach towers with contractive source and target bonds. Let ϕ be a monotone schedule.
Candidate sampling is
(Sϕ f )n = fϕ(n).
The sampled bonding map is the actual composite restriction between the selected levels. The residual
map Cϕ is not pointwise sampling of a scalar error score. It accumulates transported fine defects across
each omitted block.
For a block from a to b, its value on a residual family r is

Proof. For a bounded candidate family, supn

Successive internal terms cancel in the finite sum, leaving ρY

If the fine differential vanishes, the finite telescope is zero on every selected interval. That conclusion
remains true for unbounded gap lengths, because each individual interval is finite. What may fail
without a gap bound is boundedness of the residual transformation on arbitrary bounded residual
families, not preservation of exact inputs.
When the two bounded sections exist, Theorem 9.2 applies to the commuting square just established.
Substitute
Sϕ
 ≤1 and
Cϕ

is an additional hypothesis, not a consequence of the sampling estimate.
The existence of the two right inverses is not inferred merely from contractivity of the bonds. The split-
tower theorem provides them under its additional section and propagation-gap conditions. Keeping
these hypotheses separate prevents a resampling theorem from being mistaken for an unconditional
reconstruction algorithm.
9.5 Two distinct losses under coarsening
On scalar identity towers, a fine residual block (1, −1) has zero two-step sum. Thus Cϕr = 0 need not
imply r = 0, even for a bounded-gap schedule. This is cancellation. Retaining an independent detail
coordinate remedies that information loss in a split coarsener, as the next section shows.
Unbounded gaps cause a different issue. With ϕ(n) = n2, the bounded residual family rk = 1 has block
sums 2n + 1. These are unbounded. Therefore block accumulation cannot be a map from every bounded
residual family to a bounded sampled residual family in this example. The constant residual is not
claimed to be the differential of a bounded candidate on the identity tower. It is a witness about the
proposed operator on the entire residual space.
Neither example contradicts cofinal invariance of coherent points or eventual transports. Those theorems
concern exact compatibility. A norm on all raw defects, the retained fine detail, and the choice of a repair
section are additional structures.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
32


Komorebi Space
Monograph
A compatible change of representation transports equations correctly. It transports the selected
repair correctly only when the chosen residual sections also commute. The mismatch is an exact-
valued operator, so it can remain invisible to a residual-only check.
10 Multiscale residuals and the limits of reconstruction
10.1 A split coarsener retains its missing detail
Let C : R →R and J : R →R be linear maps with CJ = I. Put H = I −JC. Then
r = JCr + Hr,
CH = 0,
H2 = H.
The two coordinates (Cr, Hr) retain all information. The coarse coordinate alone generally does not.
The actual dyadic tower coarsener has the typed formula
(Cr)n = ρY
2nr2n+1 + r2nρX
2n+1.
(10.1)
It is obtained by telescoping two adjacent compatibility squares. With target sections, a lift is
(Jc)2n = 0,
(Jc)2n+1 = s2ncn.
The section identity gives CJ = I, so the missing detail is explicitly Hr, not an unspecified reconstruction
error. The normed construction imposes the bounds needed to make these maps bounded on its operator
family spaces.

10.3 Convergence has its own theorem
Call x norm recoverable when QNx →0. This is equivalent to SNx →x, but it does not follow merely

Theorem 10.2 (Density criterion for norm recovery). Suppose supN ∥QN∥≤K < ∞. Then the norm
recoverable vectors are exactly F. In particular all vectors are norm recoverable if and only if the finite core is
dense.
Proof. The nested identities first imply that the kernels increase: if QMy = 0 and N ≥M, then QNy =
QNQMy = 0. Thus the finite core really is an increasing union of linear subspaces.
Suppose QNx →0. Put yN = SNx = x −QNx. Idempotence gives QNyN = QNx −Q2
Nx = 0, so yN ∈F.
Since x −yN = QNx →0, these are finite-core approximants converging to x. Hence x ∈F.
Conversely, fix x ∈F and ϵ > 0. If K = 0, the common operator bound gives ∥QNx∥= 0 for every N,
so convergence is immediate. Suppose K > 0. Choose y ∈F satisfying ∥x −y∥< ϵ/(K + 1). Some M
has QMy = 0. For every N ≥M, nesting and the common operator bound give
∥QNx∥= ∥QN(x −y)∥≤K ∥x −y∥< ϵ

The approximation y is chosen before the depth N, giving precisely the quantifier order in norm
convergence. This proves equality of the two sets. Applying it to every x ∈R0 gives the density
criterion.
There are other sufficient conditions. If the transported details are absolutely summable in the Banach
space R0, their sum exists and the remaining boundary lies in P∞. If that persistent subspace is zero, the
sum equals x. Alternatively,

10.4 The actual dyadic result
For the dyadic residual hierarchy defined above, the persistent residual is zero. Zero detail at a scale
reveals every even coordinate, while an odd coordinate is passed to a smaller index at the next scale.
Strong induction on the coordinate index therefore proves that a residual with all details zero is zero.
Theorem 10.3 (Dyadic determination and finite coordinate recovery). In the actual dyadic system, all detail
coordinates jointly determine the residual. Moreover,
k + 1 < 2N
=⇒

(SNr)k = rk.
Thus every fixed residual coordinate is recovered exactly at a sufficiently large finite depth. The statement does not
assert convergence in the supremum norm over all coordinates.

has value 2N at every surviving position. That example proves sharpness of the uniform prefix cutoff.

10.5 Two boundary examples
On ℓ∞(N, R), let C remove the first coordinate and let J prepend zero. Then QNx is the tail of x, placed
at its original coordinates with zeros before N. Every fixed coordinate is eventually zero in the remainder,
and ∥QN∥≤1. Nevertheless the constant sequence 1 satisfies ∥QN1∥= 1 for every N.
Proposition 10.5 (The recovery domain is c0). For this shift cascade,
SNx →x in ℓ∞
⇐⇒

For xn = qn, 0 ≤q < 1, the error is exactly qN.
Proof. The error norm is supn≥N |xn|. It tends to zero precisely when the sequence tends to zero. For a
geometric sequence its largest tail value is the first one, qN.
As a second example, take scalar spaces with C = 1
2 I, J = 2I. The coarse value xN = 2−Nx tends to
zero, but restoration gives QNx = x. Every detail is zero and the entire input is persistent. The example
belongs to this scalar cascade, not to the actual dyadic hierarchy, whose persistent subspace was proved
to vanish.
Retained packets make omission explicit. They do not guarantee convergence. Coordinate recovery,
bounded reconstruction, summability, and attainment remain different mathematical properties.

Part III
Symmetry and effective descent
A set of solutions forgets the witnesses relating equivalent solutions. The next layer
retains those witnesses, their composition, and their automorphisms. Gluing then has
to respect the overlap identifications themselves.
11 Nonabelian coherence and relative trivialization
11.1 Coherent transition labels
Fix groups Gv and restriction homomorphisms f ∗: Gw →Gv, with the same identity and composition
laws as before. The groups need not be abelian and the homomorphisms need not be bijective.
Definition 11.1 (Cocycle). A cocycle is a family c f ∈Gv for every f : v →w, satisfying
c1v = 1,
cg◦f = c f f ∗(cg).
It determines a twisted view system with maps
Tc( f )(x) = c f f ∗(x).
A section of the twisted system satisfies xv = c f f ∗(xw).
The cocycle law says that the transition rules compose coherently. It does not assert that the twisted
system has a section. The coherent transition system and a global trivialization of that system are
different objects.
For a family a = (av) ∈∏v Gv, define
(a · c) f = avc f [ f ∗(aw)]−1.
(11.1)
Proposition 11.2 (Gauge action and its groupoid). Equation (11.1) defines an action on cocycles. A gauge
carrying c to d induces an equivalence of twisted section sets by xv 7→avxv. The cocycles and actual gauges form
a groupoid: an arrow a : c →d followed by b : d →e has label ba.
Proof. First, Tc really is a view system. For f : v →w, g : w →z, and x ∈Gz, the homomorphism and
cocycle laws give
Tc( f )Tc(g)(x) = c f f ∗(cgg∗x) = c f f ∗(cg) f ∗g∗x = cg◦f (g ◦f )∗x.
The identity equation follows from c1v = 1. No rearrangement of factors is involved.
Put d = a · c. Its identity label is av1a−1
v
= 1. For a composite, expand all factors in their original order:
d f f ∗(dg) = avc f [ f ∗(aw)]−1 f ∗(awcg[g∗(az)]−1)
= avc f [ f ∗(aw)]−1 f ∗(aw) f ∗(cg)[ f ∗g∗(az)]−1
= avcg◦f [(g ◦f )∗(az)]−1 = dg◦f .

Thus the gauge formula preserves the cocycle condition. For two gauges a, b, substitution gives
(a · (b · c)) f = avbvc f [ f ∗(bw)]−1[ f ∗(aw)]−1 = ((ab) · c) f .
The reversed order in the two inverse factors is exactly the formula for the inverse of a product. The
identity gauge acts trivially.
If d = a · c and x is a section of Tc, put yv = avxv. Then
d f f ∗(yw) = avc f [ f ∗(aw)]−1 f ∗(aw) f ∗(xw) = avxv = yv.
The inverse map is multiplication by a−1
v
at each view, proving an equivalence of the actual section sets.
For the groupoid, an arrow a : c →d means the equation a · c = d. If b : d →e, then (ba) · c = b · (a · c) = e,
giving composition. The identity label is 1 and the inverse label is a−1. Associativity and the two inverse
identities now follow from the corresponding multiplication laws in the product group. The label ba is
therefore forced by the order of application, not an arbitrary notational convention.
The action groupoid Z1(V; G)// ∏v Gv retains comparison witnesses. Its orbit set Z1(V; G)/ ∏v Gv
retains only isomorphism classes. There is no reason for the orbit set to be a group in this nonabelian
setting.
11.2 Automorphisms and ambiguity are not the same question
The stabilizer of a cocycle is the group of families with
av = c f f ∗(aw)c−1
f .
If one gauge a : c →d is known, all gauges from c to d are uniquely of the form ah, with h ∈Stab(c).
Indeed a−1b stabilizes c for every other gauge b. This is the ordinary Hom torsor principle in a groupoid.
That symmetry group must not be confused with the set of global trivializations of one fixed cocycle.
The latter will be parametrized by ordinary coherent group elements, after one trivialization has been
selected.

The cancellation uses inverse multiplication in its actual order. No commuting factors have been moved

The extension set is nonempty precisely when the earlier orbit obstruction is neutral. Once it is nonempty,
the displayed bijection makes it a singleton precisely when HU has only its identity element. Both
conditions are needed for unique existence. Changing the chosen extension changes the parametrization,
not the extension set or the criterion.
The additive theory is recovered by applying a multiplicative type tag to an additive view system and
choosing the trivial cocycle. The normalized label then becomes f ∗qw −qv. The source proves agreement
of the actual extension sets and vanishing criteria. It does not identify the whole nonabelian orbit set
with every element of the larger additive arrow residual quotient.
11.4 A small noncommutative model
Example 11.5 (A C2 loop with S3 coefficients). Take one view with a loop σ satisfying σ2 = 1, and
constant coefficients in a group H. A cocycle is determined by an element p ∈H whose square is the
identity. Gauge equivalence is conjugacy, and its automorphisms form the centralizer of p.
For H = S3, these elements are the identity and the three transpositions. There are exactly two orbit
classes. The identity cocycle has automorphism group S3, which is noncommutative. A nonidentity
involution gives a coherent cocycle whose regular twisted section equation x = px has no solution.
Proof. The only nontrivial cocycle relation is p2 = 1. At a single view the gauge action is p 7→apa−1. All
three transpositions are conjugate and none is conjugate to the identity. The stabilizer is therefore the
centralizer. Finally x = px implies p = 1 after multiplication by x−1.
The example makes an important boundary visible. A cocycle can exist and glue as an object even when
it admits no global trivializing section. Stackhood will concern the former gluing problem, not a claim
that every cocycle becomes trivial.
12 Effective gluing of objects and their identifications
12.1 Why pairwise agreement is not the right datum
For objects with symmetry, local descriptions need not be literally equal on overlaps. They may instead
come with specified isomorphisms. Those isomorphisms must satisfy their own composition law. It is
not enough to know that some isomorphism exists for each pair.

Theorem 12.1 (Effective cocycle descent). Under (12.1), restriction is an equivalence between the global cocycle
gauge groupoid and the groupoid of descent data. The overlap gauges supplied in the datum are realized, not
discarded.
Proof. Step 1: normalize the supplied identifications. The triple-overlap equation with all indices equal gives
aii = aiiaii, hence aii = 1. With indices i, j, i it gives ajiaij = 1, hence aji = a−1
ij . Composition coverage
implies object coverage by inserting identity arrows. Choose a containing patch κ(v) for each view and
put
bi(v) = aκ(v),i(v)
(v ∈Ui).
At a point of Ui ∩Uj, the triple law, now on patches κ(v), i, j, gives
bj(v) = aij(v)bi(v).
(*)
The two endpoints of an arrow may have different reference patches. No common reference patch is
being assumed.
Step 2: obtain literal matching labels. Define
eci( f ) = bi(v)−1ci( f ) f ∗(bi(w))
( f : v →w in Ui).
This is a gauge transform and hence a cocycle on Ui. If the arrow lies also in Uj, substitute the original
overlap equation and (*) at its two endpoints:
ecj( f ) = bi(v)−1aij(v)−1aij(v)ci( f )[ f ∗aij(w)]−1 f ∗(aij(w)bi(w))
= bi(v)−1ci( f ) f ∗(bi(w)) = eci( f ).
OPERATION KOMOREBI
42


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Every cancellation is between neighboring inverse factors. Commutativity is not required.
Step 3: construct the global cocycle. Every arrow is contained in a patch, again by inserting an identity in a
composable pair. Define c f to be eci( f ) in any such patch. Step 2 proves independence. For an identity
arrow, choose a patch through its view to obtain c1v = 1. For f : v →w and g : w →z, composition
coverage supplies a patch containing all three vertices. Fullness places f, g, and g ◦f in that same patch.
Its cocycle law therefore proves
cg◦f = eci(g ◦f ) = eci( f ) f ∗(eci(g)) = c f f ∗(cg).
This is the exact point where composition coverage, rather than arrow coverage alone, is used.
Undoing the normalization proves
bi(v)c f [ f ∗(bi(w))]−1 = ci( f ).
Together with (*), these gauges constitute an isomorphism from the restriction of c to the supplied
descent datum. They implement its original overlaps, proving essential surjectivity of restriction.
Step 4: retain every morphism. Let c, d be global cocycles. Their canonical restrictions have identity overlap
gauges. A morphism between those restrictions is consequently a family ri satisfying rj(v) = ri(v) on
each overlap. Assemble rv from any containing patch. For an arrow choose a patch containing both
endpoints. Its gauge equation proves
d f = rvc f [ f ∗(rw)]−1.
Thus r is a global gauge. Its restriction is the original family. Conversely, assembling the restrictions of a
global gauge preserves every coordinate and returns that gauge. Restriction is therefore bijective on each
Hom set, not merely injective on isomorphism classes. This is full faithfulness.
Step 5: obtain an equivalence. For each descent object choose the global cocycle and isomorphism con-
structed above. For an arrow between descent objects, conjugate it by these two chosen isomorphisms
and use the unique preimage under the Hom bijection of Step 4. This defines the arrow map of a
quasi-inverse. Uniqueness of the preimage verifies its identity and composition laws, and the cho-
sen isomorphisms provide the comparison to the identity functor. Hence the restriction functor is an
equivalence of groupoids. Different patch choices can alter the representative but not this conclusion.
12.2 The correct uniqueness statement
Suppose (c, bi) and (c′, b′
i) realize the same descent datum. The only possible compatible comparison is
gv = b′
i(v)−1bi(v),
using any patch through v. The overlap identities make it independent of the patch, and b′
ig = bi. It is
therefore the unique global comparison respecting the fixed local identifications.
This is not a statement that the underlying cocycle has no automorphisms. Those automorphisms can
reappear when the local identifications are forgotten. Nor does the construction choose one representative
independently of every reference patch. It proves that the choices are related by the appropriate unique
comparison.
