13 A genuine stack on the composition site
The preceding theorem can be placed in a standard site, rather than called stack-like. We use the usual
effective-descent meaning of a stack [2, 3]. The base of this site is the inclusion poset of full patches
P(V) = (Set(Ob V), ⊆).
This patch category is different from the original category of views.
13.1 The topology is defined without assuming effectivity
For a composable pair q = (v
f−→w
g−→z), let its support be |q| = {v, w, z}. A sieve R on W is covering
when
|q| ⊆W
=⇒
(|q| ,→W) ∈R
for every composable q.
(13.1)
This condition depends only on the view category, not on the coefficient groups or on a promised global
realization.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
44


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Proposition 13.1 (The composition topology). Condition (13.1) defines a Grothendieck topology Jcomp. The
sieve generated by a family of inclusions Ui ⊆W is covering exactly when the family covers every composition
support in W.
Proof. In the inclusion poset, identify a sieve on W with a downward-closed collection of subsets of W.
Pullback along Z ⊆W consists of those subsets of Z that were already members of the original sieve. We
verify the three topology axioms using this description.
The maximal sieve contains every subset of W, hence every composition support. For stability under
pullback, let R cover W and let q be a composable pair whose support lies in Z ⊆W. The same pair lies
in the full patch W, so |q| ∈R. Since |q| ⊆Z, it belongs to the pulled-back sieve on Z. Thus that sieve
covers Z.
For transitivity, suppose R covers W, and that for every U ∈R the pullback of a sieve T to U covers
U. Fix a composable pair q in W. Its support S = |q| belongs to R. Since patches are full, q is also
a composable pair in S. The covering condition for the pullback of T to S therefore puts the identity
inclusion S ⊆S in that pullback. By the definition of pullback, this says S ∈T . It holds for every q, so T
covers W.
Finally, the sieve generated by Ui ⊆W consists exactly of the subpatches Z contained in at least one Ui.
One inclusion follows because a sieve contains all restrictions of its generators. The reverse inclusion
follows because this collection is itself downward closed and contains all generators, hence is the smallest
such sieve. Consequently a support belongs to the generated sieve exactly when some Ui contains all its
endpoints. This proves the family characterization. When W is empty, there are no composable pairs to
test, so even the empty sieve is covering.
Intersections of a covering family with a subpatch remain covering. Replacing each member by a
covering family again gives a cover. Identities imply coverage of single views and arrows. The empty
patch admits the empty cover.
13.2 The actual patch pseudofunctor
On a patch W, use
KG(W) = Z1(V|W; G|W)// ∏
v∈W
Gv.
Restriction keeps the existing cocycle values and gauge values on the smaller patch. It first gives a
strict category valued functor on the opposite patch poset. Its standard pseudofunctor form has identity
comparison isomorphisms, with the required coherence laws.
Theorem 13.2 (Stackhood on the composition site). The patch pseudofunctor KG is a stack for Jcomp. It
satisfies the standard condition of descent for morphisms and effective descent for objects on every covering sieve.
Proof. We use the equivalence-of-descent-categories formulation of stackhood for the already constructed
patch pseudofunctor. Its restriction maps act on actual cocycle labels and gauge values. Their identity
and composition laws have been proved, so the descent condition is applied to a specified pseudofunctor,
not assumed as part of its definition.
From standard descent data to overlap labels. For a covering family (Ui ⊆W), standard descent data provide
local objects and comparison gauges on common subpatches, natural under further restriction. At a
view v ∈Ui ∩Uj, restrict the comparison to the singleton patch {v} and call its value aij(v). To verify
OPERATION KOMOREBI
45


Komorebi Space
Monograph
the gauge equation along f : v →w in an overlap, use the comparison on Ui ∩Uj, which is a full patch
containing the arrow. Naturality identifies its two endpoint values with the singleton values. Its own
gauge equation then becomes (12.2). Evaluating the standard triple-comparison identity at a singleton
gives (12.3). Thus these labels form the concrete descent datum of Theorem 12.1.
From the concrete realization back to standard data. By Theorem 12.1 there is a global cocycle c and local
gauges bi implementing these overlaps. On any common subpatch Z ⊆Ui ∩Uj, the required equation
between gauge arrows is an equality of their values at every v ∈Z. At such a view it is bj(v) = aij(v)bi(v),
already proved by the construction. Equality of coordinate functions proves equality of the arrows on Z.
Thus every standard common-subpatch equation holds, not only the equations on singleton tests. This
gives an isomorphism from the standard restriction of c to the original standard descent object.
Morphisms. A standard morphism between restrictions of global objects has local gauge components. Its
comparison equations at singleton patches show that these components agree on overlaps. The Hom
gluing map from Theorem 12.1 yields a unique global gauge. Conversely, a global gauge restricts to
those components and satisfies all common-subpatch equations. Restriction and gluing are inverse on
every Hom set. Standard restriction is therefore fully faithful as well as essentially surjective, hence an
equivalence.
Every covering sieve. Let R be an arbitrary covering sieve on W. Index a family by all its member
inclusions U ⊆W. Each composition support belongs to this family by the covering definition, so it is
a composition cover. Its generated sieve is exactly R: one inclusion uses that R is downward closed,
and the other uses each member as its own generator. The family argument therefore proves the descent
equivalence on the original sieve. Applying it to every covering sieve proves both descent of morphisms
and effectiveness of objects. These are the required standard stack conditions [2, 3].
The principal source declaration is GroupViewSystem.patch_isStack.
Its target is mathlib’s
Pseudofunctor.IsStack, not a project predicate that repeats the conclusion as an assumption.
13.3 The cover is nontrivial, but specific
On a four-view chain, take the four patches obtained by deleting one vertex. All patches are proper, yet
every composable pair has at most three endpoints and lies in one of them. The theorem genuinely glues
pieces smaller than the whole object.
On the three-view chain, the analogous proper patches cover each object and each arrow but miss the
support of 0 →1 →2. They do not form a cover in this topology. Consequently the theorem is neither
vacuous nor an unrestricted gluing principle.
Exact scope. This is an ordinary stack of strict group valued cocycle realizations on a specified
combinatorial site. It is not an infinity-stack, a universal heterogeneous atlas, or an arithmetic
existence theorem. The stack theorem glues coherent cocycles with their overlap isomorphisms. It
does not assert that every resulting cocycle has a global trivialization.

14 Equivalent presentations and identified boundaries
14.1 Transporting the same question
Let H : V →W be part of a category equivalence, with inverse L and counit ε : HL ⇒idW. Pull a view
system F on W back along H. Sampling a global section gives (H∗x)v = xHv.
Proposition 14.1 (Equivalence of section spaces). Pullback induces Γ(F) ≃Γ(H∗F). If U ⊆Ob W is closed
under isomorphism and U′ = H−1U, it also induces
ExtF
U(s) ≃ExtH∗F
U′ (H∗s).
Existence and uniqueness of the prescribed extension are preserved and reflected.
Proof. Write the unit of the equivalence as η : IV ⇒LH, and take the unit and counit with their triangle
identities. Set tw = ε−1
w : w →HLw. Given y ∈Γ(H∗F), define
(Ry)w = F(tw)yLw.
For an arrow f : w →w′, naturality of the inverse counit states
HL( f ) tw = tw′ f.
Contravariance of F and coherence of y therefore give
F( f )(Ry)w′ = F( f )F(tw′)yLw′
= F(tw)F(HL( f ))yLw′ = F(tw)yLw = (Ry)w.
Thus recovery gives an actual coherent section.
If x ∈Γ(F), coherence along tw gives
(RH∗x)w = F(tw)xHLw = xw.
For the other composite, the triangle identity says tHv = H(ηv). Coherence of y along ηv : v →LHv
yields
(H∗Ry)v = F(tHv)yLHv = F(Hηv)yLHv = yv.
These coordinate equations prove both inverse identities.
For the relative claim, pullback clearly sends an extension of s to an extension of H∗s on U′ = H−1U.
Conversely suppose y extends H∗s. If w ∈U, the isomorphism w ∼= HLw and isomorphism closure
imply HLw ∈U. Hence Lw ∈U′ and yLw = sHLw. Both endpoints of tw lie in U, so partial coherence
gives
(Ry)w = F(tw)sHLw = sw.
Recovery therefore preserves the required boundary. The two inverse identities remain valid on the
subsets of extensions. Bijections preserve nonemptiness and identify any two elements exactly when
their images are equal, proving preservation and reflection of existence and uniqueness.
The coefficients really are pulled back. This does not compare unrelated coefficient systems simply
because their diagrams look alike. Isomorphism closure is a sufficient general hypothesis for the relative
statement. The theorem does not assert that every unsaturated domain fails.
For group coefficients, the invisible coherent groups HU are transported by a multiplicative equivalence,
and the parameterizations x0h commute with pullback. Thus feasibility and ambiguity are both retained.
This is more than a comparison of the number of extensions. It is not an assertion that every possible
based gauge orbit set is presentation independent.

14.2 Why a boundary must retain its identification
Let R : C →D be a functor between groupoids, and fix b ∈D. A realization over b should include a
chosen isomorphism to b, rather than require literal equality of representatives.
Definition 14.2 (Marked realization groupoid). The objects of Eb(R) are pairs (x, ϕ), with x ∈C and
ϕ : Rx ∼−→b. An arrow (x, ϕ) →(y, ψ) is an arrow a : x →y with ψR(a) = ϕ.
Composition and inverses preserve this equation, so this is a groupoid. It is the explicit groupoid
homotopy fiber construction, without invoking a higher categorical interface.
Proposition 14.3 (Existence and invisible automorphisms). The groupoid Eb(R) is nonempty exactly when b
is in the essential image of R. An automorphism of (x, ϕ) is an automorphism a of x satisfying R(a) = 1Rx.
Proof. The first assertion is the object definition. For the second, the boundary equation is ϕR(a) = ϕ.
Cancel the isomorphism ϕ. Conversely R(a) = 1 makes the equation hold.
This retains precisely the symmetries invisible at the identified boundary. It avoids freezing arbitrary
representative coordinates and then mistaking the result for an invariant realization problem.

Apply this to the actual restriction functors between cocycle patch groupoids and the equivalences
induced by an equivalent view presentation. The full realization problem, including comparison
witnesses and automorphisms, is transported.
Replacing b by an isomorphic boundary b′ also gives an equivalence, by composing the identification
with b →b′. For R = id, the marked realization groupoid has center (b, 1b), and between (x, ϕ) and
(y, ψ) there is exactly one compatible arrow ψ−1ϕ. This is a useful example of contractible realization
data, not a claim that the original groupoid has no symmetries.

15 A boundary identification is data
The groupoid formulation can seem more elaborate than a set of equivalence classes until a change
of presentation is examined closely. A two-view example is enough to show what is lost by freezing
representatives instead of retaining their comparison maps. It also explains why the marked realization
construction uses an identification at the boundary.
15.1 Duplicate a view without adding information
Let the original view category have one object and only its identity arrow. Replace it by a category
with two objects, 0 and 1, and exactly one arrow between each ordered pair. These arrows are inverse
isomorphisms. The two categories are equivalent: selecting object zero and forgetting the duplicate give
inverse equivalences up to the evident isomorphism.
Use a constant group H as coefficients, with identity pullback homomorphisms. A cocycle on the
duplicated category is specified by its label r ∈H on the arrow 0 →1. Its reverse label is r−1, and
identity labels are one. A gauge (a0, a1) carries r to
r′ = a0ra−1
1 .
(15.1)
Every r is isomorphic to the trivial cocycle, for example with gauge (1, r). The automorphisms of r are
the pairs
(a0, r−1a0r),
a0 ∈H.
Thus the unframed groupoid has one isomorphism class but retains a copy of H as the automorphism
group of each object. This is compatible with the original one-view coefficient groupoid, which has one
object and automorphism group H.
15.2 Freezing both representative gauges breaks that comparison
Now require a based gauge to be the identity at every view. On the duplicated category the only
permitted gauge is (1, 1). Equation (15.1) then carries r only to itself. Distinct labels cannot be compared.
Restrict to the selected view zero. Every cocycle becomes the same one-view identity cocycle. The
distinction between r and 1 has disappeared in the target, even though the fully framed source has no
comparison arrow between them.
Proposition 15.1 (Full representative framing is not invariant). For a nontrivial coefficient group, the
restriction from the fully framed duplicated model to the fully framed single-view model need not be full, and hence
need not be an equivalence. This failure occurs even though the underlying view categories are equivalent and the
full prescribed domains are closed under isomorphism.
Proof. Choose r ̸= 1. In the fully framed duplicated model there is no arrow from r to 1, because the
only allowed gauge leaves r unchanged. After restriction to view zero, both become the same cocycle.
There is an identity arrow between their images. That target arrow has no preimage, so the functor is not
full. Fullness is necessary for an equivalence.
The failure is not repaired by declaring the two objects equivalent as sets of labels. The issue is exactly the
missing arrow. Nor does it contradict the theorem for marked relative realizations. That theorem does
not freeze every arbitrary representative coordinate. It transports an actual boundary object together
with an identification to it.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
50


Komorebi Space
Monograph
15.3 The marked fiber keeps the comparison that matters
In the same example, let R restrict an unframed duplicated cocycle to view zero. Fix the unique boundary
object. A marked realization has two pieces of data:
(r, ϕ),
r ∈H,
ϕ ∈H.
The element ϕ is the chosen identification of the restricted object with the fixed boundary. It is not
discarded just because the boundary has only one object: that object still has many automorphisms.
A comparison from (r, ϕ) to (r′, ψ) is a gauge (a0, a1) satisfying both the cocycle equation and the
boundary equation
r′ = a0ra−1
1 ,
ψa0 = ϕ.
These equations determine it uniquely:
a0 = ψ−1ϕ,
a1 = (r′)−1a0r.
(15.2)
Substituting the formulas proves both equations without commuting any factors. Thus in this example
there is exactly one identification-preserving arrow between any two marked realizations. The marked
groupoid is nonempty and equivalent to the terminal groupoid, although its raw object set has many
elements.
This is a worked two-view model of the marked transport mechanism. The generic groupoid construction
and its equivalence theorem are Proposition 14.3 and Theorem 14.4. Formula (15.2) is checked in the
independent finite suite for H = S3, retaining the order of multiplication. This finite check is separate
from the general formal theorem.
15.4 Four meanings of uniqueness
The example separates four statements that are often compressed into the word unique.
Statement
What it retains
One literal object
No different representative is present in the specified object set.
One isomorphism class
Different representatives may remain, as may their automorphisms.
A chosen realization
One object is selected, but no uniqueness follows from the choice.
One compatible comparison
The comparison respecting fixed identifications is unique, even when
many representatives exist.
The unframed duplicated groupoid has one isomorphism class and nontrivial automorphisms. Its
marked relative fiber has unique compatible comparisons. Its fully framed representative groupoid can
instead have many mutually incomparable objects. These are three different constructions, not three
descriptions of the same object with different terminology.
A descent theorem typically uses the last notion: two global realizations of the same specified local
datum are related by a unique comparison respecting that datum. It does not force their underlying
labels to be literally equal, or erase automorphisms after the local identifications have been forgotten.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
51


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Boundary data are not only values. In a symmetry-sensitive problem they include the identification
with those values. Retaining that identification is what allows a relative realization problem to
survive an equivalence of presentations.
16 Coherence need not mean removable phase
The preceding descent theory starts with transition data that already satisfy their composition laws. It is
useful to see, in a completely finite calculation, how those laws can hold while a further simplification
remains impossible. The following model separates coherent composition from a choice of coordinates that
removes every phase.
The coefficient group and the degree of the data matter. The cocycles in Section 13 label individual
arrows in a view category. Here a phase is attached to a pair of group elements. These are different
constructions. The finite example is not obtained by renaming the earlier section obstruction as a general
second cohomology group.
16.1 A two-bit phase law
Write F2 = Z/2Z additively, and let
G = F2
2,
x = (x1, x2),
c(x, y) = x2y1.
All entries and operations in this subsection lie in F2. In particular minus and plus agree, although
retaining a minus sign in the coboundary formula helps explain its origin.
Proposition 16.1 (A normalized associative phase). The phase vanishes when either argument is zero and
satisfies
c(x, y) + c(x + y, z) = c(y, z) + c(x, y + z).
(16.1)
There is no function a : G →F2 such that
c(x, y) = a(x) + a(y) −a(x + y)
for every x, y ∈G.
(16.2)
Proof. Normalization follows from multiplication by zero. Expanding the two sides of (16.1) gives,
respectively,
x2y1 + (x2 + y2)z1,
y2z1 + x2(y1 + z1),
which are equal by distributivity.
Every expression a(x) + a(y) −a(x + y) is symmetric in x, y, because G is abelian. With e1 = (1, 0) and
e2 = (0, 1), however,
c(e1, e2) = 0,
c(e2, e1) = 1.
This contradicts the symmetry required by (16.2).
The proof rules out all sixteen possible scalar functions a, not just linear functions or selected trial gauges.
The source ProjectiveExample.lean proves the normalization, cocycle, asymmetry, and no-scalar-
flattening statements. The matrix interpretation and group construction below are worked derivations of
the finite model, not separate formal declarations.

16.2 The phase is visible in actual matrices
Over R, define

Direct multiplication gives X2 = Z2 = I and ZX = −XZ. Moving Zx2 past Xy1 therefore yields
UxUy = (−1)c(x,y)Ux+y.
(16.3)
For example Ue1Ue2 = XZ, while Ue2Ue1 = −XZ. The two-bit labels add commutatively, but their chosen
matrix lifts do not commute.
Associativity of three lifted matrices requires the same total sign on both parenthesizations. Equa-
tion (16.1) is exactly that requirement:
(UxUy)Uz = (−1)c(x,y)+c(x+y,z)Ux+y+z,
Ux(UyUz) = (−1)c(y,z)+c(x,y+z)Ux+y+z.
Thus a nonzero phase is not automatically a failed associativity law. It can be the data that make the
composition law explicit.
Changing lifts to U′
x = (−1)a(x)Ux changes the phase to
c′(x, y) = c(x, y) + a(x) + a(y) −a(x + y).
No such change makes c′ vanish identically. The antisymmetric part is unchanged by every scalar change
of lift. In this model it is
κ(x, y) = c(x, y) −c(y, x) = x2y1 −y2x1.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
53


Komorebi Space
Monograph
This is a directly observable obstruction certificate for the model. Its nonzero value at (e1, e2) is enough,
without a search over gauges.
The same symmetry argument rules out removing this sign multiplier by arbitrary nonzero complex
scalar lifts as well. A multiplicative scalar coboundary b(x)b(y)/b(x + y) on an abelian group is
symmetric, whereas the multiplier in (16.3) is not. This last sentence is an algebraic consequence written
here. The retained Lean example uses the stated F2-valued formulation.
16.3 Retaining the phase restores an ordinary multiplication law
Instead of trying to discard the phase, include it in the state. On the eight-element set eG = F2 × G, put
(a, x) · (b, y) = (a + b + c(x, y), x + y).
(16.4)
The first coordinate of the left-associated product of three elements is a + b + d + c(x, y) + c(x + y, z).
The right-associated coordinate is a + b + d + c(y, z) + c(x, y + z). Equation (16.1) proves equality.
Normalization gives identity (0, 0), and
(a, x)−1 = (a + c(x, x), x).
Both inverse products are the identity because x + x = 0 and arithmetic is modulo two. Hence (16.4)
defines a group.
Projection (a, x) 7→x is a homomorphism onto G. Its kernel is {(0, 0), (1, 0)}, which commutes with
every element. The map
(a, x) 7−→(−1)aUx
is a homomorphism by (16.3). Its eight matrices are distinct: they are ±I, ±X, ±Z, ±XZ. It is therefore
faithful. The phase extension is not an assertion that G itself has acquired an ordinary representation
with these same lifts. The represented group has changed.
A precise form of retaining the obstruction. The original four labels do not support a phase-
free multiplication of the chosen matrices. Keeping one additional bit gives an eight-element
multiplication that does. This repairs the description by retaining information, not by proving that
the original phase was a coboundary.
16.4 How this example fits the realization calculus
There are three different questions in this small model. First, the phase satisfies the associativity
relation. Second, scalar changes cannot trivialize it. Third, an enlarged state that retains the phase
admits an ordinary multiplication law. Each question has a complete answer, but their answers are not
interchangeable.
The example illustrates why an obstruction should be attached to the allowed changes and to a specified
presentation problem. Enlarging the presentation can be mathematically productive, but it changes what
is being realized and what information is carried. No general theorem that every obstruction can be
removed by a finite enlargement is claimed. The finite checker enumerates all group triples, all scalar
gauges, and all multiplication triples in eG, independently of the symmetry proof above.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
54


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Part IV
The geometry of exact realization
A categorical space of realizations does not come with a numerical distance by default.
In a declared linear model, the compatibility equations become a bounded operator.
Closed range, holonomy, and conditioning then determine what small residuals actually
guarantee.
17 Graph views, holonomy, and reconstruction
17.1 Actual transports on a finite graph
Let a finite connected simple graph have vertex set V, root o, and a chosen route pi : o →i for every
vertex, with the route at o empty. Use a common real normed fiber H. An inverse consistent connection
assigns continuous linear equivalences τij, with τji = τ−1
ij . Our convention is that τij carries the value at j
into the coordinates at i.
The candidate and residual spaces are finite products with maximum norms:
E = ∏
i∈V
H,
R = ∏
(i,j)∈⃗E
H,
(dx)ij = τijxj −xi.
Both orientations of an edge are retained. If M bounds the norms of all measured edge maps, then
∥d∥≤M + 1. The empty edge case has the usual zero residual.
Distinct walks are retained as arrows of the view category. The product along a walk q = (i0, . . . , im) is
Tq = τi0i1 · · · τim−1im.
Concatenation gives the restriction composition law. An exact vertex family defines a coherent walk-view
section, and a coherent walk-view section satisfies every edge equation. Thus this graph construction
returns to the original ambient realization type rather than creating a disconnected numerical model.

These bounds also hold on the subspaces K and ker d, proving continuity of both directions. No

For route independence, the proved equivalence identifies K with the image of root evaluation from the

uniqueness makes the families equal. For z /∈K, this uniqueness argument does not assert equality of
the two generally inconsistent reconstructed families.
With isometric transports the equivalence is an isometry for the vertex maximum norm, because every
reconstructed coordinate has the root norm. With general bounded equivalences, it need not be an

18 Closed range is the stability boundary
18.1 Projection exists before a residual estimate
Let E be a real Hilbert space and R a real Banach space. Let D : E →R be continuous linear. The kernel
K = ker D is closed, so its orthogonal projection q = PK exists [4].
Proposition 18.1 (Exact orthogonal repair). For every x ∈E,
D(qx) = 0,
q2 = q,
∥qx∥≤∥x∥,
and qx is the unique closest point to x in K.
Proof. Boundedness of D makes K = ker D closed. Lemma E.1 constructs q = PK and gives the
orthogonal decomposition x = qx + (x −qx). The first term belongs to K, so Dqx = 0. Projection fixes
every point of K, giving q(qx) = qx.
Pythagoras applied to that decomposition gives ∥x∥2 = ∥qx∥2 + ∥x −qx∥2, hence ∥qx∥≤∥x∥. For any
y ∈K, the vector qx −y lies in K, whereas x −qx lies in its orthogonal complement. Consequently
∥x −y∥2 = ∥x −qx∥2 + ∥qx −y∥2 ≥∥x −qx∥2 .
Equality holds only when ∥qx −y∥= 0, or y = qx. This proves nearest-point uniqueness without any
closedness assumption on ran D. Closedness of the kernel and of the range serve different purposes.
No closed range assumption on D is needed for this projection. Such an assumption enters when the
size of the residual is asked to control the distance to the exact space.
Theorem 18.2 (Closed range and quantitative exactness). The following are equivalent:
1. ran D is closed.
2. There is C ≥0 with ∥x −qx∥≤C ∥Dx∥for all x.
3. There is C ≥0 such that every x has some exact y with ∥x −y∥≤C ∥Dx∥.
When the range is closed, the least admissible constant is the operator norm of the inverse of D : K⊥→ran D. In
the zero operator case that inverse has zero dimensional domain and its norm is zero.
Proof. Let K = ker D and K⊥carry the induced Hilbert norm. Define D⊥u = Du, but regard its target
as ran D with the norm inherited from R. It is injective: Du = 0 puts u in both K and K⊥, so u = 0. It
is surjective onto that target: if r = Dx, then u = x −qx ∈K⊥and Du = Dx = r. Thus it is a bounded
linear bijection before any continuity of its inverse is claimed.
Suppose first that the range is closed. Because R is Banach, ran D is then Banach. The domain K⊥is also
complete. The bounded inverse theorem, proved in Appendix E, gives a bounded inverse A = D−1
⊥.
Applying it to Dx = D(x −qx) yields
x −qx = A(Dx),
∥x −qx∥≤∥A∥∥Dx∥.
Hence condition 2 holds. If any C ≥0 satisfies condition 2, then for every r ∈ran D apply it to
u = Ar ∈K⊥. Since qu = 0, it gives ∥Ar∥≤C ∥r∥. Taking the operator norm proves ∥A∥≤C. This
establishes the least constant, not just a sufficient one. If D = 0, both K⊥and the range are zero spaces
and ∥A∥= 0.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
58


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Now assume condition 2. To prove closedness, take an arbitrary sequence rn ∈ran D with rn →r in R.
Choose xn with Dxn = rn and put un = xn −qxn. Then un ∈K⊥and Dun = rn. Applying the estimate
to un −um gives
∥un −um∥≤C ∥rn −rm∥.
Thus (un) is Cauchy in the complete space K⊥. Let its limit be u. Continuity gives Du = limn rn = r, so
r ∈ran D. In a normed space, a set containing the limits of all its convergent sequences is closed. This
proves condition 1 without presupposing that the initially unbounded inverse is continuous.
Condition 2 implies condition 3 by choosing y = qx. Conversely, if condition 3 supplies an exact y for a
given x, the nearest-point property of qx gives
∥x −qx∥≤∥x −y∥≤C ∥Dx∥.
The same C works for all x, proving condition 2. This last implication does not require the selected
answers y to depend linearly or continuously on x.
The last equivalence is important. A nonlinear choice of exact answers cannot bypass the failure of
a uniform residual bound. Failure of closed range is not merely a defect of one selected projection
algorithm.
18.2 A small residual far from exactness
On ℓ2(N), index the standard basis from one and define Den = n−1en. The map is bounded and injective,
so its exact space is zero. Yet
∥en∥= 1,
∥Den∥= 1/n −→0.
No finite C can bound dist(en, ker D) by C ∥Den∥. The range is not closed. For example finite truncations
of (1/n)n≥1 belong to the range and converge in ℓ2, but a preimage of the limit would be the constant
sequence, which does not belong to ℓ2.
This diagonal model is formalized on the infinite Hilbert space used in Section 30. In the namespace
KomorebiSpace.DiagonalRegularization, T_not_closed_range applies the general obstruction
theorem to actual unit coordinate vectors. The declaration w_mem_closure_not_range proves that
the displayed limiting datum belongs to the closure of the range but not to the range itself. Formal
coordinate j represents the printed coordinate n = j + 1.
18.3 Norm zero versus algebraic zero
If an additive correction image is not closed, distance to that image can vanish without actual membership.
Thus an infimum of residual error can be zero while the exact protected correction is unattained. The
algebraic obstruction quotient and its possible seminorm answer different questions. One must identify
a closed image, compactness condition, or other attainment principle before replacing one question by
the other.
18.4 Stable repair with protected data
The preceding orthogonal projection does not generally preserve a separately declared observable. To
join the analytic theory to Section 4, let p : E →S be continuous linear into a normed space. Define
M = ker p,
B = D|M : M →R,
H = M ∩ker D.

The closed subspace M inherits its Hilbert structure from E. A candidate x is feasible for protected repair

Theorem 18.3 (Nearest feasible protected repair). Every feasible x has a unique closest exact answer y∗
preserving p(x). Its correction h∗= x −y∗is the unique vector in the orthogonal complement of ker B inside M
satisfying Bh∗= Dx. For every other feasible answer y,
∥x −y∥2 = ∥x −y∗∥2 + ∥y∗−y∥2 .
Moreover, the following conditions are equivalent:
ran B is closed
⇐⇒
∃C ≥0 ∀feasible x : ∥x −y∗∥≤C ∥Dx∥.
When these conditions hold, the least nonnegative constant is the transverse inverse norm of B, including the
zero-range case.
Proof. Feasibility gives some h0 = x −y0 ∈M with Bh0 = Dx. Conversely, every h ∈M satisfying
Bh = Dx produces an exact protected answer y = x −h. Thus the answer problem and the correction
problem are equivalent, with correction set
{h ∈M : Bh = Dx} = h0 + ker B.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
60


Komorebi Space
Monograph
This is a nonempty closed affine subset of the Hilbert space M. Closedness of ker B follows from
continuity of B, independently of whether its image is closed.
Let k0 = PM
ker Bh0 and h∗= h0 −k0. Then Bh∗= Bh0 = Dx and h∗⊥ker B in M. If a different initial
correction is chosen, it differs from h0 by an element of ker B. Its orthogonal-complement component is
therefore again h∗. This proves that the construction is independent of the feasibility witness.
Every other correction can be written uniquely as h∗+ k with k ∈ker B. Orthogonality gives
∥h∗+ k∥2 = ∥h∗∥2 + ∥k∥2 .
Writing y∗= x −h∗and y = x −h∗−k, we have x −y = h∗+ k and y∗−y = k. Substitution proves the
stated Pythagorean identity. Its last term vanishes only when y = y∗, establishing the unique closest
answer.
If ran B is closed, Theorem 18.2 applied on M gives a bounded inverse of B on (ker B)⊥M. The vector h∗
is exactly that inverse applied to Dx, so its norm obeys the required bound.
For the converse, suppose the bound holds for every feasible raw candidate. Every x ∈M is feasible,
because y = 0 has Dy = 0 and py = px = 0. Its closest protected exact answer is PM
ker Bx. Thus the
assumed estimate reduces on M to
x −PM
ker Bx
 ≤C ∥Bx∥
(x ∈M).
Theorem 18.2 forces ran B to be closed. The same restriction also proves that no smaller nonnegative
constant than the transverse inverse norm can work. If the range is zero, feasible candidates are already
exact under permitted correction and the least correction is zero, so the optimal gain is zero.
The relevant stability boundary is therefore D(ker p), not automatically D(E). Restricting permitted
changes can turn a well-conditioned unconstrained problem into an obstructed or poorly conditioned
protected one. The nearest protected answer is generally not Pker Dx.
For D(x1, x2, x3) = x1 −x2, p(x) = (x1, x3), and x = (2, 0, 5), the ordinary orthogonal answer is
(1, 1, 5). The protected answer is (2, 2, 5). The respective squared errors are two and four. Both
have zero residual, but only the latter preserves the declared data. The theorem is formalized in
ProtectedHilbertRepair.lean, using the actual operator on ker p. The displayed three-coordinate
values are an illustrative calculation, not a separate universal algorithm.

20 Quantitative descent in a common Hilbert model
20.1 Local exactness and matching are separate operators
Let E be a real Hilbert space of raw local candidates. Let D : E →R and C : E →Y be continuous linear
maps into Banach spaces, measuring local equation defects and overlap mismatch, respectively. Put
L = ker D,
B = C|L,
M = ker D ∩ker C.
The exact matched families form M. First project to L, then project inside L to ker B. Denote the resulting
map by P.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
64


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Theorem 20.1 (The two-stage repair is the joint projection). The operator P is the orthogonal projection onto
M, equivalently onto the kernel of ∆= (D, C). If u = x −PLx and v = PLx −Px, then
u ⊥v,
∥x −Px∥2 = ∥u∥2 + ∥v∥2 .
Proof. The subspace L = ker D is closed, hence Hilbert with the inherited inner product. The restriction
B = C|L is continuous, so its kernel is a closed subspace of L. Under the inclusion into E, this kernel is
exactly M = L ∩ker C. Thus both projections used to define P exist.
For an input x, write ℓ= PLx and let Px be the projection of ℓonto ker B inside L. Then Px ∈M,
u = x −ℓ∈L⊥, and v = ℓ−Px ∈L is orthogonal to M. Since M ⊆L, the vector u is also orthogonal to
M. Their sum x −Px = u + v is therefore orthogonal to M. A vector in M with that error is the unique
orthogonal projection of x, so P = PM.
The two errors themselves are orthogonal because u ∈L⊥and v ∈L. Expanding the squared norm of
their sum gives ∥x −Px∥2 = ∥u∥2 + ∥v∥2. Finally ker(D, C) = ker D ∩ker C = M, so this is also the
projection onto the kernel of the joint defect. The proof uses a second projection inside L. Replacing it by
the ambient projection onto ker C would invalidate the membership step and need not preserve local
exactness.
This identity does not require the local and overlap operators to commute. The second projection is
taken in the already exact subspace, not in the whole raw space without restriction.
For a concrete failure of the unrestricted procedure, take L = {(a, 0)} and ker C = {(a, a)} in R2. Their
intersection is zero. Starting from (1, 0), projection to L does nothing, while subsequent projection to
ker C gives (1/2, 1/2), which is no longer in L. The restricted second stage instead gives the correct joint
answer zero.
20.2 A stable repair requires a stable matching problem
Suppose ran D and ran B are closed, with residual gains a, b. Then
∥u∥≤a ∥Dx∥,
∥v∥≤b ∥CPLx∥≤b(∥Cx∥+ ∥C∥a ∥Dx∥).
Therefore
∥x −Px∥≤a(1 + b ∥C∥) ∥Dx∥+ b ∥Cx∥.
(20.1)
The Pythagorean identity gives the sharper estimate
∥x −Px∥2 ≤a2 ∥Dx∥2 + b2 ∥Cx∥+ a ∥C∥∥Dx∥
2.
For the maximum norm on R × Y, two sufficient gains are therefore
G2 =
q
a2 + b2(1 + a ∥C∥)2 ≤G1 = a(1 + b ∥C∥) + b.
The inequality follows from
√
u2 + v2 ≤u + v for nonnegative u, v. The formal interface supplies the
sum-type gain G1, while its squared-error theorem also yields G2. Neither is claimed to be the least joint
gain. In either bound, ∥C∥may be replaced by any certified bound on C restricted to (ker D)⊥.
Theorem 20.2 (Closed range for the joint defect). Assume ran D is closed. Then
ran(D, C) is closed
⇐⇒
ran(C|ker D) is closed.
Under either condition, a finite joint residual-to-repair bound exists.

Figure 18: Local correction and overlap correction are orthogonal errors when the second problem is restricted to

Proof. Suppose the range of the joint map is closed. To test closedness of ran B, take a sequence Bkn →y
with kn ∈ker D. The joint images are
(Dkn, Ckn) = (0, Bkn) −→(0, y).
Closedness supplies x ∈E with (Dx, Cx) = (0, y). The first coordinate gives x ∈ker D, and the second
gives Bx = y. Hence the matching range is closed. This direction did not require closedness of ran D.
For the reverse direction, assume both ran D and ran B are closed, and take joint images converging to
(r, y). Thus Dxn →r and Cxn →y. Decompose each input orthogonally as
xn = un + kn,
un ∈(ker D)⊥,
kn ∈ker D.
Because r ∈ran D and the transverse inverse A of D is bounded by Theorem 18.2, the first components
determine
un = A(Dxn) −→A(r) = u,
Du = r.
Continuity of C now gives
Bkn = Ckn = Cxn −Cun −→y −Cu.
Closedness of ran B supplies k ∈ker D with Bk = y −Cu. There is no need for the sequence kn itself to
converge. The single vector u + k satisfies
D(u + k) = r,
C(u + k) = Cu + Bk = y.

Komorebi Space
Monograph
Thus the joint limit lies in the joint range, proving closedness. The target product is Banach under its
maximum norm, so Theorem 18.2 applies to the joint map and gives a finite repair gain. The earlier
two-stage estimate supplies an explicit, though not necessarily least, value.
This Hilbert model is a quantitative counterpart of gluing, but it is not a norm on an arbitrary categorical
stack. It starts from a fixed raw candidate space, fixed norms, and actual local and overlap operators.
Changing those choices requires its own comparison.
21 Controlled motion of exact spaces
21.1 A two-sided condition number theorem
Let D, D′ : E →R be continuous linear maps from a real Hilbert space to the same normed target. Write
q = Pker D, q′ = Pker D′. Suppose
∥(I −q)x∥≤C ∥Dx∥,

 ≤C′ D′x

C, C′ ≥0,
and ∥D −D′∥≤ε.
Theorem 21.1 (Motion of the exact projector). Without either kernel inclusion as a hypothesis,
q −q′ ≤max(C, C′)ε.
(21.1)
In particular a uniform residual gain M makes D 7→Pker D M-Lipschitz on the class of operators carrying that
gain.
Proof. For z ∈ker D′, the old residual bound gives
∥(I −q)z∥≤C ∥Dz∥= C
(D −D′)z

Interchanging the roles gives

its norm. Surjectivity is still substantive, and cannot be assumed for a redundant residual map whose
target contains unattainable directions.

Proof. Let R0 = I −AD and R1 = I −A′D′. From DA = D′A′ = I we obtain DR0 = 0 and D′R1 = 0.
Each Ri fixes its corresponding kernel. Thus R1 restricts to a map from ker D to ker D′, while R0 restricts

The inverse identities require two additional cancellations. First,

R1A = A −AZD′A = A −AZ(I −T) = 0.

For y ∈ker D′, they give
R1R0y = R1(y −ADy) = R1y = y.
Both restricted maps are bounded and linear as restrictions of bounded linear operators. They are
therefore inverse continuous linear maps, establishing the asserted equivalence.

21.5 What fails without the hypotheses
Take D0 = diag(1, 0) and Dt = diag(1, t). The operator difference has norm |t|. For every nonzero t,
however, the new kernel is zero, while the old kernel is the vertical axis. Their projections differ in norm
by one. Operator closeness alone does not imply kernel continuity.
More generally, suppose a unit vector x is exact for the original operator but orthogonal to the new
kernel. Every new residual gain C′ obeys
1 =
x −q′x
 ≤C′ D′x
 ≤C′ε.
Losing an exact direction under a small operator change forces the new gain to grow. At the strict radius
boundary, D = I, A = I, and D′ = 0 have a = ε = 1, yet D′ is not surjective. Thus the strict condition in
Theorem 21.2 cannot be replaced universally by a non-strict one.

22 Reading a stability certificate
A numerical residual becomes useful only after it is tied to an operator, a norm, and a conclusion. This
section separates uncertainty in a measured residual from uncertainty in the operator itself. It also
distinguishes closeness to a reference exact space from closeness to a changed one. All estimates below
refer to the actual canonical Hilbert projection used earlier.
22.1 The statement carried by a certificate
Let D : E →R be bounded linear from a real Hilbert space into a real normed space. A residual certificate
with gain C ≥0 is the universally quantified estimate
∥x −qx∥≤C ∥Dx∥
for every x ∈E,
q = Pker D.
A reported number C is not a certificate unless this estimate has been justified in the stated model. A
successful test at one input is likewise not the universally quantified estimate.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
72


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Suppose the measured vector is y, with a certified error radius η such that ∥Dx −y∥≤η. The triangle
inequality converts the measured ball into a bound for the true residual.
Proposition 22.1 (Measurement and operator uncertainty). With the reference certificate above,
∥x −qx∥≤C(∥y∥+ η)
if ∥Dx −y∥≤η.
More generally, let eD : E →R be the bounded linear operator actually used in the measurement. If
D −eD
 ≤ε,
 eDx −y
 ≤η,
then
∥x −qx∥≤C(∥y∥+ η + ε ∥x∥).
(22.1)
The projection in this conclusion is still the projection for the reference operator D.
Proof. The first assertion follows from ∥Dx∥≤∥y∥+ η. For the second, decompose
Dx = (D −eD)x + ( eDx −y) + y.
Its norm is at most ε ∥x∥+ η + ∥y∥. Apply the reference residual certificate.
The input-size term has a mathematical purpose. A small error in an operator can produce a large error
on a large input. It cannot generally be replaced by a number depending only on the measured residual.
Even a measurement of zero may reflect a difference between the measured kernel and the reference
kernel.
22.2 A vanishing measured residual can still miss the reference space
For a concrete family, take E = R2 with the Euclidean norm and R = R, and define
D(a, b) = a,
eD(a, b) = a + tb.
The reference projection is q(a, b) = (0, b), its least residual gain is one, and
D −eD
 ≤|t|. On the
input x = (−tB, B), the measured operator returns exactly zero. Its distance to the reference kernel is
nevertheless |tB|.
Setting η = 0 and y = 0 in (22.1) gives the valid upper bound |t|
√
1 + t2|B|. Deleting the input-size term
would give zero and would be false whenever tB ̸= 0. The input is exact for the changed model and not
exact for the reference model. This is precisely the distinction that the estimate is designed to retain.
There is no statistical assertion here. The error radii are mathematical inputs. Estimating them from
noisy observations, unknown dynamics, or a fitted model is an additional task that requires its own
assumptions and evidence.
22.3 A preserved kernel gives a stronger conclusion
A useful regime occurs when the changed operator annihilates every old exact vector:
eDq = 0.
This says that the old kernel is contained in the new kernel. Equality is not assumed. Let

 ≤ε
and suppose the strict budget Cε < 1 holds.

Thus the exact space is unchanged and the residual gain degrades by an explicit factor.
Proof. Let q = Pker D, and write u = x −qx. The stated kernel inclusion makes both Dqx and eDqx zero.
Hence Du = Dx and eDu = eDx. Applying the reference residual bound to u, whose projection onto

If eDx = 0, the same estimate forces u = 0, hence x = qx ∈ker D. This is the inclusion not initially

before applying the perturbation estimate. Without it, one obtains a term proportional to ∥x∥, not the
absorbable term proportional to ∥u∥.
This result does not require the reference operator to be surjective. It is different from the split-surjective
perturbation theorem, which allows the kernel to move and constructs an actual transport between the
old and new kernels. Both theorems have a strict quantitative radius, but they retain different structural
data.
For D(a, b) = a and eD(a, b) = (1 −t)a, with 0 ≤t < 1, the kernel is preserved and the exact gain is
1/(1 −t). The bound is attained on transverse inputs. At t = 1, the changed operator is zero and its
kernel becomes all of R2. This illustrates why the strict endpoint cannot simply be included.

A claim about the new exact space cannot be obtained by silently replacing the reference projector in
the first row. Conversely, a theorem that establishes equality of kernels under preservation need not be
weakened to a moving-kernel estimate when the stronger hypothesis is actually available.
22.5 Norm conventions are part of the certificate
On a finite family of real Hilbert fibers, the maximum norm and the Euclidean product norm satisfy
∥x∥∞≤∥x∥2 ≤√
n ∥x∥∞,
where n is the number of components. The first inequality follows because each squared component
is one term in the sum. The second follows by bounding every component by the maximum. These
inequalities transfer bounds with explicit dimension factors. They do not assert that the same numerical
best constant remains optimal.
The graph chapters use the maximum norm on vertex and directed-edge products unless a different
geometry is declared. The nearest-point statements use a Hilbert source norm. In the worked graph
calculations that follow, selecting one orientation and using a sum of squared residuals defines a different
numerical operator model. It has the same edge-exactness equations for an isometric connection, but a
different residual norm.
A repair certificate should therefore identify its candidate space, residual map, preserved data, norms,
gain, and error budgets. The exact equation alone does not specify these quantities. Explicitly retaining
them is what makes a numerical conclusion transportable between examples.

75


Komorebi Space

23 A geometry of transformations needs its own cost
The preceding analytic chapters measure the size of a residual or the distance from a candidate to an
exact subspace. Neither measurement automatically defines a distance between mathematical objects. In
an additive category, the zero map is often perfectly compatible and perfectly uninformative. A geometry
that assigns zero cost to every compatible map can therefore identify all objects at distance zero.
A cost of transformation must be declared separately. It may measure an admissible computational
expense, a loss of retained information, or another specified quantity. The interpretation alone proves
none of its mathematical laws. The construction below begins only after the required laws have been
established.
23.1 Extended directed distance
Let C be a category and let W assign each arrow a value in the extended nonnegative reals [0, ∞]. Suppose
W(idA) = 0,
W(g f ) ≤W( f ) + W(g).
The order of ordinary composition is first f, then g. Define
dW(A, B) =
inf
f :A→B W( f ).
The infimum of an empty family is ∞. No assumption of connectedness, finite cost, symmetry, or
attainment is made.
Proposition 23.1 (Directed cost geometry). For every arrow f : A →B,
dW(A, B) ≤W( f ),
dW(A, A) = 0,
and for all objects,
dW(A, C) ≤dW(A, B) + dW(B, C).
The symmetric function
dsym
W (A, B) = max{dW(A, B), dW(B, A)}
has zero diagonal, is symmetric, and satisfies the extended triangle inequality. The relation dsym
W (A, B) = 0 is an
equivalence relation.
Proof. The infimum is bounded above by each of its entries. Apply this to the identity to obtain the
zero diagonal. If either term on the right of the triangle inequality is infinite, there is nothing to prove.
Otherwise, for every ε > 0, choose arrows with costs less than the corresponding infima plus ε. Their
composite has cost less than dW(A, B) + dW(B, C) + 2ε. Letting ε tend to zero gives the result. This
argument does not require an optimizing arrow.
Each of the two directed distances from A to C and from C to A is bounded by dsym
W (A, B) + dsym
W (B, C).
Taking their maximum proves the symmetric triangle law. Zero diagonal and symmetry give reflexivity
and symmetry of the zero-distance relation. The triangle inequality and nonnegativity give transitivity.
The formal source proves these laws directly in R∞
≥0 and constructs the zero-distance setoid. It does not
identify zero distance with categorical isomorphism. Passing to the quotient can separate zero-distance
classes, but would still not prove that those classes are the intended semantic objects.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
76


Komorebi Space
Monograph
23.2 A concrete path category
Consider four vertices A, B, C, D, and the free path category generated by
A

Concatenation is additive and empty paths cost zero, so all cost axioms hold. The directed distances are
dW

Figure 22: Directed path costs need not be symmetric or finite. Vertices A and B have zero cost in both directions,
but they are not isomorphic in the free path category. The distinct zero-cost arrows have no imposed inverse
relations.
The symmetric zero-distance classes are {A, B}, {C}, and {D}. The distance between the first two classes
is five. The third class is infinitely far from each of them. Yet A and B are not isomorphic in this category.
Any path from one to the other has positive length, so concatenating it with a return path cannot give
the length-zero identity. This is a categorical distinction even though the numerical cost is zero.
One can instead impose relations declaring the two arrows inverses. That changes the category. The
distance table alone does not record whether those relations were imposed.

77


Komorebi Space
Monograph
23.3 Why compatibility alone can collapse the geometry
Proposition 23.2 (The zero-arrow collapse). Suppose C has zero morphisms and W(0 : A →B) = 0 for every
pair of objects. Then
dW(A, B) = 0
for all A, B.
Proof. The zero arrow is one member of the family over which the infimum is taken. Thus 0 ≤
dW(A, B) ≤W(0) = 0.
For the preadditive tower differential, the zero family of maps is exact. Consequently any proposed
nonnegative arrow cost that vanishes on every exact map also vanishes on every zero map. If it satisfies
the cost axioms at all, its object distance collapses by the proposition.
The qualification is important. A raw operator defect norm need not satisfy the unweighted composition
inequality required of W. The transport Leibniz identity gives coefficients involving the surrounding
maps. It is not legitimate to first call an arbitrary defect norm a subadditive cost and then use the metric
construction.
23.4 Preservation changes the admissible arrows
One response to collapse is to restrict arrows to those satisfying a declared preservation contract. For
objects equipped with maps pA : A →S, a morphism can be required to satisfy
pB f = pA.
Identities and composition preserve this equation. Unless pA = 0, the zero arrow is not admissible. In
other applications the arrows might be required to be isomorphisms or to retain a particular observable.
These are different choices of category, not automatic consequences of small residuals.
Even after this restriction, separation remains a theorem to prove rather than a consequence of termi-
nology. Distinct objects can still be connected by arbitrarily cheap preserving maps. Symmetry can
fail, and the infimum can fail to be attained. A quantitative comparison therefore needs four pieces of
information: the objects, the admissible arrows, the actual cost, and the meaning of zero distance.
The role of this construction is modest but useful. It prevents a compatibility measure from being silently
promoted to a universal geometry. It also gives a valid directed geometry when the separate categorical
and cost assumptions really hold.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
78


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Part V
From approximation to exact realization
A vanishing defect is a statement about measurements. A limiting realization also
requires control of the candidate itself. This part follows the movement of approximate
answers, the closure of admissible correction images, and the meaning of the ambient
completion.
24 Exactification without a fixed contraction rate
The constructed linear repair in Section 8 is a useful special case of a more general principle. One can
prove convergence without one fixed contraction factor, without a linear update rule, and without a
globally Lipschitz iteration. What must not be lost is control of the total movement.
24.1 The two limits that must be established
Let (M, dist) be a complete metric space. Let ω : M →[0, ∞) be a nonnegative residual function, and
call a point exact when ω(x) = 0. We regard ω as a real-valued function when discussing continuity.
Assume it is lower semicontinuous, so that every sublevel set {x : ω(x) ≤a} is closed.
For an approximation sequence fn, the statements
ω( fn) −→0
and
fn −→x
are different. The first concerns the observed discrepancy. The second concerns the mathematical
candidate in the chosen metric. Neither implies the other without additional hypotheses.
Theorem 24.1 (Summable movement gives exactification). Suppose bn ≥0, ∑∞
n=0 bn < ∞, and
dist( fn, fn+1) ≤bn,
ω( fn) −→0.
Then there is a limit x ∈M satisfying
fn −→x,
ω(x) = 0,
dist( fn, x) ≤
∞
∑
k=n
bk.
Proof. For integers m > n, apply the triangle inequality successively along the finite chain fn, fn+1, . . . , fm.
It gives
dist( fn, fm) ≤
m−1

Given ε > 0, convergence of the nonnegative series supplies N for which the final tail is below ε whenever
n ≥N. Interchanging m, n when necessary proves the Cauchy condition for all pairs of indices at least
N. Completeness, not residual decay, is the hypothesis that now supplies a limit x ∈M.
Keep n fixed. The reverse triangle inequality says
dist( fn, fm) −dist( fn, x)

Komorebi Space
Monograph
Passing to the limit in the uniform tail bound proves the claimed distance certificate at that n.
It remains to prove exactness of the limit. Fix ε > 0. Since ω( fm) →0, an entire tail of the sequence
belongs to the sublevel set Cε = {z : ω(z) ≤ε}. Lower semicontinuity makes this set closed, so the
limit of that tail belongs to it. Hence ω(x) ≤ε for every positive ε. If ω(x) > 0, choosing ε = ω(x)/2
contradicts this inequality. Since ω is nonnegative, ω(x) = 0. We have not used, or asserted, continuity
of ω or the stronger equality ω( fm) →ω(x) for arbitrary convergent inputs.
The formal theorem is slightly more general: it is stated in a complete pseudometric space. In a metric
space the limit of this fixed sequence is unique. That does not mean different admissible approximation
procedures select the same exact point.
24.2 Variable rates and a movement budget
Let qn, Cn ≥0, and define the prefix product
Q0 = 1,
Qn+1 = qnQn.
Assume
ω( fn+1) ≤qnω( fn),
dist( fn, fn+1) ≤Cnω( fn).
(24.1)
Theorem 24.2 (Variable-rate exactification). If Qn →0 and ∑n CnQn < ∞, then ( fn) has an exact limit and
dist( fn, x) ≤ω( f0)
∞
∑
k=n
CkQk.
(24.2)
For constant qn = q ∈[0, 1) and constant Cn = C, the sharper restarted estimate is
dist( fn, x) ≤
C
1 −q ω( fn).
Proof. The estimate ω( fn) ≤Qnω( f0) follows by induction. At n = 0 it is equality. If it holds at n,
nonnegativity of qn permits multiplication and gives
ω( fn+1) ≤qnω( fn) ≤qnQnω( f0) = Qn+1ω( f0).
Because Qn →0 and ω( f0) is a fixed finite number, the residual tends to zero. The movement assumption
then gives
dist( fn, fn+1) ≤CnQnω( f0).
This is a summable nonnegative budget by the hypothesis on ∑CnQn. All assumptions of Theorem 24.1
now hold, and its tail certificate is exactly (24.2). If ω( f0) = 0, the same estimates make every step zero
and every residual zero. No cancellation of that value was used.
For constant rates, the global estimate can be sharpened by restarting at the current index. Fix n. A new
induction on j gives
ω( fn+j) ≤qjω( fn),
dist( fn+j, fn+j+1) ≤Cqjω( fn).
Sum the finite chain from n to n + m, and then let m →∞using the already established limit. The
geometric series equals 1/(1 −q), including q = 0, where its first term is one and all later terms vanish.
This proves the restarted bound. The variable-rate proof itself requires only nonnegative qn, decay of
their cumulative products, and summability of the movement weights. It does not insert an unstated
upper bound qn < 1 at each step.

80

Monograph
The individual factors qn need not all be below one. Temporary residual increases are permitted if the
prefix products decay and the weighted movements remain summable. Conversely, a decaying product
is not enough when its movement budget diverges.

24.5 Protected meaning survives a controlled limit
Return to Banach and normed linear spaces, with continuous linear maps d : E →R and p : E →S. Put
ω( f ) = ∥d f ∥. The residual is continuous, hence lower semicontinuous.
Proposition 24.3 (Controlled paths kill the actual obstruction). Suppose E is complete, ∥d fn∥→0, p( fn) =
p( f0), and dist( fn, fn+1) ≤bn for a summable nonnegative budget. Then there is x with
fn →x,
dx = 0,
p(x) = p( f0),
and the constrained obstruction [d f0] ∈R/d(ker p) vanishes.
Proof. The function f 7→∥d f ∥is continuous because d is bounded linear and the norm is continuous. It
is therefore an admissible lower semicontinuous residual for Theorem 24.1. That theorem supplies a
limit x ∈E, together with ∥dx∥= 0, which means dx = 0.
Continuity of p gives p( fn) →p(x). The left side is the constant sequence with value p( f0). Uniqueness
of limits in the normed target therefore gives p(x) = p( f0). Define the actual vector a = f0 −x. Linearity,
OPERATION KOMOREBI
82


Komorebi Space
Monograph
rather than a closure argument, now yields
p(a) = p( f0) −p(x) = 0,
da = d f0 −dx = d f0.
Thus a ∈ker p and d f0 ∈d(ker p). Membership in this algebraic image is precisely what makes the
class [d f0] zero. Merely knowing membership in the closure of that image would not justify the same
conclusion.
This proposition converts a controlled approximation argument into a statement about the algebraic
quotient defined earlier. Merely showing that d fn →0 would not produce the admissible vector f0 −x,
because there might be no candidate limit x.
24.6 When two repair paths select the same point
Proposition 24.4 (Path independence requires a comparison). Let fn →x and gn →y in a metric space. If
dist( fn, gn) ≤βn,
βn →0,
then x = y. In contrast, exactness and preservation of a nonzero observable alone do not force a unique answer.
Proof. For each n, the triangle inequality gives
dist(x, y) ≤dist(x, fn) + dist( fn, gn) + dist(gn, y) ≤dist(x, fn) + βn + dist(gn, y).
Given any ε > 0, choose n large enough that the three terms on the right are each below ε/3. Thus
dist(x, y) ≤ε for every positive ε, so the distance is zero. The metric separation axiom gives x = y. In a
pseudometric space only zero distance would follow without an additional separation hypothesis.
For the negative assertion let E = R × (R × R), d(s, u, r) = r, and p(s, u, r) = s. Both x = (1, 0, 0)
and y = (1, 1, 0) have zero defect and protected value one, but their middle coordinates differ. They
are distinct points of the same feasible fiber. Constant sequences at those points meet exactness and
preservation but not a vanishing comparison bound, so these conditions alone cannot imply path
independence.
A stronger worked example uses actual convergent paths from the same initial input:
f (a)
n
= (s, a(1 −2−n), 2−n)
(a ∈R).
All paths begin at (s, 0, 1), keep p = s, and have residual 2−n. Their maximum-norm movements are
1
2 max{|a|, 1}2−n, so every path satisfies geometric exactification. Their limits are (s, a, 0). For different
a, b,
dist( f (a)

not zero. The missing uniqueness hypothesis is visible in the exact direction along which the paths
separate.

A residual profile ηn indexed by resolution is not an iteration trajectory. Nevertheless a useful conver-

Proposition 24.5 (Cofinal sampling of a decreasing profile). Suppose ηn ≥0 is nonincreasing and ϕ : N →
N is monotone and unbounded. Then
ηn →0
⇐⇒
ηϕ(n) →0.
Without the monotonicity of η, the reverse implication can fail even for a cofinal schedule.
Proof. The schedule tends to infinity, so convergence of the full profile implies convergence of the
sampled one. Conversely, choose a sampled value below any prescribed ε > 0. Every later index of the
original profile has no larger value, by monotonicity. This proves convergence of the full profile.
For failure without monotonicity, take η2n = 0, η2n+1 = 1, and ϕ(n) = 2n. All sampled values vanish,
but infinitely many original values equal one.
Even in the decreasing case, the numerical rate changes. The profile ηn = 1/(n + 1), sampled at
ϕ(n) = 2n −1, becomes 2−n. This is exponential decay in the number of observations, not a proof of a faster
correction process at the original resolution scale.
24.8 Three hypotheses that cannot simply be dropped
Summable movement. In maximum-norm R3, let
fn = (s, Hn, (n + 1)−1),

The first coordinate is protected and the residual is the last coordinate. It tends to zero with the same

OPERATION KOMOREBI

coordinate diverges. Here Cn = 1, so ∑CnQn is the divergent harmonic series. Residual decay and
semantic preservation hold, but exactification of this path fails.
Completeness. In the metric space (0, ∞), take fn = 1/(n + 1) and ω(x) = x. The steps are summable
and the residual tends to zero, but there is no limit in this space. Completing the space adds the missing
zero. It is essential to say whether that completion belongs to the intended model.
Lower semicontinuity. In R, define ω(0) = 1 and ω(x) = 0 for x ̸= 0. The same positive sequence
converges with summable movement and residual zero at every step, yet its limit is not exact. The zero
sublevel set is not closed. This example isolates why convergence of measurements along a sequence
must interact correctly with the ambient topology.

25 The residual floor and the closure of admissible changes
An algebraic obstruction asks whether a residual is exactly the defect of an allowed correction. A
numerical floor asks how closely allowed defects can approximate it. These questions agree under a
closedness hypothesis. Without that hypothesis they can disagree even in a complete Hilbert setting.
25.1 Distance to the actual correction image
Let R be a normed additive group and B ≤R an additive subgroup. In the protected linear model,
B = d(ker p). For a residual Ω∈R, define
νB(Ω) = inf
b∈B ∥Ω−b∥.
The subgroup contains zero, so this is a finite nonnegative real number. It is an infimum, not an assertion
that a nearest admissible residual exists.
Proposition 25.1 (The floor detects closure). For every b ∈B,
0 ≤νB(Ω) ≤∥Ω−b∥.
Moreover,
νB(Ω) = 0
⇐⇒
Ω∈B.
If B is closed, this is equivalent to Ω∈B.
Proof. The set of candidate distances is nonempty because 0 ∈B, bounded below by zero, and contains
the finite value ∥Ω∥. Its infimum is therefore a finite nonnegative real, and the defining lower-bound
property gives νB(Ω) ≤∥Ω−b∥for every b ∈B.
Suppose νB(Ω) = 0 and fix ε > 0. There must be b ∈B with ∥Ω−b∥< ε. Otherwise every candidate
distance would be at least ε, making ε a lower bound and forcing the infimum to be at least ε, a
contradiction. Thus every open ball about Ωmeets B, which is the closure criterion.
Conversely, if Ω∈B, every such ball supplies b with ∥Ω−b∥< ε. The infimum then lies between zero
and ε for every positive ε, so it is zero. If B is closed, membership in its closure is exactly membership
in B. No step asserts that the infimum has a minimizing element when B is not closed, and closedness
alone is being used here only for the zero-floor membership conclusion.
Translation by a member b0 ∈B does not change the floor: replacing b by b −b0 gives the same set of
distances. Thus the floor is well defined on the algebraic quotient R/B. When B is a linear subspace, this
is the usual quotient seminorm. A nonzero algebraic class can have seminorm zero when B is not closed.
The retained source implements the floor on residual representatives and proves the closure criterion.
This quotient interpretation is its elementary translation-invariance calculation, not a new claim that
every obstruction quotient carries a separated norm.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
86


Komorebi Space
Monograph
25.2 A precise three-way distinction in this fixed model
For this specified subgroup and residual there are exactly three possibilities:
Residual location
Algebraic class
Meaning of the floor
Ω∈B
Zero
Exact cancellation exists, and the floor is zero.
Ω∈B \ B
Nonzero
Arbitrarily small corrected residuals exist, but
no exact cancellation exists.
Ω/∈B
Nonzero
The floor is strictly positive and blocks every
allowed correction.
This is a classification for the stated normed-group correction problem, not a universal trichotomy for
every possible Komorebi model. Testing which case holds can itself be difficult. The definitions do not
supply a decision algorithm.
In finite-dimensional real or complex linear models, every linear subspace is closed. The middle row
is therefore absent. It becomes possible in infinite dimensions, or for additive subgroups that are not
closed real-linear subspaces. For example the rational subgroup of R is dense, so the distance from any
irrational real to that subgroup is zero even though its coset is nonzero.
Closedness also should not be confused with a numerical error bound back in the candidate space.
The statement Ω∈B supplies an admissible correction, but not a bound on its size. The closed-range
estimates of Sections 18 and 21, or their protected counterparts, are the additional analytic tools that
control that size in their declared settings.
25.3 A persistent floor blocks every correction sequence
The correction image can vary with the resolution or the admissibility rule. Let Bn ≤R and let Ωn ∈R
be given.
Proposition 25.2 (Uniformly positive floors obstruct asymptotic repair). Suppose c > 0 and eventually
c ≤νBn(Ωn).
For any choices bn ∈Bn, it is impossible that
∥Ωn −bn∥−→0.
Proof. Every allowed correction satisfies ∥Ωn −bn∥≥νBn(Ωn). The corrected norms are therefore
eventually at least c. They cannot converge to zero.
The quantifier covers all admissible sequences, not just the output of one repair routine. On the other
hand, the hypothesis is an eventually uniform positive lower bound. Having a nonzero obstruction class
at each stage is not the same statement. The classes might have zero floors, or their positive floors might
tend to zero.
A finite-dimensional illustration uses R = R2, B = R × {0}, and Ωn = (n, 1). Every correction leaves
the second coordinate equal to one, so the floor is one independently of n. Replacing the residuals
by (n, 1/(n + 1)) gives nonzero algebraic classes at every stage, but their floors tend to zero. The
persistent-floor theorem applies to the first sequence and not the second.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
87


Komorebi Space
Monograph
25.4 The bridge back to exactification
Proposition 24.3 and Proposition 25.1 fit together closely. If corrections produce a norm-convergent,
protected path with vanishing residual, the actual quotient class vanishes. If only the corrected residuals
tend to zero, all that follows is membership in d(ker p).
In particular, when the class is nonzero but its floor is zero, no protected path with vanishing residual
can satisfy all the exactification hypotheses. Something must fail: the path may escape, its movements
may not be summable, the candidate space may not be complete, or its residual and semantic maps may
not have the required continuity. The next model isolates escape while keeping the spaces Hilbert and
both maps continuous.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
88


Komorebi Space
Monograph
26 Exact finite repairs can escape the intended ambient space
This model links the algebraic obstruction, the numerical floor, the movement of approximations, and
the semantic image of an inverse limit. It uses a diagonal operator whose entries are completely explicit.
Every finite problem is solvable. The protected infinite problem is not.

26.4 Escape is not an accident of this particular algorithm
Suppose an arbitrary protected candidate (u, 1) satisfies ∥d(u, 1)∥≤ε. On its first N coordinates,

small residual.
Thus changing the iteration rule cannot fix the obstruction while preserving all the declared requirements.
One must change the input, the preservation contract, or the intended ambient class. The inequality is a
quantitative obstruction to a bounded repair procedure, rather than a failure report from one chosen
recurrence.
26.5 Every finite operator is invertible, but the gains diverge

therefore supplies no uniform gain as N →∞.
In the infinite space, ∥en∥= 1 but ∥Ten∥= 1/n, while ker T = {0}. A uniform estimate dist(u, ker T) ≤
C ∥Tu∥would force 1 ≤C/n for every n, which is impossible. This agrees with the nonclosed range
already established directly. The low residual directions do not reflect new exact vectors. They reflect
increasingly ill-conditioned approximate directions.
26.6 A coherent inverse-limit point still exists in a larger space
At each finite level, the protected exact equations have the unique solution
(−1, . . . , −1; 1) ∈RN ⊕R.
Truncation maps the solution at level N + 1 to the one at level N. These finite solution sets are singletons,
so their inverse limit is also a singleton. Its coordinate description is
(−1, −1, . . . ; 1) ∈RN>0 × R.
The coherent point is not missing. It lies outside the intended ℓ2-based semantic space.
This example does not contradict finite-fiber existence. It distinguishes the ordinary inverse limit of finite
views from the image of a separate comparison map that sends square-summable semantic objects to
their finite prefixes. The prescribed coherent point is not in that image. Replacing ℓ2 by all sequences
makes the equation solvable, but changes the topology and the class of allowed objects.
Four statements, four answers. Every finite prefix is exactly solvable. The ordinary coherent
inverse-limit point exists. Its protected Hilbert realization does not exist. Approximate protected
Hilbert realizations have residuals tending to zero only at the cost of unbounded correction size.
All four facts hold simultaneously in one explicit model.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
92


Komorebi Space
Monograph
27 A bounded repair budget can recover existence
The preceding example does not merely lack a convergent iteration. It has no bounded family of
increasingly accurate protected corrections. This suggests a converse question. If approximate corrections
stay in one bounded region, does an exact answer exist?
For bounded linear equations in a Hilbert space, the answer is yes. This is an existence statement, not a
claim that the given sequence converges in norm. A stronger conclusion becomes available when the
candidates are the least norm solutions of nested finite constraint systems.
The two bounded-realization theorems in this chapter have formal counterparts in the accompany-
ing Lean project. The printed proof of bounded approximate feasibility uses the weak-subsequence
lemma below, whose argument remains written background material. A formal counterpart does
not by itself verify that printed proof route. The later budget, regularization-limit, noise, and
solver-certificate results also have formal counterparts under their stated hypotheses, as do the
explicit infinite diagonal estimates, asymptotic budget constant, and rounded noisy rate. Exact finite
models have separate executable checks. The Hilbert and regularization background is classical [6].
No novelty is claimed for weak compactness or quadratic regularization.
27.1 The correction space is the protected kernel
Let E, Y, Z be real Hilbert spaces, let d : E →Y and p : E →Z be bounded linear maps, and fix a raw
candidate f ∈E. Write
H = ker p,
T = d|H : H →Y,
r = d f.
The space H is a closed Hilbert subspace with the norm inherited from E. An allowed correction is a ∈H,
and its repaired candidate is f −a. Thus
p( f −a) = p f,
d( f −a) = r −Ta.
The protected exact problem is precisely Ta = r. Restricting the domain before optimizing is important.
A penalty on ∥pa∥would only discourage a change in the protected data, not prohibit it.
The adjoint below always means T∗: Y →H, taken with the inherited Hilbert metric on H. In ambient
coordinates this is the projection of d∗onto H. A coordinate basis of ker p need not be orthonormal. Its
Gram matrix must then be included in any calculation of correction energy.
27.2 The compactness needed for the existence argument
Lemma 27.1 (Bounded sequences have weakly convergent subsequences). Every bounded sequence (an) in
a real Hilbert space has a subsequence converging weakly to some a. Moreover,
∥a∥≤lim inf
k
∥ank∥.
Proof. Choose M ≥0 with ∥an∥≤M. If M = 0, every term is zero and the conclusion is immediate.
Otherwise let H0 be the closed linear span of the countably many vectors an. Finite rational linear
combinations of those vectors form a countable dense set, so H0 is separable. Apply Gram–Schmidt to a
countable dense sequence, omitting zero remainders. This gives either a finite orthonormal basis or a
countable orthonormal sequence (ej) whose closed span is H0. The ambient Hilbert space itself need not
be separable.

for the second choose a subsequence of that subsequence, and continue. Taking the kth term from the
kth nested subsequence, with increasing original indices, yields ank for which every fixed coordinate
converges. Call the coordinate limits cj. In the finite-dimensional case only finitely many successive
selections are required.

Theorem 27.2 (Bounded approximate feasibility). For a bounded linear map T : H →Y between real Hilbert
spaces and r ∈Y, the following are equivalent:
r ∈ran T,
there is a bounded sequence (an) ⊂H with ∥Tan −r∥−→0.
If all ∥an∥≤M, an exact solution exists with norm at most M.
Proof. If Ta = r, the constant sequence an = a is bounded and has zero residual. For the converse, let
∥an∥≤M and Tan →r in norm. By the preceding lemma there is a subsequence ank converging weakly
to a ∈H with ∥a∥≤M.
For every fixed y ∈Y, the adjoint identity and weak convergence give
⟨Ta, y⟩= ⟨a, T∗y⟩= lim
k ⟨ank, T∗y⟩= lim
k ⟨Tank, y⟩.
The last limit equals ⟨r, y⟩because norm convergence implies convergence of each continuous pairing.
Thus ⟨Ta −r, y⟩= 0 for every y. Taking y = Ta −r gives ∥Ta −r∥2 = 0, hence Ta = r.
This proof does not claim that Tank converges in norm merely because ank converges weakly. Its norm
convergence to r was a separate hypothesis. The boundedness of the candidate corrections is used to
obtain a weakly convergent subsequence, and their accurate residuals are used to identify its image
exactly. The bound ∥a∥≤M comes from weak lower semicontinuity, so the exact answer stays inside
the same closed budget ball.
OPERATION KOMOREBI

Komorebi Space
Monograph
No closed range assumption appears. The theorem concerns a single datum and a bounded family of
its corrections. Closed range is the stronger, uniform statement that every attainable datum admits a
correction controlled by one residual gain.
An immediate contrapositive is useful. If r ∈ran T \ ran T, then every sequence with Tan →r satisfies
∥an∥→∞. Otherwise infinitely many terms would lie in one bounded ball, contradicting the theorem
along that subsequence. This recovers the escape mechanism of Section 26 without using the diagonal
formula.
27.3 Existence does not make an arbitrary sequence converge strongly
Take H = Y = ℓ2 and Ten = en/n. The vectors an = en have norm one and satisfy Tan →0. An exact
answer exists, namely zero. Yet ∥en −em∥=
√
2 for n ̸= m, so no subsequence converges in norm.
The conclusion of Theorem 27.2 is the existence of a weak limit along a subsequence that solves the
equation. It is not summable movement, a strong convergence rate, or a selection rule for the original
candidates. The minimum correction for the zero datum is zero, which this particular sequence never
approaches in norm.

nonnegative sequence tends to infinity, giving the last conclusion.
The finite answers need not extend one another coordinate by coordinate. Their shared feature is
minimum energy in one common ambient Hilbert space. This is the extra geometry that forces their
differences to be orthogonal to the earlier minimizers.

Finite consistency is not enough by itself. For nested closed affine problems in one Hilbert space,
a uniform bound on the minimum correction norms is exactly what upgrades finite feasibility to
ambient feasibility. This is a datum-specific test, not a uniform inverse bound for the operator.
28 The attainable residual at a fixed correction budget
The unrestricted floor dist(r, ran T) forgets how large a correction must be. To retain that information,
fix a nonnegative budget M and define
βT,r(M) =
inf
∥a∥≤M ∥r −Ta∥.
The norm is the actual correction norm in H = ker p. It is not the norm of arbitrary coordinates used to
represent that kernel. The function β describes the smallest residual compatible with a declared amount
of allowed change.
28.1 At every finite budget the residual minimum exists
Theorem 28.1 (Attainment and shape of the budget profile). For a bounded linear map between real Hilbert
spaces, the infimum defining β(M) is attained for every M ≥0. The profile is nonincreasing and convex. For
0 ≤M1 ≤M2,
0 ≤β(M1) −β(M2) ≤∥T∥(M2 −M1).
Writing C = ran T, b = PCr, and q = ∥r −b∥, one has
lim
M→∞β(M) = q.
The equality β(M) = q at a finite budget is equivalent to the existence of a with Ta = b and ∥a∥≤M. In
particular,
β(M) = 0
⇐⇒
∃a ∈H : Ta = r, ∥a∥≤M.
Proof. The feasible ball is nonempty because it contains zero, and the infimum lies between zero and
∥r∥. Choose an in that ball with ∥r −Tan∥→β(M). A weakly convergent subsequence has limit a
with ∥a∥≤M. Bounded linearity makes r −Tank converge weakly to r −Ta. Applying weak lower
semicontinuity in the target gives
∥r −Ta∥≤lim inf
k
∥r −Tank∥= β(M).
The reverse inequality holds by the definition of the infimum, so a attains it. When M = 0 the only
feasible correction is zero, and the same conclusion reads β(0) = ∥r∥.
For convexity, take minimizers a1, a2 at budgets M1, M2 and t ∈[0, 1]. The candidate at = ta1 + (1 −t)a2
satisfies
∥at∥≤tM1 + (1 −t)M2,

and its residual is t(r −Ta1) + (1 −t)(r −Ta2). The triangle inequality therefore gives
β(tM1 + (1 −t)M2) ≤tβ(M1) + (1 −t)β(M2).
Monotonicity follows from inclusion of balls. If 0 ≤M1 ≤M2 and M2 > 0, choose a minimizer a2 at M2
and put a′

Since r −b is orthogonal to C and b −Ta ∈C, every correction satisfies
∥r −Ta∥2 = q2 + ∥b −Ta∥2 .
(28.1)
Thus β(M) ≥q. Given ϵ > 0, range density in C supplies one correction a with ∥b −Ta∥< ϵ. For every
