First letting the budget increase and then letting ϵ ↓0 proves the limit.
Finally, if β(M) = q, choose an attaining correction in that ball.
The orthogonal identity forces
∥b −Ta∥2 = 0, hence Ta = b. Conversely such a correction attains residual q. Setting q = 0 proves the
exact-feasibility criterion. Attainment at fixed budget is essential to the forward implication: a zero
unrestricted infimum would not itself supply a preimage.
Although ran T may not be closed, the image T({a : ∥a∥≤M}) of each closed budget ball is closed and
convex. To see closedness, take a convergent sequence in that image, lift it to the bounded ball, and
apply the weak limit argument. The full range is the increasing union of these closed images. Passing to
an unlimited budget can therefore lose closedness even though every fixed-budget optimization is well
posed.
The nearest reachable image yM = PT({a:∥a∥≤M})r is unique, so the best residual vector r −yM is unique
as well. Corrections mapping to yM can differ by vectors in ker T. Among those corrections there is a
unique least norm correction. These two uniqueness statements should not be confused with injectivity
of T.
28.2 A dual witness can rule out every correction in the budget

βT,r(M) = max

(28.2)
Thus any single z with ∥z∥≤1 and
⟨r, z⟩−M ∥T∗z∥> ε
certifies that no correction of norm at most M has residual at most ε.
Proof. For every ∥a∥≤M and ∥z∥≤1, the adjoint identity and Cauchy–Schwarz give

Taking the infimum over corrections proves that every proposed dual value is at most β(M). This part
alone already proves the impossibility certificate.
To prove sharpness, let aM be an attaining correction from Theorem 28.1 and put v = r −TaM. If v = 0,
then β(M) = 0 and z = 0 attains the dual bound. Assume henceforth v ̸= 0 and set z = v/ ∥v∥.
Fix any feasible a and put h = a −aM. The segment aM + th is feasible for 0 ≤t ≤1. Minimality gives,

The support value of a Hilbert ball can be computed directly. If T∗z ̸= 0 and M > 0, it is attained at
a = MT∗z/ ∥T∗z∥and equals M ∥T∗z∥. If either factor is zero, every feasible pairing is zero and the
same formula holds. Thus
⟨T∗z, aM⟩= M ∥T∗z∥.
Substituting this equality gives
⟨r, z⟩−M ∥T∗z∥= ⟨r −TaM, z⟩= ∥v∥= β(M).
We have constructed an attaining witness and thereby justified the word “maximum.” The proof uses
one-sided variations in a convex ball, not an unproved exchange of an infimum and a supremum.
In finite dimensions this is a checkable inequality, not merely an optimization prescription. A program
may propose the correction or the witness. The displayed inequality can be verified independently of
the proposal procedure. In a protected ambient calculation, use the restricted adjoint, not d∗without
projection onto ker p.

29 Regularization selects a protected correction
A fixed budget asks how small the residual can become without exceeding an allowed correction size.
Quadratic regularization reverses the emphasis. It asks for the best compromise between the residual
and the size of the change. The resulting problem has a unique answer even when the exact protected
equation has no solution.
That last sentence is both its usefulness and its limitation. The existence of a regularized answer is not
a proof of exact realizability. The limiting behavior, rather than the mere existence of the minimizer,
supplies the diagnosis.
29.1 The normal equation is derived in the protected space
For α > 0, define on H = ker p
Jα(a) = ∥Ta −r∥2 + α ∥a∥2 .
Both norms are the declared Hilbert norms. All minimizers below are corrections in H, so preservation
is exact for every parameter, not just in the limit. This is ordinary quadratic Tikhonov regularization
applied to the actual protected correction operator. The standard regularization background is discussed
in [6]. The proof here does not require T to be compact.
Theorem 29.1 (A unique regularized correction). For every α > 0, there is a unique minimizer
aα = (T∗T + αI)−1T∗r.
It satisfies T∗(r −Taα) = αaα. For every a ∈H,
Jα(a) −Jα(aα) = ∥T(a −aα)∥2 + α ∥a −aα∥2 .
(29.1)
Proof. Step 1: the normal operator has an actual bounded inverse. Let B = T∗T + αI : H →H. It is bounded
and self-adjoint. For every h ∈H,
⟨Bh, h⟩= ∥Th∥2 + α ∥h∥2 ≥α ∥h∥2 .
If h ̸= 0, Cauchy–Schwarz and division by ∥h∥give ∥Bh∥≥α ∥h∥. The same inequality holds at zero. It
implies injectivity of B.
To prove closedness of its range, suppose Bhn →y. Applying the lower bound to hn −hm gives
∥hn −hm∥≤α−1 ∥Bhn −Bhm∥.
Thus hn is Cauchy. Completeness of H supplies a limit h, and continuity gives Bh = y. If y is orthogonal
to the range of B, then for every h,
0 = ⟨Bh, y⟩= ⟨h, By⟩.
Taking h = By gives By = 0, and injectivity gives y = 0. A closed subspace with zero orthogonal
complement is the whole Hilbert space, so ran B = H.
The inverse therefore exists. Its linearity follows from uniqueness of preimages, and its bound is
B−1y
 ≤α−1 ∥y∥. This construction does not assume closedness of ran T. It uses the positive term αI
in the normal operator.
Step 2: define the optimizer and prove its normal equation. Set aα = B−1T∗r. Then Baα = T∗r, or equivalently
T∗(Taα −r) + αaα = 0.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
102


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Because the construction takes place in H = ker p, it already satisfies the exact protection requirement.
Step 3: prove global minimality, rather than just stationarity. Write any proposed correction as a = aα + h
and set s = Taα −r. Expanding both squared norms gives
Jα(aα + h) −Jα(aα) = ∥Th∥2 + α ∥h∥2 + 2⟨s, Th⟩+ 2α⟨aα, h⟩
= ∥Th∥2 + α ∥h∥2 + 2⟨T∗s + αaα, h⟩
= ∥Th∥2 + α ∥h∥2 .
This is the asserted energy identity. It is nonnegative and, since α > 0, vanishes only for h = 0. Therefore
aα is the unique global minimizer. In the zero correction space, the same construction and identities
reduce to its single element, zero.
A declared regularization parameter is part of the problem. A calculation that changes α is solving a
different optimization problem, even if the protected data and forward operator stay fixed. On the other
hand, the preservation condition itself does not change with α.
29.2 Coordinate bases must retain the correction metric
In finite dimensions, choose orthonormal coordinates in the ambient candidate and residual spaces. Let
B have linearly independent columns spanning ker p. The columns of B need not be orthonormal. Write
a = Bc. The coefficient vector c is a representation of the correction, not necessarily an isometric one. Set
S = dB,
G = BTB.
The correct coordinate equation is
(STS + αG)c = STr.
(29.2)
The matrix G is positive definite because the columns are independent. Replacing G by the identity
changes the meaning of correction size unless the chosen basis is orthonormal.
For example, let d(a1, a2) = a1, p(a1, a2) = a1 −a2, r = 1, and α = 1. A basis of ker p is the column
B = (1, 1)T. Here S = 1 and G = 2, so the correction is (1/3, 1/3). Penalizing the coordinate with G = 1
instead gives (1/2, 1/2). The latter is not the optimizer for the ambient Euclidean norm. The difference
is a metric change, not a discrepancy between equivalent calculations.
The executable example in this edition constructs the nullspace basis over the rationals, retains its Gram
matrix, solves (29.2), and returns both the correction and an exact normal-equation residual. It also
handles a zero-dimensional protected kernel without pretending that a nonzero correction exists.
29.3 Parameter monotonicity and the fixed-budget problem
Proposition 29.2 (Regularized answers lie on the budget frontier). If 0 < α < γ, then
∥aα∥≥∥aγ∥,
∥r −Taα∥≤∥r −Taγ∥.
At Mα = ∥aα∥,
β(Mα) = ∥r −Taα∥.
When vα = r −Taα ̸= 0, its normalization is an optimizing witness in Theorem 28.2 at that budget.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
103


Komorebi Space
Monograph
Proof. Let Aα = ∥aα∥2 and Rα = ∥r −Taα∥2. Comparing the two admissible minimizers in each other’s
objectives gives
Rα + αAα ≤Rγ + αAγ,
Rγ + γAγ ≤Rα + γAα.
Add these inequalities and cancel the residual terms. The result is
(γ −α)(Aα −Aγ) ≥0.
Since γ > α, it follows that Aα ≥Aγ. Return to the first comparison to obtain
Rα −Rγ ≤α(Aγ −Aα) ≤0.
Taking square roots, which preserves order for nonnegative numbers, proves both monotonicity state-
ments.
At Mα = ∥aα∥, the vector aα is budget-feasible. If another correction a in that ball had strictly smaller
residual norm, then
Jα(a) = ∥r −Ta∥2 + α ∥a∥2 < ∥r −Taα∥2 + α ∥aα∥2 ,
contradicting its defining minimality. Thus it attains the budget frontier.
For the witness statement, let v = r −Taα ̸= 0. The normal equation gives T∗v = αaα. Also r = v + Taα,
hence
⟨r, v⟩= ∥v∥2 + ⟨aα, T∗v⟩= ∥v∥2 + α ∥aα∥2 .

witness attains the zero budget residual.
This links a penalized calculation to a hard-budget certificate. It does not say that every larger budget
must produce a different answer. Once the least-squares floor is attained, excess budget can be irrelevant.
If T∗r = 0, every regularized correction is zero and the entire profile is constant.
29.4 The limit separates projection from actual realizability
Keep C = ran T, b = PCr, and q = ∥r −b∥. Since r −b ∈ker T∗, the regularized corrections depend on b,
not on the component that is orthogonal to the closure of all attainable residuals.

Proof. Step 1: identify the unavoidable component. Since r −b ⊥ran T, the adjoint identity gives T∗(r −b) =

∥r −Ta∥2 = q2 + ∥b −Ta∥2 .
In particular both the residual square and the minimum objective are at least q2.
Step 2: take the small-parameter limit in the correct order. Let ϵ > 0. Since b lies in the closure of the range,
choose a single a ∈H with ∥r −Ta∥2 < q2 + ϵ. This a is chosen before α. Minimality gives
q2 ≤∥r −Taα∥2 ≤Jα(aα) ≤q2 + ϵ + α ∥a∥2 .
For sufficiently small α, the last term is below q2 + 2ϵ (and if a = 0 it is already below q2 + ϵ). Hence
both intervening quantities tend to q2. Their difference is α ∥aα∥2, which tends to zero. The orthogonal
decomposition now implies ∥Taα −b∥2 →0, and taking square roots gives the first two limits.
Step 3: when the projected datum is attained, obtain a uniform bound. Suppose b ∈ran T. The preimage set is
a nonempty closed affine subset, so projection of zero supplies its unique minimum-norm element a†.

least ϵ from a†. The argument just given would produce a strongly convergent subsequence of them, a
contradiction. This proves the limit for the entire parameter family.
Step 5: in the unattained case the norms tend to infinity. If b /∈ran T and the norms failed to tend to infinity,
there would exist M < ∞and positive αn →0 with ∥aαn∥≤M. Their images still tend to b by Step 2.
Theorem 27.2 would then put b in the actual range, a contradiction. This proves divergence of the norms,
not just unboundedness somewhere along the path.
The vanishing penalty α ∥aα∥2 is not a boundedness certificate for aα. Its coefficient is tending to zero. In
the escaping case, the penalty and residual can both tend to zero while the actual corrections diverge.

The first term tends to zero by the noise/parameter hypothesis. The second tends to zero by the attained-

the same fixed operator and Hilbert metrics. The conclusion is a least-squares correction when the
unavoidable residual component is nonzero.
The theorem does not manufacture an exact solution when r is outside the range. For the positive-floor
attainable case it converges to a least-squares correction. For the unattainable projected datum it cannot
produce a bounded exact-data limit. The range hypothesis must therefore precede the parameter-choice
conclusion.
Regularization gives a unique, exactly protected answer at every positive parameter. Whether
those answers converge to an exact realization, converge only to a best approximation, or escape is
determined by the projected datum’s actual range membership. A smooth regularized path does
not erase that distinction.

30 A complete regularization diagnostic for the escaping model
The abstract alternatives become explicit for the diagonal model. They also answer a natural objection to
the hard prefix corrections: perhaps a smoother correction could fit increasingly well without escaping.
It can fit more efficiently, but it cannot avoid the obstruction.

This is a deterministic guarantee for the specified datum and Hilbert noise bound. It is not a statistical
confidence statement. The identical parameter rule in the escaping problem cannot produce convergence
to an exact protected Hilbert solution, because no such solution exists.
30.4 What the calculation permits a machine to certify
The finite program can check a normal equation, exact preservation, and a quadratic optimality identity.
It can return a dual witness ruling out a smaller residual at a given budget. In the infinite diagonal
example it can return partial sums together with rational tail enclosures. Those are distinct certificates
with distinct conclusions.
None of them alone establishes unrestricted semantic realizability. The bounded-budget theorem needs
a genuinely uniform bound. The exactification theorem needs control of candidate movement. The
OPERATION KOMOREBI
112


Komorebi Space
Monograph
regularization limit needs actual attainment of the projected datum. The repeated appearance of a small
residual does not replace any of those hypotheses.
The useful next diagnostic is therefore not just “Did the residual decrease?” It is “What happened to the
correction norm, what quantity was preserved, and what existence theorem applies to this datum?”
31 Certifying an inexact protected correction
An existence theorem for a regularized optimizer does not guarantee that a numerical procedure has
reached it. The procedure may stop early, use a poorly scaled basis, or report the residual of the wrong
equation. These are mathematical distinctions, not implementation details that can be deferred until
after a certificate has been interpreted.
The relevant question is now more specific. Given a proposed correction, what quantities can be
checked without first computing the exact optimizer? The answer is a normal-equation residual and,
independently, a primal and dual objective gap. Both give quantitative statements about the declared
regularized problem. Neither promises that the original unregularized realization problem is solvable.
The accompanying formalization constructs the positive-parameter optimizer and proves the energy
identity, global uniqueness, and the estimates in this chapter, with a separate bridge to the actual
protected kernel. Regularization limits and the combined inexact stopping theorem also have formal
counterparts. The explicit infinite diagonal results also have formal counterparts. The printed
weak-subsequence argument remains written background material. The exact Python certificate
verifier is tested, not universally verified as a refinement of Lean.
31.1 There are three different residuals
Keep H = ker p, T = d|H, and a residual datum r. Fix α > 0. Write aα for the unique minimizer of
Theorem 29.1 and let a ∈H be any proposed correction. Three quantities now have different meanings:
v = r −Ta,
g = T∗(Ta −r) + αa,
e = a −aα.
The vector v is the fit residual. The vector g is the normal-equation residual, equal to one half of the
derivative of the quadratic objective after identifying the dual with H. The vector e is the unknown
correction error. The exact optimizer generally has v ̸= 0 but always has g = 0.
The adjoint in g is the restricted adjoint on H, not the unrestricted ambient adjoint. Equivalently,
T∗v = PHd∗v.
Thus the correction certificate must measure the part of the normal equation that belongs to the permitted
correction space. A numerical program that forgets this projection can incorrectly reject an exact protected
optimizer.

113


Komorebi Space
Monograph

Sharp scalar examples.

operator.

exactly ∥e∥. Both preserve p f because both corrections belong to H. The theorem therefore controls a
genuine protected candidate error, not just an intermediate coordinate vector.

31.3 Why a vanishing normal residual is not enough
Let T = 0 on R, let r = 0, and take αn = n−2 for n ≥1. The exact optimizer is zero for every such n. The
fixed proposal an = 1 instead has
vn = 0,
gn = αn −→0,
Jαn(an) = αn −→0,
|an −aαn| = 1.
Thus the fit residual, the normal residual, and even the absolute objective value can all tend to zero
without convergence to the selected optimizer. The certificate correctly reports |gn| /αn = 1.
This is not an infinite-dimensional obstruction. It occurs in one dimension because the regularizing
penalty itself disappears. The question is not whether the reported residual is small in isolation, but
whether it is small relative to the parameter that converts it into an error bound.
31.4 A dual certificate does not require solving the normal equation

quadratic penalties supply the control.

Consequently Dα(z) ≤Jα(a) for every pair. At a = aα and z = zα = r −Taα, equality holds, so the dual

α ∥a −aα∥2 ≤Jα(a) −Dα(z).

Set a = aα and zα = r −Taα. The first square is zero by this definition. The normal equation gives

For every other z, weak duality with aα gives Dα(z) ≤Jα(aα). This proves attainment of the dual

For a general proposed pair (a, z), decompose its gap as
Jα(a) −Dα(z) = [Jα(a) −Jα(aα)] + [Jα(aα) −Dα(z)].
The second bracket is nonnegative by weak duality, while Theorem 29.1 bounds the first below by
α ∥a −aα∥2. This proves the correction-error certificate.
Finally take z = r −Ta. The first square in the gap is zero, while T∗z −αa = −g. The second becomes
∥g∥2 /α, proving the residual-witness formula. A small duality gap controls optimizer error only after
division by the specified positive parameter.
A proposed correction and a proposed dual witness can come from different procedures. The verifier only
needs the mathematical problem, the two vectors, and the displayed identities. A poor witness remains
valid as a lower bound, but can give a loose gap. The residual witness avoids a second optimization
routine and recovers the bound in Theorem 31.1 exactly.

32 Coordinates, noise, and a justified stopping rule
A certificate can be exact and still be interpreted incorrectly. Two common errors are to measure
a coordinate covector with the wrong norm, or to keep a solver tolerance fixed while sending the
regularization parameter to zero. Both change what a small reported number means.
32.1 The residual of the coordinate equation is a covector
In finite dimensions, use orthonormal coordinates in the ambient candidate and residual spaces. Let the
columns of B form an arbitrary basis of H = ker p, not necessarily an orthonormal one. Write a = Bc,
S = dB, and G = BTB. As before, G is positive definite. For an inexact coefficient vector c, the residual
of the coordinate normal equation is
q = (STS + αG)c −STr.
The vector q stores pairings against basis vectors. It is not the coordinate vector of the Hilbert normal
residual unless the basis is orthonormal.
Proposition 32.1 (The invariant norm of a coordinate certificate). The actual normal residual is
g = BG−1q,
∥g∥2 = qTG−1q.
(32.1)
For any invertible change of basis B′ = BC, the same proposed correction has
c′ = C−1c,
G′ = CTGC,
q′ = CTq,
and
(q′)T(G′)−1q′ = qTG−1q.
Proof. Because B has independent columns, for every nonzero coefficient vector c,
cTGc = cTBTBc = ∥Bc∥2 > 0.
Thus G is positive definite and invertible. The matrix P = BG−1BT is symmetric and obeys
P2 = BG−1(BTB)G−1BT = P,
PB = B.
Its range is precisely the column space H, so it is the orthogonal projection onto the entire permitted
correction space.
The restricted Hilbert normal residual is
g = PdT(dBc −r) + αBc.
Substitute S = dB, G = BTB and the expression for P:
g = BG−1(ST(Sc −r) + αGc) = BG−1q.
Equivalently q = BTg records the pairings of g with the basis columns. Its squared Hilbert norm is
gTg = qTG−1BTBG−1q = qTG−1q,
using symmetry of G−1. This proves both coordinate formulas.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
118


Komorebi Space
Monograph
For the basis change B′ = BC, the same ambient vector is represented by c′ = C−1c. Also S′ = SC and
G′ = CTGC. Substitute these formulas into the new coordinate normal equation:
q′ = ((S′)TS′ + αG′)c′ −(S′)Tr
= CT(STS + αG)CC−1c −CTSTr = CTq.
The inverse metric is (G′)−1 = C−1G−1(CT)−1. Hence
(q′)T(G′)−1q′ = qTCC−1G−1(CT)−1CTq = qTG−1q.
For a zero-dimensional kernel these are empty matrices and zero vectors, and the resulting scalar norm
is zero. The spanning assumption is indispensable: a basis of a proper subspace would compute a
projection to a different correction problem.
In the previous example, B = (1, 1)T, G = 2, and the proposal c = 1/4 gives q = −1/4. Thus
qTG−1q = 1/32, as required. Replacing B by sB changes q to sq and G to s2G. The Euclidean norm of the
coordinate covector can be made arbitrarily small or large by changing the nonzero scalar s. The actual
correction, normal residual, and certified error do not change.

convergence of the proposals.
Protection is not merely asymptotic: an ∈ker p implies p( f −an) = p f at every index. The proof
compares no changing operators or changing kernels. Both the pointwise certificate and the convergence
conclusion have formal counterparts under these hypotheses.
The accompanying Lean formalization includes the pointwise estimate (32.2), the regularization limit,
and the resulting convergence theorem. Their statements retain the same fixed operator, Hilbert geometry,
and attainment requirements.

For the escaping datum, the same rule does not create convergence to a nonexistent protected Hilbert
answer. It only makes the numerical proposals track the regularized path accurately. Tracking an
escaping path and converging to a realization are different conclusions.
32.4 The executable verification contract
The program takes d, p, f, α and a proposed ambient correction, with optional measured residuals, a full
kernel basis, and an independent dual witness. It rejects a correction that changes the protected data
OPERATION KOMOREBI
121


Komorebi Space
Monograph
and checks the basis’s dimensions, rank, and kernel membership. It does not silently project a rejected
proposal into another problem.
The output records f −a, protection before and after, measured fit, actual model residual, the Gram-
corrected normal certificate, objective values, and squared error bounds. Verification recomputes the
record without calling an optimizer. Independent determinant-based tests compare the bounds with
actual errors in small ambient constrained systems. A certificate establishes consistency with its included
problem, not authentication that this is the externally intended task.
A certificate needs a declared target. Fit measures agreement with data. The normal residual bounds
optimizer error at a specified parameter. The duality gap bounds objective error. A realization
theorem is still needed to conclude convergence to an intended exact object.

Part VI
Worked realization problems
The constructions become easier to distinguish when a complete example is carried
through from its view equations to its obstruction, its ambiguity, and its numerical
repair. The following calculations instantiate the preceding principles. They do not
introduce a second, silently enlarged formal theory.
33 A closed signal loop
33.1 Prescribed differences as affine view transitions
Consider four scalar views arranged around a cycle. Their values are x0, x1, x2, x3, and the prescribed
differences are r0, r1, r2, r3. Indices are taken modulo four. The compatibility equations are
xi+1 −xi = ri.
(33.1)
Work over Q or R. These equations can be presented as a strict set-valued view system: an oriented
edge has pullback z 7→z −ri, and a path has the composite affine pullback. Use the category of paths,
retaining the full loop as a path rather than imposing that it is an identity arrow in advance.
Every edge map is bijective. Around the cycle their composite is translation by −∑i ri. A global
realization must be fixed by that translation. Thus the example makes local invertibility and global
compatibility visibly different without needing a noncommutative group.
33.2 The complete solvability calculation
Summing (33.1) gives the necessary condition
r0 + r1 + r2 + r3 = 0.
(33.2)
It is sufficient as well. Given an anchor a, set
x0 = a,
x1 = a + r0,
x2 = a + r0 + r1,
x3 = a + r0 + r1 + r2.
The first three equations hold by construction, and the last is exactly (33.2). Every solution has this form
because the first three equations recursively determine it from x0. Therefore a compatible residual has
a one-dimensional family of primitives, with constant shifts as its ambiguity. Prescribing the anchor
removes that ambiguity.
In matrix form, the difference operator is
D =

Thus ℓis a complete feasibility test for this prescribed-difference problem. It annihilates every actual
difference family, but takes a nonzero value on an incompatible one. This is the finite obstruction-
certificate pattern of Proposition 4.2 and Proposition 35.3 in a fully visible model.
The same proof works on a cycle of any positive length: partial sums give a primitive, and the final
wraparound equation is the zero-sum condition. No Fourier transform or numerical approximation is
needed for this solvability argument.
33.3 Repairing an incompatible residual is a new problem
Take

Each of the first three edge equations can be satisfied, but the full sum is one. There is no coherent
closed-loop signal with exactly these four differences. Computing a long list of partial values does not
remove the missing wraparound equation.
To repair the residual, now regard the vector of edge data as a candidate in its own right. Its defect is ℓ(r).
The exact subspace is the zero-sum hyperplane. With the ordinary Euclidean norm and no protected
edge coordinates, the nearest compatible residual is

rather than 1/4. The larger error is not a failure of nearest repair: the feasible set has changed.

33.5 Where the packet and transfer viewpoints enter
The raw edge family contains information about four transitions. Its zero-sum constraint links all
four. Summing a block of differences transfers information to a coarser transition, while retaining the
differences within the block preserves the detail that the sum omits. For example,
r0 + r1 = x2 −x0
contains the two-step transition but does not determine r0 and r1 separately. Replacing (r0, r1) by
(r0 + t, r1 −t) leaves that coarse transition unchanged.
A packet of coarse value and independent detail retains both directions. A residual-only summary can
detect failure of the wraparound equation but cannot tell which protected edge may be changed. An
anchor or a mean fixes the remaining signal ambiguity after feasible edge data have been chosen. These
are three different roles: transition compression, feasibility repair, and representative selection.
This example is a complete finite calculation built from the strict realization, additive obstruction, and
protected Hilbert principles. The companion program checks the displayed rational values, the zero-sum
criterion, the primitive reconstruction, and the orthogonality equations. The calculation does not claim
formal verification of a Fourier or signal-processing library.

34 A loop with a nontrivial fixed space
The previous cycle has scalar translations and a scalar total obstruction. A linear connection can behave
differently: its loop may be nontrivial while retaining a whole subspace of feasible anchors. The
following three-vertex model distinguishes full anchor preservation, feasible-anchor preservation, and
global nearest-point repair in one calculation.
34.1 The connection and its exact families
Let the fiber at each of three vertices be R2. Use identity transport along the route 0 →1 →2, and let the
closing edge carry the reflection
J =
 
1
0
0
−1
!
.
For one chosen orientation of each edge, the residuals are
r01 = x1 −x0,
r12 = x2 −x1,
r20 = Jx0 −x2.
The reverse transports are inverses, so this defines an inverse-consistent isometric connection. Exactness
first forces x1 = x0 and x2 = x0, and the remaining edge then forces Jx0 = x0. Hence every exact family
is
((a, 0), (a, 0), (a, 0)),
a ∈R.
(34.1)
The loop is not the identity on the whole fiber. Its fixed space is the first coordinate axis. Every anchor
on that axis extends uniquely, and every anchor with nonzero second coordinate is obstructed if its full
value is to be preserved.
The three-edge residual also gives a direct telescope:
r01 + r12 + r20 = (J −I)x0.
Its second component is −2(x0)2. In this chosen route normalization, the loop residual determines the
transverse anchor component. It does not constrain the first component, which is the exact freedom seen
in (34.1).
34.2 Three different questions for the same raw data
Take
x0 = (2, 1),
x1 = (4, −1),
x2 = (3, 2).
The measured oriented residuals are
r01 = (2, −2),
r12 = (−1, 3),
r20 = (−1, −3).
Preserving the entire original anchor is impossible, because (2, 1) is not fixed by J. The earlier obstruction
criterion therefore gives a negative answer to that particular preservation problem.
Preserving only the feasible projection of the anchor gives a different answer. Orthogonally project (2, 1)
to (2, 0), then reconstruct along the tree. The result is
Panchorx = ((2, 0), (2, 0), (2, 0)).
It is the unique exact family preserving the first component of the original anchor. It does not preserve
its second component, and makes no claim to do so.

For global nearest-point repair, declare the Euclidean product norm on (R2)3. The closest member of
(34.1) uses the mean of the three first coordinates, namely three:
Pnearestx = ((3, 0), (3, 0), (3, 0)).
To verify this without appealing only to a general theorem, write the squared error at parameter a:
(2 −a)2 + (4 −a)2 + (3 −a)2 + 12 + (−1)2 + 22 = 3(a −3)2 + 8.
It is minimized uniquely at a = 3. The anchor-based answer has squared error eleven. The globally
nearest answer has squared error eight. Their difference has squared norm three, giving the expected
Pythagorean identity 11 = 8 + 3.
No two of these conclusions conflict. Full anchor preservation is infeasible. Feasible-anchor preservation
has a unique answer. Unprotected global nearest repair selects a different answer. A zero-residual output
without its preservation contract would not distinguish them.

For this paragraph use one orientation per edge and the Euclidean norm on the six residual coordinates.

Since ∥v∥2 = 3, this matrix has eigenvalue four in the direction of v and eigenvalue one on its orthogonal

These two identities show that the least nonnegative gain for the full defect-to-distance estimate in
this Euclidean model is one. The lower bound is attained, for example, on a candidate with zero first
components and second-component vector (1, 1, 0), which lies in v⊥. The squared input distance and
squared residual are both two.
For the displayed raw candidate, the squared residual is
(22 + (−2)2) + ((−1)2 + 32) + ((−1)2 + (−3)2) = 28.
Its squared nearest-point error is eight, consistent with the estimate. The certificate is universal for this
fixed matrix because the Gram identities hold for all vectors, not because that particular example passed.
If both orientations are retained with a Euclidean sum-of-squares residual norm, each isometric edge
contributes its squared residual norm twice. The residual norm is multiplied by
√
2, and the least gain
becomes 1/
√
2. With the maximum residual norm used in the general graph theorem, that duplication
does not change the maximum. This is a concrete illustration of why the norm convention must travel
with a certificate.
34.4 What this example connects
The strict section equations identify the exact families. The holonomy fixed space determines which
anchors are feasible. The protected observable selects which exact answer is admissible. The Hilbert
geometry selects a nearest representative when that is the question being asked. The Gram matrices
determine the quantitative gain in one declared coordinate model.
The example does not require the loop to be trivial on the whole fiber. Nor does it require the anchor
repair to be globally orthogonal. Both of those stronger conclusions would be false. The realization
calculus is useful here because it keeps the correct weaker structures available rather than forcing the
model into a stronger but incorrect description.
35 Mathematical meaning and the remaining boundaries
35.1 A relative theory can be complete without being absolute
The strict space Γ(F) is canonical relative to its diagram: its universal property determines it up to
the appropriate unique isomorphism. The additive obstruction is canonical relative to its admissible
corrections. A marked realization problem is invariant under the specified comparison equivalences. A
repair can be canonical relative to a protected observable, or orthogonal relative to a fixed inner product.
These are meaningful and different kinds of canonicity. They need not culminate in one object inde-
pendent of every choice of views, coefficients, topology, semantics, and geometry. Some changes of
view intentionally forget information. Others strengthen the question. Treating every such change as an
equivalence would remove the very distinctions the calculus is designed to preserve.
The appropriate central statement is therefore not that every possible mathematical presentation lives in
one fixed universal space. It is that a declared system of representations can be organized into coherent
OPERATION KOMOREBI
128


Komorebi Space
Monograph
realizations, with explicit extension, comparison, and repair problems. A proposed bridge between two
such systems has to show what structure it preserves and what information it loses.
35.2 Internal realization and intended objects
Suppose a separate category S contains the intended semantic objects of an application, and a realization
construction gives a comparison functor
R : S −→K,
where K is the relevant category or groupoid of coherent data. Defining K does not establish that R is
faithful, full, or essentially surjective. These are new mathematical questions about the actual semantic
comparison.
Faithfulness asks whether distinct semantic arrows with the same source and target can become indistin-
guishable. Fullness asks whether every arrow between the images of two semantic objects comes from
an arrow between those objects. Essential surjectivity asks whether every object of K is isomorphic to
the image of an actual semantic object. Depending on the application, one may seek only a restricted
image or a classified obstruction to belonging to it.
This distinction prevents a formal globalization of encoded data from being mistaken for an unrestricted
arithmetic or physical existence theorem. The abstract construction does not manufacture missing
semantic objects.
35.3 Every finite test can pass while the infinite problem fails
Example 35.1 (Finite prefixes without a global point). Let Xn = {m ∈N : m ≥n}, with bonding maps
given by inclusion. Every level is nonempty, and every finite prefix has a coherent point. The inverse
limit is nevertheless empty.
Proof. For a fixed terminal level N, the integer N belongs to every fiber Xn with n ≤N. Assign that same
integer at each such level. Every restriction is an inclusion, so it preserves the assigned integer and all
finite coherence equations hold. This proves finite solvability with the quantifiers in the order “for every
N, there exists a prefix.”
Suppose instead that x = (xn) is a global point. Equality along each inclusion says that the underlying
natural numbers of xn+1 and xn are equal. Induction therefore makes every underlying coordinate equal
to the number m underlying x0. Membership of xm+1 in Xm+1 now gives m ≥m + 1, a contradiction.
Thus no global point exists.
There is no conflict with a gluing theorem for prefixes. The prefix chosen above for length N + 1 restricts
to the value N + 1, not to our chosen value N on the shorter prefix. Nor could another choice of one
solution for every length give a matching family: its value at level zero would force the same forbidden
global integer. Separate existence at every length is strictly weaker than existence of a compatible family
of prefix solutions.
This counterexample is formalized in FinitePrefixBoundary.lean using the original resolution and
partial-section types. It explains why inverse-limit existence needs more than separate finite solvability.
Finiteness, compactness, surjectivity, or another coherence principle must be proved in the relevant
application. The example does not contradict the strict gluing theorem: a compatible matching family of
all prefixes was never supplied.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
129


Komorebi Space
Monograph
35.4 A positive infinite-existence criterion
The failed example isolates a missing compactness or lifting hypothesis. Finite fibers supply a concrete
positive criterion without requiring every bonding map to be surjective.
Proposition 35.2 (Finite fibers and persistent images). Let (Xn, ρn) be a sequential inverse system of nonempty
finite sets. Its inverse limit is nonempty. More precisely, if ρn,m : Xm →Xn is composite restriction, then
im(evn) = Yn,
Yn =
\
m≥n
im ρn,m.
Thus a prescribed value at level n extends precisely when it survives every later image.
Proof. Write In,m = ρn,m(Xm) for m ≥n. Each In,m is nonempty. The composition law gives
In,m+1 = ρn,m
 ρm(Xm+1)
 ⊆In,m.
These are subsets of the finite set Xn. Every strict decrease removes an element permanently, so only
finitely many strict decreases are possible. There is an index sn ≥n such that In,m = In,sn for all m ≥sn.
Consequently Yn = In,sn is nonempty.
We next prove that restriction induces a surjection Yn+1 →Yn, even though Xn+1 →Xn need not be
surjective. For membership, let z ∈Yn+1. Given m ≥n + 1, write z = ρn+1,m(w) for some w ∈Xm. Then
ρnz = ρn,m(w). Together with the automatic condition at m = n, this shows ρnz ∈Yn.
For surjectivity, fix y ∈Yn and choose
m ≥max{sn, sn+1, n + 1}.
Since y ∈In,m, there is w ∈Xm with ρn,m(w) = y. Put z = ρn+1,m(w). Stabilization at level n + 1 gives
z ∈Yn+1, and composition gives ρnz = y.
Choose x0 ∈Y0 and recursively choose xn+1 ∈Yn+1 above xn. The preceding surjectivity justifies each
step. Adjacent coherence implies every composite coherence equation, so this gives a point. This is an
existence construction using countably many choices, not an asserted effective procedure for locating the
stabilization indices.
Finally, fix a level n. Every global point satisfies xn = ρn,m(xm) for each m ≥n, hence xn ∈Yn. Conversely,
start with any y ∈Yn, lift recursively above level n through the surjections just proved, and define the
lower coordinates by xi = ρi,n(y). The composite restriction laws verify coherence across and below
level n. The resulting point evaluates to y. These two inclusions prove the asserted equality of evaluation
images, not merely nonemptiness of the inverse limit.
This is the standard finite-set inverse-limit principle, here with its sequential persistent-image proof [1].
Its formalization in FiniteRealizationExistence.lean constructs an explicit equivalence with the
pinned library’s section type, then applies the finite cofiltered-system and eventual-range theorems. The
printed sequential proof and this formal route establish the same stated criterion. Both use ordinary
classical choice. Surjective bonding maps on arbitrary nonempty sets give a second sufficient condition
by direct recursive lifting. Neither criterion says that arbitrary data in a non-surjective finite tower
extend.

35.5 A finite computation that exposes the preservation contract
Consider a raw vector x = (2, 0, 5) ∈R3 with defect d(x) = x1 −x2. Unconstrained orthogonal repair
gives (1, 1, 5), with squared error two. If the first and third coordinates must be preserved, the unique
exact repair is (2, 2, 5), with squared error four.
Both outputs are exact. Only one satisfies the second preservation problem. Neither theorem contradicts
the other because the admissible changes differ. An implementation that reports only zero residual
would not distinguish the two tasks.
For a finite linear system over a field, the same question admits independently checkable positive and
negative certificates. Let A ∈Km×n encode compatibility, P ∈Kp×n the protected values, and x ∈Kn
the raw candidate. Let the columns of N be a basis of ker P. The correction equation is
ANz = Ax,
y = x −Nz.
It is equivalent to the stacked system Ay = 0, Py = Px.
Proposition 35.3 (A finite certificate alternative). Exactly one of the following occurs:
1. There is y with Ay = 0 and Py = Px. The full answer set is y + H, where H = ker A ∩ker P.
2. There are row vectors λ ∈K1×m and µ ∈K1×p with
λA = µP,
λAx = 1.
The negative witness annihilates every admissible correction defect, since λAN = µPN = 0. The ambiguity
dimension in the positive case is
dim H = n −rank
 
A
P
!
.
Proof. Set T =
 
A
P
!
and b =
 
0
Px
!
. A vector y is an exact protected answer if and only if Ty = b.
Perform Gaussian row operations on the augmented matrix, keeping their product U. Each elementary
row operation is invertible over the given field. Thus Ty = b and UTy = Ub have exactly the same
solutions.
If no row of the reduced augmented system has zero left side and nonzero right side, assign arbitrary
values to its free variables and solve for its pivot variables. This supplies a solution y0. For any vector y,
Ty = b
⇐⇒
T(y −y0) = 0.
Translation by y0 is therefore a bijection from ker T to the answer set, with inverse y 7→y −y0. Rank–
nullity applied to the actual linear map T : Kn →Km+p gives the ambiguity dimension. Dependent
prescription rows do not alter this argument.
If the reduced system is inconsistent, choose a row with zero left side and right side α ̸= 0. Let w be
the corresponding row of U, and split it according to the two blocks as w = (u, v). The retained row
operations give the literal identities
uA + vP = 0,
vPx = α.
Division is permitted because α is a nonzero field element. Define λ = −α−1u and µ = α−1v. Multiplying
the displayed equations by α−1 gives
λA = µP,
λAx = µPx = 1.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
131


Komorebi Space
Monograph
For every admissible correction a ∈ker P, these same row equations imply λAa = µPa = 0. The witness
therefore annihilates the entire correction image while taking value one on the required defect.
The alternatives cannot occur together. If y were an exact protected answer in the presence of such a
witness, then
1 = λAx = µPx = µPy = λAy = 0.
A field has 1 ̸= 0, so this is impossible. Consistency or inconsistency of the reduced system proves
exhaustiveness, and the contradiction proves exclusivity. Empty row or column blocks cause no extra
division: normalization is used only in the inconsistent case, where the nonzero scalar has already been
obtained. The proof does not transfer to integral coefficients by silently allowing the same division.
The formal proof gives these literal row-vector equations over an arbitrary field. It uses algebraic dual
separation of the stacked image rather than verifying the row-reduction program. The affine answer
equivalence and rank-nullity formula are proved separately. Thus a normalized obstruction is an actual
checkable witness, while universal correctness of the supplied Python elimination implementation
remains a different question.
35.6 One equation, three preservation problems
For the following three cases work over Q, with A = (1, −1, 0) and x = (2, 0, 5). With P(x) = (x1, x3), the
unique answer is (2, 2, 5). With P(x) = x3, the entire answer set is {(t, t, 5) : t ∈Q}. With P(x) = (x1, x2),
no answer exists. In this last case
λ = (1/2),
µ = (1/2, −1/2)
is a normalized negative certificate over Q or R: λA = µP and λAx = 1.
The package implements this finite interface over Q in examples/protected_repair.py. It returns a
particular exact answer and an ambiguity basis, or a normalized dual witness. Its verifier checks the
certificate equations directly. A Gaussian particular answer is not advertised as the nearest answer of
Theorem 18.3. That is a separate geometric selection.
Exact field elimination does not solve the integer parity problem by changing its coefficient domain.
Over Q, 2m = 1 has the solution 1/2. Over Z, it has none. The rational interface rejects floating-point
inputs rather than silently treating an approximate decimal as exact arithmetic. Its finite tests establish
the tested implementation behavior, not an arbitrary-input Lean refinement theorem.
35.7 Why this is relevant to machine reasoning
The framework gives a machine explicit mathematical objects to manipulate: views, restrictions, partial
realizations, typed defects, admissible corrections, and comparison witnesses. A successful step can
carry both an output and a certificate of what remained fixed. A failed step can return an obstruction in
a specified quotient rather than an unexplained failure flag.
The split coordinate theorem is especially direct. In its stated class, the same candidate is represented
by protected information and residual data, and repair zeros only the residual coordinate. The descent
theorem explains which local certificates can be assembled. The analytic results explain when a small
measured defect has a quantitatively reliable meaning.
These observations motivate a machine oriented mathematical language. They do not establish an
autonomous reasoning system, an empirical intelligence gain, or a general efficient algorithm. Classical
OPERATION KOMOREBI
132


Komorebi Space
Monograph
choice, infinite products, quotient membership, and bounded inverse constructions can be mathemat-
ically valid without yielding executable finite procedures. Implementation claims need their own
specifications and tests.
35.8 What this monograph closes, and what it leaves open
The book supplies a coherent account of strict realization spaces, additive and nonabelian extension
problems, split tower repair, retained multiscale detail, a genuine composition-site stack, presentation
transport, graph holonomy, closed-range stability, and moving exact spaces. The convergence and
obstruction theory separates small measurements from small movements, coherent coordinate families
from their semantic image, and zero residual from zero transformation cost. The bounded-budget
theory supplies a positive existence criterion and keeps range membership separate from regularized
solvability. Its fixed-budget dual witness can certify a limit on every admissible correction, while its
diagonal example computes the actual leading cost of approximation. Each layer is attached to the data
that make it meaningful.
The following broader targets are not conclusions of this edition: arbitrary higher categorical coefficients
and infinity-stack descent, unrestricted changes of view doctrine, norm reconstruction for every bounded
infinite residual hierarchy, nonlinear or unknown-connection repair, perturbation across changing Hilbert
geometries without new comparison data, and semantic essential surjectivity for unrestricted arithmetic
or physical applications.
The accompanying Lean project contains 162 production modules and 4 entry points, with compiler
records, declaration axiom reports, and rejection controls. The bounded-budget, regularization-limit,
noise, inexact-certificate, and coordinate-invariance results have formal counterparts under their stated
hypotheses. The explicit infinite diagonal estimates, leading budget constant over all real budgets, and
rounded noisy rate also have formal counterparts. The budget results do not require finite-dimensionality,
separability, injectivity, or closed range. Their robustness comparison uses one fixed correction space.
Further extensions require their own hypotheses and evidence.
The organizing principle. Coherent realization, obstruction to extension, freedom of completion,
and stability of repair belong to one mathematical conversation. Their unification is useful precisely
when it does not identify them with one another.

A A reader’s workshop
These short problems isolate choices that recur in the main development. Each has a complete solution.
They are reading exercises and direct applications of the cited theory, not additional entries in the formal
theorem inventory.
1. A stable limit does not make every seed persistent
At every level take Xn = {0, 1}, with each bonding map sending both elements to zero. Find the inverse
limit and the image of evaluation at every level. Does nonemptiness of every finite fiber make the seed
one extendable?
Solution. For a coherent family, xn = ρn(xn+1) = 0 for every n. The all-zero family is coherent and is
therefore the only point. Every evaluation image is {0}. Equivalently, the image of restriction from any
strictly later level is already {0}, so the persistent image is {0}. The seed one never extends. This is
compatible with Proposition 35.2, which proves nonemptiness and characterizes the persistent image,
not surjectivity of every evaluation.
2. A residual chart can change without changing the defect
Let d : R2 →R be d(u, r) = r. For a ∈R, let Aa(r) = (ar, r). Compute the exact repair Pa, compare it
with Pb, and decide when they commute.
Solution. Since dAa = I,
Pa(u, r) = (u −ar, 0),
Pb(u, r) −Pa(u, r) = ((a −b)r, 0).
Both fix the horizontal exact axis. Thus PaPb = Pb and PbPa = Pa. They commute exactly when they are
equal, which occurs exactly when a = b, as can be checked at input (0, 1). Every repair has zero residual.
The different answers reflect the different right inverses, as in Theorem 9.1.
3. Pairwise invertibility does not supply a triple law
Let t be a nonidentity transposition in S3. Suppose three proposed overlap comparisons require frames
b0, b1, b2 satisfying
b1 = tb0,
b2 = tb1,
b2 = tb0.
Every comparison is individually invertible. Can the three frames exist?
Solution. The first two equations give b2 = t2b0 = b0. The third then gives b0 = tb0, and right
cancellation gives t = 1, contrary to the assumption. Invertibility of every proposed overlap map does
not prove the composition law needed for descent. Theorem 12.1 applies to data satisfying that law, not
to arbitrary lists of pairwise isomorphisms.
4. Zero noisy output is not a certificate of reference exactness
Take D(a, b) = a, eD(a, b) = a + 1
10b, and input x = (−10, 100). The measured operator returns zero
exactly. Find the distance to the reference kernel and evaluate the reference uncertainty bound.
Solution. The reference kernel is the vertical axis, so the distance is ten. Its residual gain is one, and the
operator error norm is 1/10. With y = 0 and η = 0, Proposition 22.1 gives
dist(x, ker D) ≤1
10
p
102 + 1002 =
√
101.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
134


Komorebi Space
Monograph
The true distance ten is below this bound. A formula omitting the operator-error term would instead
claim a zero distance, which is false. The input is exact for the measured model and not for the reference
one.
5. A prefix computation can hide a wraparound defect
On a four-cycle, take proposed differences (2, −1, 3, −3) and anchor zero. Integrate the first three edges.
Which equation fails, and what changes when only the final edge is allowed to move?
Solution. The first three edges give values (0, 2, 1, 4). Their required last difference is x0 −x3 = −4, not
−3. The total prescribed sum is one, so the original loop has no realization. Keeping the first three edges
forces the unique correction (2, −1, 3, −4), whose sum is zero. This is a protected repair of the edge data.
It is not a realization of the original data without modification.
6. Residual decay can hide unprotected drift
In maximum-norm R3, use p(s, u, r) = s and d(s, u, r) = r. Compare the paths (1, 0, (n + 1)−1) and
(1, Hn, (n + 1)−1), where Hn = ∑n
j=1 1/j. Which hypotheses distinguish them?
Solution. Both preserve p = 1 and have the same residual ratios (n + 1)/(n + 2). The first moves by
1/((n + 1)(n + 2)) and converges to (1, 0, 0). The second moves by 1/(n + 1) and has an unbounded
second coordinate. In the variable-rate theorem, the first has Cn = 1/(n + 2) and a summable weighted
budget. The second has Cn = 1, making that budget harmonic and divergent. The difference is
movement, not residual decay or semantic preservation.
7. How a finite observation changes the apparent rate
Let ηn = 1/(n + 1). Find its profile after sampling at ϕ(k) = 2k −1. Does the new profile prove
exponential convergence in the original index?
Solution. The sampled value is ηϕ(k) = 2−k. Recovering the original index gives k = log2(n + 1) on the
sampled stages, so the value is still 1/(n + 1). The schedule preserves vanishing but changes the meaning
of the horizontal axis. No faster original process has been proved. Proposition 24.5 is a convergence
equivalence under a monotone cofinal schedule, not a rate-invariance theorem.
8. What does a zero floor fail to certify?
For the diagonal model of Section 26, can an exact protected answer exist in ℓ2 ⊕R? Does a coherent
family of exact finite prefixes exist? Explain why the answers differ without contradicting finite-fiber
existence.
Solution. Exactness with protected value one forces every sequence coordinate to equal minus one. This
is not square summable, so there is no protected Hilbert answer. Each finite prefix nevertheless has the
unique solution (−1, . . . , −1; 1), and these solutions are coherent under truncation. Their inverse limit
contains the all-minus-one sequence in the full product. The finite-fiber theorem supplies that coherent
point. It does not prove that the point is in the image of the square-summable semantic space. The
correction image is dense but does not contain the raw residual, so its floor is zero and its algebraic class
is nonzero.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
135


Komorebi Space
Monograph
9. A phase witness without enumerating every gauge
For c(x, y) = x2y1 on F2
2, show that no scalar coboundary equals c. Then compute the square of the lifted
label (0, e1 + e2) in the eight-element extension.
Solution. Scalar coboundaries a(x) + a(y) −a(x + y) are symmetric, but c(e1, e2) = 0 and c(e2, e1) = 1.
Thus no scalar coboundary equals c. For x = e1 + e2, c(x, x) = 1, so (0, x)2 = (1, 0) and its fourth power
is the identity. The corresponding matrix is XZ, whose square is −I. The additional phase bit records
precisely the sign lost by projecting back to the four-label group.
10. The finite energy identity
In R2, first prescribe a1 + a2 = 1, then also prescribe a1 = 3/4. Find the two minimum norm corrections
and check the exact nested-affine identity.
Solution. The first correction is (1/2, 1/2), with energy 1/2. The second is (3/4, 1/4), with energy
5/8. Their difference has squared norm 1/8 = 5/8 −1/2. The identity uses the affine nature of both
constraint sets. For the nested closed convex sets [1, ∞) and [2, ∞) in R, the minimum points instead
have squared difference one and energy difference three.
11. A basis is not automatically an isometry
In Euclidean R2, let p(a) = a1 −2a2, d(a) = a1, r = 1, and α = 1. Compute the protected regularized
correction using the basis (2, 1) of the kernel. What changes if the Gram matrix is omitted?
Solution. The coordinate c gives a = (2c, c), with S = 2 and G = 5. The normal equation is 9c = 2, so
a = (4/9, 2/9). Its objective is 5/9. Replacing G by one gives c = 2/5 and a = (4/5, 2/5), whose actual
ambient objective is 21/25 > 5/9. That replacement solves a different metric problem.
12. A robust negative certificate
A nominal scalar system has T = 1, r = 1. The operator error is at most 1/10 and the data error at most
1/20. Can a correction of norm at most 1/2 achieve true residual at most 3/10? What does the same test
say at budget two?
Solution. At budget 1/2, the nominal optimum is 1/2. Subtracting 1/20 + (1/10)(1/2) leaves 2/5 >
3/10, so the proposed accuracy is excluded for every admissible true model. At budget two, the lower
estimate is negative and clips to zero. It excludes nothing. The disappearance of this certificate is not
proof of exact feasibility.
13. A smaller coordinate residual without a better correction
For the protected example in Section 31, keep the same correction (1/4, 1/4) but replace the kernel basis
B = (1, 1)T by B′ = B/100. Compute the coordinate normal residual and its actual Hilbert norm.
Solution. In the original basis, c = 1/4, G = 2 and q = 3c −1 = −1/4. In the new basis, c′ = 25,
G′ = 1/5000 and q′ = −1/400. The coordinate residual is one hundred times smaller, but
(q′)T(G′)−1q′ =
5000
160000 = 1
32 = qTG−1q.
The physical correction and the certificate have not improved. The changed number records a changed
basis.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
136


Komorebi Space
Monograph
14. How small must the solver residual be?
For n ≥1, suppose αn = n−2, the measured-data error is bounded by n−2, and the exact-data regularized
corrections converge to the desired minimum-norm answer. Does a solver normal-residual tolerance n−2
guarantee convergence of the proposals? Give a sufficient replacement.
Solution. The noise term is at most 1/(2n), but the solver term is at most one. Thus the displayed
tolerance does not provide the required guarantee. The scalar counterexample T = 0, r = 0, an = 1
meets it and does not approach the exact optimizer. Replacing it by n−3 makes the solver term at most
1/n, so the triangle inequality and the assumed vanishing bias prove convergence. This is a sufficient
worst-case rule, not a necessary condition for every particular operator.
15. Exact optimization of measured data is not exact realization
Let d be the identity on R, protect no nonzero observable, take raw input f = 2, measured residual r = 1,
and α = 1. Find the exact measured-data optimizer and compare the fit residual with the actual repaired
residual.
Solution. The objective (a −1)2 + a2 is minimized at a = 1/2. Its normal residual is zero, so the
solver certificate is exact. The measured fit residual is r −da = 1/2. The actual repaired candidate is
f −a = 3/2, whose model residual is 3/2. The difference is d f −r = 1. A certificate about solving the
measured optimization problem does not remove measurement error or the regularization bias.
A checklist for applying the theory
Before selecting a theorem, state the raw and exact objects, the coefficient structure, the maps that relate
views, and the data that must remain fixed. Then ask which conclusion is needed: existence, a description
of all answers, an identification-preserving comparison, a stable distance estimate, or an executable finite
certificate. Finally record the hypotheses that make that conclusion available.
The examples above fail only when one of those distinctions is suppressed. The missing information is
not supplied by giving the same construction a more general name.

32.2
B.4 Three questions before using a finite check
First determine whether the tested object is the actual object in the theorem. A scalar simulation can
illustrate an operator-valued statement without checking every dependent fiber. Next determine whether
the test checks a universal finite domain or a selected finite range. Finally identify any limit that remains
outside the computation.
The phase extension is exhaustively checked on its eight elements. The diagonal example is different:
rational finite sums verify its displayed enclosures, while the proof of nonexistence uses the infinite
square-summability condition. Calling both results simply “tested” would conceal the most important
distinction.
The companion Lean guide records these scopes and identifies the checked examples. General source dec-
larations are checked in the accompanying formal project. Printed examples and conceptual connections
have only the formal coverage explicitly documented for them.

C.3 Using the correspondence
For a proof involving a restriction or a change of representation, compare the source and target spaces
before identifying two displayed formulas. For an analytic estimate, check the norm, the permitted
correction space, and the operator whose adjoint is used. The declarations preserve these distinctions
explicitly. A continuous linear equivalence alone is not an isometry, and a coordinate equation alone
does not fix the metric of its residual.
The source contains further definitions, lemmas, and examples beyond this table. The Lean guide
explains its organization and the formal treatment of the principal analytic results. Verification records
retain the exact source inventories and declaration types from the accepted compilation.

D Proof and formal verification
The printed arguments and the accompanying Lean development have complementary roles. The text
explains constructions and their mathematical context. A Lean declaration fixes a precise statement
whose proof is checked by the kernel. A correspondence between them records the actual hypotheses
and conclusion, while allowing the formal proof to use a different route.
All 16 principal theorem groups on bounded realization, budgets, regularization, and certificates have
complete formal counterparts. The table identifies these statements by their numbers in the main text.
This coverage concerns the stated results, rather than every proof route or illustrative calculation.

D.1 Bounded realization and budget geometry
The formal bounded-realization theorem concerns bounded linear maps between real Hilbert spaces.
It gives an exact preimage within the same norm bound as the approximating corrections. Its proof
uses compactness of a dual ball and the Riesz isometric equivalence. The printed proof uses the
weak-subsequence lemma, Lemma 27.1, whose particular proof route is not separately formalized here.
Neither route requires separability or closed range. Neither asserts strong convergence of the original
approximating sequence.
The nested-constraint theorem constructs the minimum-norm points of nonempty closed affine sets.
It proves the exact energy identity, norm monotonicity, and the equivalence between a nonempty
OPERATION KOMOREBI
145


Komorebi Space
Monograph
intersection and bounded minimum norms. Under that bound, the corrections converge strongly to
the unique minimum-norm point of the intersection. If the intersection is empty, their norms tend to
infinity. The affine assumption is retained: for general convex sets the corresponding energy statement is
an inequality.

All namespaces in this appendix have the prefix KomorebiSpace. The protected application uses the
actual subtype ker p with its inherited Hilbert norm. It constructs a repaired candidate preserving the
declared observable and the original correction budget.
The budget-profile theorems concern bounded operators between real complete Hilbert spaces. The
budget is nonnegative and may be zero. Attainment of a residual minimum over a bounded correction
ball does not require the entire operator range to be closed. The sharp support formula supplies an
actual maximizing witness, expressed formally by IsGreatest. It does not infer attainment from the
mere existence of a supremum.

D.2 Regularization, noise, and stopping
For positive parameter α, the regularized normal operator is constructed as a continuous linear equiva-
lence. Its inverse defines the correction. The formal development proves uniqueness, the energy identity,
parameter monotonicity, budget optimality, and the two data-sensitivity estimates. The fixed-space
protected application uses the restricted operator and its actual adjoint.
The limit theory distinguishes projection onto the closure of the range from membership in the range
itself. Fitted values converge to the projected datum and residual norms converge to the irreducible
floor. If that projected datum is attained, the corrections converge in norm to its unique minimum-norm
preimage. Otherwise their norms tend to infinity. The data and stopping theorems use this limit theorem
together with their explicit parameter, noise, and solver-tolerance assumptions.

estimate is derived rather than assumed.
D.3 Certificates and their coordinate metric
The solver certificate controls correction error, fitted error, and objective excess through the actual Hilbert
normal residual. The primal and dual identity supplies objective bracketing and a correction-distance
bound for an arbitrary dual witness. These are universal statements over the declared Hilbert spaces,
with α > 0 wherever coercivity is used.

The coordinate certificate uses a basis of the entire permitted correction space, with its inherited Hilbert
metric. The proof derives invertibility of the Gram matrix from that basis, identifies the coordinate
residual with the pairings of the Hilbert normal residual, and proves
g = BG−1q,
∥g∥2 = qTG−1q.
Every invertible basis change is allowed. The changed coordinates describe the same correction, the
residual transforms as a covector, and the displayed squared norm is invariant. The proof includes the
zero-dimensional correction space. It does not replace the Hilbert norm by a coordinate supremum norm
or assume that the basis is orthonormal. These metric identities hold for every real α. The optimizer
estimates still require positive α.
D.4 The infinite diagonal example
Proposition 30.1, Theorem 30.2, and Corollary 30.3 have formal counterparts in the namespace Komore
biSpace.DiagonalRegularization. The formal space is the real Hilbert space lp at exponent two. Its
natural index starts at zero, so formal coordinate j denotes printed coordinate n = j + 1. The operator,
data, corrections, residuals, and hard prefixes are elements or maps of that actual infinite space.

The first pair identifies the universal regularized correction, proves it is the unique global minimizer,

nonnegative omitted-tail bounds. The asymptotic theorem proves both scalar limits, the budget-profile
limit π/4 over all real M tending to infinity, and the hard-prefix comparison with constant one. Its
budget conclusion is not restricted to the regularization mesh.
The noisy theorem uses the actual attainable datum and its minimum-norm preimage. It retains the
literal ceiling in m = ⌈δ−2/3⌉and proves both displayed inequalities for every Hilbert perturbation
within the stated noise bound. The bias estimate is derived within the model. The inexact rate adds the
stated solver tolerance to this same model and parameter choice.
The range obstruction uses actual unit coordinate vectors with residual norm 1/(j + 1) and applies the
general unit-witness theorem. The hard-prefix images converge in Hilbert norm to w, placing it in the
closure of the range. An exact preimage would have every coordinate equal to one, contradicting square
summability. These declarations formalize the concrete example following Theorem 18.2. Exact finite
enclosures remain separate illustrations of the infinite results.
D.5 Verification record and trust boundary
The source contains 166 compilation targets (162 production modules and 4 entry points), 1,859 explicit
theorem or lemma declarations, and 5,339 exported declarations. The latter includes definitions and
generated helpers; neither count measures independent mathematical discoveries.
A fresh replay compiled every target with warnings treated as errors and automatic implicit parame-
ters disabled. It audited all project declarations by defining module and recursive axiom dependen-
cies, rejecting unsafe declarations and project-specific axioms. Source, compiler, installed-cache, and
generated-object identities bind the results to this run.
All 56 diagnostic controls had their specified outcomes: 18 successes, 35 intended elaboration rejections,
and three axiom-policy rejections. The suite reran the 52 accepted controls and added four for the range
obstruction and inexact rate. Earlier records remain as historical evidence.
The environment is Lean 4.34.0-rc2 with mathlib commit
aeb72f1e9981b6d35b426b16dca7759be3e7ba06.
Permitted axioms are propext, Classical.choice, and Quot.sound. Lean’s compiler and kernel and
the pinned external dependency cache remain trusted; the cache was recorded but not rebuilt. Finite

executable examples provide separate computational evidence; they are not verified extractions of the
Lean proofs.

E Analytic facts used in the proofs
This appendix supplies the analytic ingredients used at several points in the book. They are classical
facts, not additional novelty claims. Writing out their proofs makes the dependence on completeness,
convexity, the inner product, and the declared operator norm visible. The statements are restricted to the
real Hilbert and real Banach settings used in the main arguments. They provide background support for
the main results.
The projection and inverse constructions have standard formal counterparts in the mathematical library
cited in [4, 5]. The regularization context is also discussed in [6]. The arguments below are ordinary
written proofs. The accompanying Lean project does not by itself constitute a formal verification of these
printed proofs.
E.1 Projection onto a closed convex set
Lemma E.1 (Existence, uniqueness, and the variational inequality). Let H be a real Hilbert space, let C ⊆H
be nonempty, closed, and convex, and fix x ∈H. There is a unique c ∈C minimizing ∥x −c∥. It is characterized
by
⟨x −c, z −c⟩≤0
(z ∈C).
(E.1)
If C = c0 + L is a closed affine subspace with direction space L, the condition is x −c ⊥L.
Proof. Produce a Cauchy minimizing sequence. Put d = infz∈C ∥x −z∥. Nonemptiness makes this a
finite nonnegative real number. Choose cn ∈C so that ∥x −cn∥→d. Convexity places (cn + cm)/2 in C.
The parallelogram identity gives
∥cn −cm∥2 = 2 ∥x −cn∥2 + 2 ∥x −cm∥2 −4
x −cn + cm
2

2
≤2 ∥x −cn∥2 + 2 ∥x −cm∥2 −4d2.
The right side tends to zero as both indices tend to infinity. Thus (cn) is Cauchy. Completeness supplies
a limit c ∈H, closedness puts it in C, and continuity of the norm gives ∥x −c∥= d. A minimizing
sequence in an arbitrary closed set need not have been Cauchy. The midpoint was the essential use of
convexity.
Prove uniqueness. If c, c′ ∈C both attain d, the same midpoint inequality gives
c −c′2 ≤2d2 + 2d2 −4d2 = 0.
Therefore c = c′.
Derive the variational inequality. For z ∈C and 0 < t ≤1, convexity makes c + t(z −c) feasible.
Minimality and the inner-product expansion yield
0 ≤∥x −c −t(z −c)∥2 −∥x −c∥2 = −2t⟨x −c, z −c⟩+ t2 ∥z −c∥2 .
Divide by the positive number t and let t ↓0. This gives (E.1). Conversely, if that inequality holds, then
for every z ∈C,
∥x −z∥2 = ∥x −c∥2 −2⟨x −c, z −c⟩+ ∥z −c∥2 ≥∥x −c∥2 .
It therefore characterizes the unique minimizer.
For an affine subspace, c + h and c −h are feasible for every h ∈L. Applying the inequality with both
signs gives ⟨x −c, h⟩= 0. Orthogonality conversely implies the same variational inequality. In this case
the preceding expansion becomes the Pythagorean equality, not merely an inequality.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
151


Komorebi Space
Monograph
For a closed linear subspace L, denote its projection by PL. The lemma gives a unique decomposition
x = PLx + (x −PLx),
PLx ∈L,
x −PLx ∈L⊥.
Adding two such decompositions, or multiplying one by a scalar, proves linearity by uniqueness. The
Pythagorean identity gives ∥PLx∥≤∥x∥, so PL is bounded. It fixes L, annihilates L⊥, and satisfies
P2
L = PL. For x, y ∈H, orthogonality gives
⟨PLx, y⟩= ⟨PLx, PLy⟩= ⟨x, PLy⟩.
Thus PL is self-adjoint. These properties are conclusions of the construction, not extra data assumed for a
proposed projection.
E.2 Riesz representation and the adjoint
Lemma E.2 (Representing a bounded functional). For every bounded real linear functional ℓ: H →R on a
real Hilbert space, there is a unique u ∈H with
ℓ(h) = ⟨h, u⟩
(h ∈H),
∥ℓ∥= ∥u∥.
Proof. If ℓ= 0, take u = 0. Otherwise choose x with ℓ(x) ̸= 0. Boundedness makes L = ker ℓclosed. Use
Lemma E.1 to form v = x −PLx. Then v ⊥L and ℓ(v) = ℓ(x) ̸= 0, in particular v ̸= 0.
For any h ∈H, the vector h −ℓ(h)v/ℓ(v) belongs to L. Pairing it with v therefore gives
⟨h, v⟩= ℓ(h)
ℓ(v) ∥v∥2 .
Consequently u = ℓ(v)v/ ∥v∥2 represents ℓ. If u′ also represents it, pairing the difference of the two
formulas with h = u −u′ gives ∥u −u′∥2 = 0, hence uniqueness.
Cauchy–Schwarz gives |ℓ(h)| ≤∥u∥∥h∥, so ∥ℓ∥≤∥u∥. If u ̸= 0, the unit vector h = u/ ∥u∥gives the
reverse inequality. The zero case was already included.
Let T : H →R be a bounded linear map between real Hilbert spaces. For fixed z ∈R, the functional
h 7→⟨Th, z⟩is bounded by ∥T∥∥z∥∥h∥. The lemma therefore defines a unique vector T∗z ∈H with
⟨Th, z⟩= ⟨h, T∗z⟩.
Uniqueness of representatives proves that T∗is linear. The same bound proves ∥T∗z∥≤∥T∥∥z∥.
Conversely,
∥Th∥= sup
∥z∥≤1
|⟨Th, z⟩| ≤∥T∗∥∥h∥,
where the first equality follows by testing z = Th/ ∥Th∥when Th ̸= 0. Hence ∥T∗∥= ∥T∥.
The defining pairing also gives the identity
(ran T)⊥= ker T∗.
(E.2)
Indeed, z is orthogonal to every Th exactly when ⟨h, T∗z⟩= 0 for every h, which is equivalent to T∗z = 0.
Orthogonal complements do not change on taking closure. The projection decomposition then gives
ran T = (ker T∗)⊥.
This is why a self-adjoint injective operator has dense range. It does not yet say its range is closed. In the
regularized normal-operator proof, the coercive lower bound supplies that separate fact.
OPERATION KOMOREBI
152


Komorebi Space
Monograph
E.3 Why a bounded bijection has a bounded inverse
The closed-range arguments use the inverse theorem only after proving that the relevant source and
target subspaces are complete and that the restricted map is bijective. The following derivation records
this analytic step explicitly.
Lemma E.3 (Baire’s complete-space principle). A nonempty complete metric space cannot be the union of
countably many closed sets with empty interior.
Proof. Suppose F1, F2, . . . are closed with empty interior. Choose a nonempty open ball whose closed ball
B1, of positive radius at most 1/2, avoids F1. Such a choice is possible because the complement of F1 is
open and meets every nonempty open set. Recursively choose a closed ball Bn+1 of positive radius at
most 2−(n+1), contained in the open ball with the same center and radius as Bn, and disjoint from Fn+1.
The intersection of that open ball with the complement of Fn+1 is nonempty and open, so it contains a
sufficiently small closed ball. This also covers the case of isolated points.
The centers form a Cauchy sequence: all centers after stage n lie in Bn, whose diameter is at most 21−n.
Completeness gives a limit. For each fixed n, a tail of centers lies in the closed set Bn, so the limit lies
there. It therefore belongs to no Fn, contradicting that these sets cover the space.
Lemma E.4 (Continuous inverse for Banach spaces). Let S : X →Y be a bounded linear surjection between
real Banach spaces. There is a constant C < ∞such that every y ∈Y has a preimage x with ∥x∥≤C ∥y∥. If S is
also injective, its inverse is bounded linear.
Proof. If Y = {0}, take the zero preimage. Otherwise write BX for the closed unit ball. Surjectivity gives
Y =

S(nBX).
By Lemma E.3, at least one of the closed sets on the right has nonempty interior. Thus there are n ≥1,
y0 ∈Y, and ρ > 0 such that
B(y0, ρ) ⊆S(nBX).
Here B(y0, ρ) is an open ball. If ∥h∥< ρ, both y0 + h and y0 belong to this closure. Choose approximating
image sequences for both. Subtracting their preimages shows h ∈S(2nBX). Scaling by 2n gives a
constant c = ρ/(2n) > 0 with
B(0, c) ⊆S(BX).

budget.

If S is bijective, the preimage is unique. Uniqueness makes the inverse additive and homogeneous, and
the bound just obtained makes it continuous. In the merely surjective case the construction does not
assert a linear choice of preimage. In particular it does not assert that every Banach-space surjection has
a bounded linear right inverse.
For example, in Theorem 18.2 the map to be inverted has source (ker D)⊥and target ran D. Both are
closed subspaces of the stated Banach spaces. The inverse lemma applies only after the proof has
established those closedness and bijectivity facts. It would not apply with an unclosed range equipped
with its inherited norm.
E.4 Neumann inversion with both inverse identities
Lemma E.5 (A strict perturbation radius). Let W be a real Banach space and T : W →W bounded linear, with

Proof. First recall why the space of bounded operators on W is complete. An operator-norm Cauchy

linearity. The sequence of operator norms is bounded, so the pointwise limit is a bounded operator. For a

produced each pointwise limit.

E.5 The dependency order
The analytic arguments can now be read without a circular invocation of their conclusions. Projection
gives the orthogonal decomposition and Riesz representation. The latter gives adjoints and the relation
between range and adjoint kernel. Baire’s principle independently gives the Banach inverse theorem.
Those ingredients yield closed-range estimates and the positive regularized normal inverse. Neumann’s
series gives the separate small-perturbation inverse and its explicit radius.
The weak-subsequence proof in Section 27 uses projection onto the closed separable span generated by
the sequence, followed by a coordinate diagonal argument. It does not assume that the whole ambient
Hilbert space is separable. The budget-attainment and regularization-limit proofs use that sequential
result. Strong convergence is proved later from the minimum-norm condition and the norm identities,
not inferred from weak convergence alone.

F Background and references
The references identify standard sources for limits, descent, Hilbert projection, bounded inverse theo-
rems, and regularization. Appendix E gives the analytic arguments used repeatedly in the text. The
accompanying Lean source and its pinned mathematical-library revision determine the formal interfaces
used in this edition. Online library documentation is a navigation aid and may describe a different
revision. The consultation dates below are retained from the recorded source checks.
